================================================================================
## AI chat, meal logging, onboarding, settings  [ai-and-input]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (69)
- The AI Chat tab must be present only when a server base URL is configured; when the app is in backend-optional local mode the tab must be absent and any deep link, restored navigation state, or widget tap targeting chat must return the user to Home without showing a blank screen.
- On opening AI Chat the app must probe the server's AI provider exactly once and land in one of three terminal states: Ready, "No AI Provider Configured" (reached only from an HTTP 404, with the guidance to configure a provider in the web app and no action button), or "Unable to Connect" (reached from any other status or transport failure, with a Retry control). The app must not poll, retry automatically, or re-probe after a successful send; a spinner must never be a terminal state.
- A user must be able to send a chat message of up to 2000 characters inclusive; a longer message must be refused locally with "Message is too long (max 2000 characters)" and no network request. The send control and the input field must be disabled while a request is in flight so a double-send is impossible.
- The chat transcript must live only in memory, capped at the most recent 100 messages, and must be lost on process death — no chat content is persisted to disk. A Clear control must appear only when the transcript is non-empty and must clear the transcript, clear any error, and stop any speech in progress.
- Chat failures must always present curated copy and never a raw error message: request timeout, no connectivity, session expired, server error, and an unclassified fallback each have their own fixed wording, rendered as a dismissible inline row at the bottom of the message list rather than a modal.
- The chat screen must show four tappable starter suggestions when the transcript is empty; tapping one must only populate the input field, never send. A permanent footer above the input must read "AI responses are informational only. Always consult your healthcare provider." and each assistant reply must render the server-supplied disclaimer beneath it.
- Assistant markdown must be sanitized before rendering: all image markdown and HTML image tags are stripped so AI-generated content can never trigger an outbound network fetch, and only http and https links may be opened — every other scheme is silently ignored.
- A user must be able to turn spoken responses on and off from the chat screen, and while on, choose from the device's available speech voices for their language. Selecting a voice must persist it, survive relaunch, fall back to the system voice if the saved voice is no longer installed, and sync to the watch. Spoken output must always be the assistant's reply with ". This is not medical advice." appended, with markdown removed for natural reading; blank stripped text must not be spoken.
- Speech must duck other audio rather than interrupt it, and must stop when the user turns speech off, clears the chat, or leaves the screen.
- If a chat request is interrupted because the app left the foreground, the app must say so honestly on return and invite the user to resend, rather than leaving a stalled request indicator.
- A user wearing the watch must be able to ask the AI a question from the watch and receive an answer on the watch, whether or not the phone app is open. Watch questions are limited to 500 characters, and the watch shows one question and one answer at a time with no transcript.
- When no server is configured, a watch chat attempt must return the terminal message "AI chat needs a GlycemicGPT server — none is set up on your phone." and must not offer a retry, because retrying cannot succeed.
- The watch must offer system dictation, scribble, and keyboard input for a question, plus three one-tap quick questions, and must speak the answer aloud when spoken responses are enabled — always with ". This is not medical advice." appended.
- A user must be able to place a chat complication on their watch face and a chat widget in the Smart Stack; tapping either must open the watch chat screen, optionally pre-filled.
- The watch must show a specific, honest failure for each condition — phone not connected, request timed out, no server configured, session expired — never a generic "something went wrong" when a more accurate cause is known. The watch's own timeout must not be shorter than the request timeout it is waiting on.
- The app must not include an AI daily-brief screen. AI insights and briefs arrive only as server-pushed notifications, delivered in their own category at normal (non-critical, non-time-sensitive) priority, with their own configurable sound including an explicit Silent option.
- Meal logging must be reachable only when a server is configured and meal intelligence is enabled; when the local setting is off the meal screen must show its disabled state immediately with no network request, and the server probe remains the authority. If the server reports the feature is off during an upload, the screen must move to its disabled state.
- A user must be able to log a meal by taking a photo or choosing one from their library. Choosing from the library must not require a photo-library permission, and the chosen image must not carry location data. If camera access is denied the app must say so inline in error styling, keep the library path available, and offer a link to Settings, because iOS will not re-prompt.
- Photos must be downscaled to a longest edge of 1280 pixels, oriented upright, and re-encoded as JPEG under 5 MiB by stepping quality down from 90 to 40 in steps of 10, failing with "Image is too large to upload after compression" if it still exceeds the cap. The uploaded bytes must contain no EXIF, GPS, or other metadata, and this must be verified by an automated test.
- Temporary capture files must be deleted after upload, on cancellation, and when a new capture supersedes an old one; a full sweep runs only when the meal screen resets. A capture that is currently being read must never be deleted by another capture's cleanup.
- The "Estimating carbs…" indicator must be impossible to leave stuck: every failure path clears it, image decoding must avoid loading a full-resolution bitmap so the app is not terminated for memory, oversized source images must be refused up front with "That photo is too large to process. Try a smaller one.", and if the app is terminated mid-upload the next launch must show an honest failure rather than resume into a spinner.
- Every surface that displays a carb estimate must display, at the same time, "Rough estimate — an AI guess that's often wrong. Never use it to calculate an insulin dose or bolus." This applies to the meal result (pinned above the scroll region so it cannot scroll away), the meal history list, the common foods list, the re-log sheet, the edit sheet, and the Home recent-meal card. The qualifier must use a calm amber treatment, never the error/alarm color.
- Carbs must always be presented as a low-to-high range with a confidence signal, never as a bare number; a single-point estimate still renders with units. Nothing in the meal feature may present insulin, units, or a bolus.
- VoiceOver must announce the carb value, the confidence, the never-dose qualifier, and any uncertainty note as a single element, so the number can never be heard without the qualifier.
- Confidence must be conveyed by bar length as well as color, and the medium and low levels must remain distinguishable without color perception.
- When the estimate came from multiple reads that disagreed, or the AI was unsure what the food was, the app must show the server's uncertainty note in the caution treatment; when the reads agreed it must still show the note quietly, never implying the estimate is safe to dose from. Uncertainty information is available only on a newly created record and must never be fabricated for a history row.
- A user must be able to confirm or correct what the food is, as an action separate from correcting the carbs, and the app must explain that confirming lets the estimate be grounded against real nutrition data. When the AI was unsure of the identity, the prompt must say so in error styling. A confirmed record must show that it was confirmed and allow the user to change it.
- A user must be able to correct the carb range. Values must be rejected — never silently clamped — when either is not a number, is negative, exceeds 1000 grams, or when the low exceeds the high, each with its own message, and no invalid value may reach the server. The same validation and copy must be shared with the common-food editor. A corrected record must still show the original AI estimate alongside the correction.
- A user must be able to ask "How was this estimated?" and see how many times the photo was read, what each read returned, and whether the result was grounded against an external source or is vision-only. This must load on demand only, and must be discarded when the food identity changes, because grounding and precedence have changed.
- When the server returns nutrition information the app must render it verbatim in the server's own words — the assumed portion with its prompt to check portion size, macros in whole units with their glucose notes, net carbs behind their caveat and clearly secondary to the total range, and the section disclaimer. Any non-finite or malformed number must be dropped rather than displayed, and a block with nothing left to show must not render at all.
- Heart and blood-pressure nutrition information must appear only for a confirmed, grounded record, attributed to its source and trust tier, and must be framed as awareness rather than a directive. A sugar caveat must not appear if the sugar value it refers to was dropped.
- A user must be able to save a meal's estimate as a named common food, see it confirmed by the name the server returned, and be told when that name is already taken.
- When the server is unreachable, meal history and common foods must show a clear "can't reach your server" state with a Retry control — never an empty-list message that would imply the user has logged nothing. When a list is already loaded and a refresh fails, the failure must appear as a small inline notice above the existing list rather than replacing it.
- A user must be able to delete a meal record or a common food with immediate effect and no confirmation, with any failure surfaced as a transient message; pump unpairing and driver deactivation must keep their confirmations.
- A user must be able to re-log a saved common food by viewing its saved carb range in a read-only sheet that carries the never-dose qualifier — this must not create a new record, matching the current server capability.
- Home must show the most recent meal with its carb range, confidence, timestamp, and the never-dose qualifier, and must offer a single obvious entry point to log a meal. The meal entry point must be absent when no server is configured or meal intelligence is off, and must remain available when a probe fails for transient reasons so the meal screen can present its own degraded state.
- First-run onboarding must present welcome, features, a safety disclaimer, optional server setup, and sign-in, and the safety disclaimer must be impossible to skip or swipe past — only an explicit "I Understand" advances it. The disclaimer must include a card stating that photo carb estimates are AI guesses that are frequently wrong, can misidentify the food entirely, and must never be used to calculate an insulin dose or bolus.
- A user must be able to finish onboarding without a server and use the app as a direct pump monitor; taking that path must cancel any in-flight connection test before clearing the saved server URL, and must clear any server-supplied alert thresholds so a departed server's values are never left armed.
- Both onboarding completion paths — after sign-in and after choosing local-only mode — must request notification permission before navigating away, and that single request must include the critical-alert option, because iOS grants the prompt only once and the on-device alert floor is silently suppressed without permission.
- A user must be able to test their server connection before signing in, see a clear success or failure result, and have the typed URL restored to the previous working value if the test fails. Repeated taps within one second must be ignored.
- If iOS blocks access to a server on the local network, the app must say exactly that and link the user to the Local Network setting, rather than reporting a generic connection failure. This must be distinguishable in the UI from a wrong URL, a down server, and an insecure-scheme rejection.
- A user must be able to opt in to reaching a self-hosted server over unencrypted http on a private or LAN address, only after acknowledging a dialog that states the traffic is unencrypted and readable by anyone on the network, and only for private/LAN addresses — public addresses must always require https. The opt-in must be available inline during onboarding, because a new user cannot reach Settings before signing in.
- While insecure LAN http is actually in use, a persistent banner must appear above every screen including onboarding, and it must appear only when cleartext would genuinely be sent — never for a public http URL the app refuses to contact.
- Host classification for the LAN exception must never perform a DNS lookup, must accept only strictly-formatted IP literals (rejecting octal, hexadecimal, bare-decimal, and leading-zero forms) plus mDNS names ending in .local with at least one label, and must classify IPv4-mapped IPv6 addresses by their embedded IPv4.
- Sign-in credentials, the access token, its expiry, and the refresh token must be stored in the system keychain and must remain readable while the device is locked after the first unlock, so overnight token refresh and background alert handling cannot fail silently. The password must be cleared from app state on both success and failure.
- The app must choose its start destination from whether onboarding was completed, independent of whether the session is still valid, so an expired session can never re-onboard a user or lock locally-cached pump data behind an impossible sign-in.
- Settings must present Account, Network, Drivers, Sync, Meal Intelligence, Notifications, Watch, Appearance, About, and (debug builds only) Developer, in that order, and must refresh its state every time the screen is shown.
- In backend-optional local mode, Settings must hide the backend sync control, the meal intelligence section, and the AI notification sound row while keeping data retention, low and high alert sounds, and everything else — and must switch the alert-threshold editor from read-only to editable.
- Alert thresholds must be read-only whenever a server is configured, showing the four current values, the unit, and a note that they are managed on the server, or "Waiting for the first sync from your server." when none has arrived. Saving a server URL must make the editor read-only in the same interaction, and any save attempt must re-check the live backend state before writing.
- In local-only mode a user must be able to set all four thresholds in their chosen unit. All four must land within 20–500 mg/dL and satisfy urgent low ≤ low < high ≤ urgent high, values are stored canonically in mg/dL, and out-of-range or out-of-order input is rejected with a specific message — never clamped. The mmol/L bounds quoted in the error message must themselves be values that round back into the accepted mg/dL range.
- On-device alarms must not fire from default thresholds. Until thresholds arrive from a server or are saved locally, Settings must state in error styling that on-device alarms are off until all four are set.
- Alert thresholds must be written durably before the save is reported as complete, and must remain readable by the alerting path while the device is locked.
- Signing out must clear server-supplied alert thresholds so the alarm floor cannot fire from a departed account's values, while preserving thresholds the user set on this device.
- A user must be able to choose a sound for low alerts, high alerts, and AI notifications from a set of sounds included in the app, preview each one, and choose Silent for AI notifications. Low-alert sounds must be distinctly alarm-like.
- Low and high glucose alerts must attempt to break through Focus and Do Not Disturb, and low alerts must be able to sound at full volume even when the device is silenced, using critical alerts where the entitlement is available. Low alerts must re-alert on each update; high and AI alerts alert once. If critical alerts are unavailable, the app must say plainly that the phone's silent switch and volume can silence the alarm rather than implying protection it does not have.
- The notification status card must report every condition that would prevent an alert reaching the user — permission denied, sound off, critical alerts not permitted, Time Sensitive not permitted, Scheduled Summary enabled — each with a link to the relevant setting, and must re-check every time the app returns to the foreground.
- Settings must show whether the watch is paired, whether the watch app is installed, and whether it is currently reachable, plus the last glucose and insulin-on-board values sent to the watch with their age, labelled explicitly as canonical wire values in mg/dL. Values outside 20–500 mg/dL must be shown as unavailable.
- A user must be able to configure what the watch app and its complications show — insulin on board, the glucose graph and its 1/3/6-hour range, alert vibration, theme, and the graph overlays — with changes taking effect on the watch without further action.
- Because watchOS does not support third-party watch faces, the app must deliver glanceable data through watch complications the user places on an Apple face and through Smart Stack widgets, and must explain in Settings how to add them. Settings must not offer a watch-face gallery or a face push.
- Settings must show the installed phone and watch app versions, flag a mismatch, and direct the user to TestFlight to update; the app must not attempt to download or install any build itself.
- A user must be able to choose System, Dark, or Light appearance, per device, and the choice must apply immediately across the app, including to the calm-caution treatment used by safety notes.
- A user must be able to display glucose in mg/dL or mmol/L. All storage, transport, thresholds, alert evaluation, watch payloads, and the 20–500 safety range remain mg/dL; the choice affects display and speech only. Conversion uses a single constant of 18.0156, converts once from the most precise source, and rounds last; a spread or standard deviation converts without the offset.
- When the app has picked a unit automatically from region or a data source, it must say so once above the selector with a dismissible note, and must never block the UI with it.
- A user must be able to enable or disable meal intelligence for their account, with the local effect immediate and the account sync attempted afterwards. When the sync fails the app must say "Saved on this device. Couldn't sync to your account." and must not imply it will retry. Rapid toggling must never let a stale response overwrite a newer choice, and a rejected account must fail closed to disabled.
- A user must be able to set local data retention between 1 and 30 days, defaulting to 7, and that value must bound the analysis windows offered elsewhere.
- A user must be able to open the app's open-source license documents with no connectivity, select and copy text across paragraph boundaries, and return to their previous scroll position after a rotation or size change. The screen must state nothing of its own about licensing — every word comes from a document generated from the repository at build time, and a build whose license documents are missing or empty must fail rather than ship.
- Debug builds only must expose a developer section with a driver debug console, a pump-label toggle, a simulate-backend-unreachable fault, and a compressed-staleness fault; these must be compiled out of release builds entirely, and the fault toggles must additionally refuse to act in a release build.
- The debug console must let a developer inject a normal glucose reading, a fresh low reading, and a stale low reading through the same code path a real reading takes, so the alert floor's fire and suppress behaviors and the honest "NOT watching" surface can be validated without pump hardware — which is the only way to exercise them in the Simulator.
- When the session has expired, or when a server is configured but the user is not signed in, a tappable banner must appear that takes the user to Settings; a user in local-only mode must never see it, because being signed out is that mode's normal state.

### IMPOSSIBLE (9)
- Runtime/custom plugin installation and removal — the "Custom Plugins" card, Add Plugin from a user-supplied .jar, per-plugin Remove that deletes its settings, and the untrusted-source warning :: iOS forbids downloading and executing code that is not part of the signed app bundle. App Review Guideline 2.5.2 prohibits apps that download, install, or execute code which introduces or changes features or functionality, and there is no supported dynamic-loading mechanism for third-party binaries on iOS. This is already settled by the project's decision that drivers are compile-time-linked Swift modules; the Settings surface has to reflect that. :: ALT: A built-in driver registry: every supported pump driver ships inside the app, is listed with its name and version, and can be enabled or disabled (keeping the deactivation confirmation dialog, since deactivating a driver can stop glucose monitoring). Community drivers are contributed as pull requests and arrive in the next TestFlight build. If a 'this driver is community-maintained and not verified' provenance signal is wanted, ship it as per-driver metadata on the card rather than an install-time warning.
- In-app self-update — checking GitHub Releases, downloading an app build, verifying its size, and launching an installer :: There is no way for an iOS app to install another iOS app or replace itself. Guideline 2.5.2 forbids downloading executable code, and there is no installer intent, no FileProvider-style install handoff, and no user-approved sideload path from inside an app on stock iOS. :: ALT: TestFlight is the update channel: it notifies testers, and builds install through the TestFlight app. The app may still call the GitHub Releases API to detect that a newer build exists and show the version, size, and release notes, but the only action available is a button that opens TestFlight or the App Store. Keep the https-only and host-allowlist rules for the metadata request even though nothing is downloaded.
- Pushing a watch app build to the paired watch — the Watch App Update card's download → push → install state machine, its 100 MB download cap, and its install timer :: watchOS apps are distributed only as part of the paired iPhone app's bundle, installed by the system through the App Store or TestFlight. No API installs, sideloads, or transfers an app to the watch, and the same code-download prohibition applies. :: ALT: Report the installed watch app version (obtained over WatchConnectivity) alongside the phone version, flag a mismatch as a diagnostic, and instruct the user to update the iPhone app from TestFlight — the watch app follows automatically once the Watch app's 'Automatic App Install' is on, or manually from the Watch app's install list.
- Custom watch faces — the watch-face gallery (Digital Full / Analog Mechanical variants), pushing a face to the watch, and face-level configuration such as showing seconds :: watchOS does not permit third-party watch faces. No API renders a face, and no app can install a face of its own design. This is the single largest genuine feature loss in the port and should be stated plainly in the PRD rather than described as a redesign. :: ALT: Three partial substitutes, none of which is a face: (1) WidgetKit accessory complications (accessoryCircular, accessoryCorner, accessoryInline, and accessoryRectangular, which can show a small glucose graph) that the user places on any Apple face — this recovers glanceability and the chat/BG tap targets, including the Android chat complication, via `widgetURL` deep links; (2) Smart Stack widgets on watchOS 10+, which surface BG/IoB with relevance-based presentation without any face configuration; (3) optionally, bundling `.watchface` sharing files that pre-configure an Apple face (e.g. Modular Ultra or Infograph) with the app's complications and an accent color, which the user installs with one tap — this gives a curated 'Digital' and 'Analog' starting point built from Apple's face types, but the rendering, layout, and overlays are Apple's, not the app's. The graph overlays (basal area, bolus markers, IoB overlay, activity-mode bands) survive only inside the watch app's own screens and the accessoryRectangular widget.
- Choosing an arbitrary system ringtone or notification sound for an alert category (RingtoneManager picker, arbitrary content URIs, persistable read URI permission, per-URI title resolution) :: iOS exposes no API to enumerate, preview, or select the system ringtone/alert-sound library, and notification sounds can only reference files inside the app bundle or the app container's Library/Sounds directory (Linear PCM, MA4, µLaw, or aLaw packaged as .aiff/.wav/.caf, 30 seconds maximum). There is no cross-app URI permission model, so the persistable-read-permission behavior has no counterpart. :: ALT: Ship a curated set of sounds inside the app — a deliberately aggressive alarm set for low alerts, a notification set for high alerts and AI — plus an explicit Silent option for AI, with in-app preview playback so the picker still feels like a choice. Optionally allow the user to import an audio file into the app container, which is the only way to support a user-supplied sound.
- The notification channel model — per-category sound, importance, vibration pattern, and DND-bypass settings that the user can independently tune in system settings, plus channel deletion/recreation to change a sound :: iOS has no notification channels. The system offers only app-level notification settings (plus per-app Focus and Scheduled Summary allowances); there is no OS surface where a user tunes 'Low Glucose Alerts' separately from 'AI Notifications'. Notification categories and thread identifiers group and act on notifications but are not user-configurable. :: ALT: Move category granularity into the app: in-app per-category preferences (sound, whether the category is delivered at all) that the app honors when it builds each `UNNotificationRequest`. The upside is that the entire versioned-channel recreation mechanism, its lock, and its non-atomic increment hazard vanish, because on iOS sound and interruption level are per-request properties. The downside is that a user who mutes the app in iOS Settings mutes everything at once, which is exactly why the notification status card must report each suppressing condition.
- Raising the device's system alarm/ringer volume so a low-glucose alarm is loud regardless of how the phone is set ("Boost Volume for Lows" as implemented) :: No iOS API sets system output volume, and none overrides the silent switch for ordinary notifications. `MPVolumeView` manipulation of system volume is unsupported for this purpose and would be rejected. :: ALT: Critical alerts: with the `com.apple.developer.usernotifications.critical-alerts` entitlement (Apple grants it only on request, typically to health and safety apps) and the user's `.criticalAlert` authorization, a notification with `.critical` interruption level and `UNNotificationSound.criticalSoundNamed(_:withAudioVolume:)` plays at an app-specified volume up to 1.0 and ignores both the silent switch and Focus. That delivers the user-facing outcome — a low alarm that is loud and unmissable — but it is an app-specified playback volume, not a change to the device's volume, and it is contingent on Apple approving the entitlement. Without the entitlement, the honest fallback is `.timeSensitive` (Focus bypass only, when the user permits) with copy that says plainly that the silent switch and volume can silence the alarm.
- An App Transport Security exception scoped to private/LAN IP ranges — allowing cleartext http:// to 10.x, 172.16–31.x, 192.168.x and CGNAT while keeping ATS enforced for every public host :: ATS exceptions are declared per-domain in Info.plist and do not accept IP addresses or CIDR ranges. `NSAllowsLocalNetworking` covers only unqualified single-label hostnames, `.local` names, and the link-local ranges (169.254.0.0/16, fe80::/10) — it does not cover RFC1918 addresses, which is the common self-hosting case. Worse, on modern iOS the presence of `NSAllowsLocalNetworking` causes `NSAllowsArbitraryLoads` to be ignored, so the two cannot be combined to get 'LAN cleartext plus ATS everywhere else'. :: ALT: Set `NSAllowsArbitraryLoads = YES` with no `NSAllowsLocalNetworking` key and keep `UrlSecurityPolicy` as the in-app enforcement point — the exact architecture Android already uses, where the platform permits cleartext globally and the app is the sole guard preventing a public host from being reached over plaintext. This preserves the security property but moves it entirely into app code, requires an App Review justification for the blanket ATS exception, and means a policy bug is now the only thing between a user and cleartext to a public host. The alternative is to require https on iOS and point self-hosters at a reverse proxy, a trusted self-signed certificate, or a mesh VPN.
- A guaranteed long-running background execution window on demand (Android's foreground service holding the process for a 90-second LLM call, with an ongoing user-visible notification) :: iOS has no foreground service. `beginBackgroundTask` grants roughly 30 seconds, is not guaranteed, and cannot be renewed; `BGTaskScheduler` runs opportunistically at times the system chooses and is unsuitable for on-demand work; and there is no mechanism to display an ongoing 'working…' notification tied to background execution. :: ALT: A background `URLSessionConfiguration` upload/download task, which the system runs out of process and delivers to the app via `handleEventsForBackgroundURLSession` even if the app was suspended or terminated — this achieves the outcome (the request completes and the answer arrives) but asynchronously, so any state the answer needs to land in must be persisted rather than held in memory. For the watch relay specifically, the better answer is to remove the need entirely by having the independent watchOS app call the backend directly, since watchOS routes its own `URLSession` over LTE, Wi-Fi, or the paired phone.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (15)
- AI Chat text-to-speech output and voice picker :: `AVSpeechSynthesizer` provides speech, but the voice metadata model is different enough that the Android voice-picker logic cannot be transliterated. iOS voice identifiers look like `com.apple.voice.compact.en-US.Samantha`, so the Android display-name derivation (text after `x-`, then after the last `-`, then last 3 chars) produces garbage. There is no `isNetworkConnectionRequired` flag, so the "Online" suffix has no source; quality is an enum (`.default`/`.enhanced`/`.premium`) not an Int, so the `quality >= 400` → "HD" rule needs remapping. Siri voices are not exposed to third-party apps, and Personal Voice requires a separate `AVSpeechSynthesizer.requestPersonalVoiceAuthorization` consent. Additionally, iOS requires explicit `AVAudioSession` configuration (category, and a ducking/mixing policy) that Android's TTS engine does not, and the persisted `ai_tts_voice` values from Android will never resolve on iOS. :: REC: Take the first option. Keep the exact toggle interaction (tap while off enables immediately; tap while on opens a menu whose first item is "Disable TTS", then a divider, then voices with a checkmark on the selected one), keep the persisted keys `ai_tts_enabled` (default false) / `ai_tts_voice` (default empty = system), keep the fallback to the system voice when a persisted identifier is absent, and keep the inseparable safety behavior: the spoken string is always `response + ". This is not medical advice."` run through the markdown stripper, skipped when blank, spoken with the queue flushed. Configure `AVAudioSession` with `.playback`, `.duckOthers`, and `.spokenAudio` mode, and deactivate with `.notifyOthersOnDeactivation` when speech ends. Do not add Personal Voice for v1. Voice/TTS settings remain a single shared pair that the phone pushes to the watch, as on Android.
- AI chat network transport — the 90s long-inference request surviving app suspension :: The two-client timeout split itself is trivial on iOS (two `URLSessionConfiguration`s: 15s `timeoutIntervalForRequest`/`timeoutIntervalForResource` vs 90s, with `httpMaximumConnectionsPerHost = 2` mirroring `ConnectionPool(2, 90s)`, and the chat session excluded from the reachability/network-health monitor for the same documented reason). The genuine problem is lifecycle: on Android the ViewModel and process typically survive a backgrounded 90-second LLM call. On iOS the app is suspended within seconds of backgrounding, and in-flight tasks on a default `URLSession` fail. `beginBackgroundTask` buys roughly 30 seconds, not 90. So the Android behavior — send a question, switch apps, come back to the answer — does not survive a straight port. :: REC: Ship option 1 for the phone chat screen: preserve the in-memory-only transcript exactly as Android has it, cancel on background, and add explicit honest copy on return. It is the only option that does not introduce chat persistence, and the chat screen is a foreground, attended experience. Use a background `URLSession` only for the watch-relay path (see the watch relay entry), where the user is not looking at the phone by definition. Keep the 15s/90s split and keep the debug simulate-unreachable fault applied to the chat session so the fault toggle still exercises chat.
- Watch AI chat relay (phone side) — replacing WearChatRelayService :: Every mechanism this service depends on is Android-only. There are no foreground services on iOS, so there is no way to hold the process up for a 90-second call on demand and no ongoing "Processing watch request" notification. `WCSession.sendMessage` from the watch will wake a suspended iOS counterpart app in the background, but the woken app gets a short, unguaranteed execution window — nowhere near 90 seconds — and `replyHandler` has its own timeout. Blocking a thread deliberately (Android's `runBlocking` on the binder thread) has no safe analogue. Separately, the relay exists on Android partly because Wear OS network access is unreliable; on watchOS the app is independent since watchOS 6 and its `URLSession` works over LTE, Wi-Fi, or transparently through the paired iPhone, so most of the relay's reason for existing disappears. :: REC: Do option 1 as the primary path and option 2 as the fallback when the watch reports no independent connectivity. Preserve every piece of the relay's behavior contract regardless of transport: reject empty input; reject >500 characters with "Message too long (max 500 chars)"; and when no server is configured return the terminal copy "AI chat needs a GlycemicGPT server — none is set up on your phone." verbatim, checked before any work begins and deliberately never saying "try again". Port `sanitizeErrorMessage` unchanged (timeout / no internet / session expired / server error / generic). Drop the foreground-service notification and the reference counting — they have no iOS meaning. Fix the existing 30s-watch-vs-90s-phone mismatch while you are here by aligning the watch's watchdog with the actual request timeout. The alert-dismiss path ports as a `WCSession` message that acknowledges locally first (so a cleared alert on the watch is truthful when the backend is unreachable) and carries the alert id, closing the documented Android bug where an empty payload can acknowledge the wrong alert.
- Watch AI chat UI and the watch-face chat launcher :: The screen's logic ports, but two mechanisms do not. Voice input: watchOS has no `RecognizerIntent`; text entry is the system input controller (dictation, Scribble, and the keyboard on Series 7+), presented via SwiftUI `TextFieldLink` or `presentTextInputController`, which runs out of process — so there is no "recognizer missing" case and no microphone/speech usage description needed from the app. Launcher: watchOS has no third-party watch faces, so the Android `ChatComplicationDataSource` becomes a WidgetKit accessory widget (`accessoryCircular`/`accessoryCorner`) that the user places on an Apple face, plus a Smart Stack widget, both deep-linking into the chat view via `widgetURL`. :: REC: Use the system input controller. Keep the whole `Idle | Loading | Success | Error` machine, the optional prefill from the widget deep link truncated to 500 characters, the three quick-query buttons ("How am I doing?", "Breakfast advice", "Why is my BG high?"), the blue accent on the ask button, the loading watchdog, the one-question-one-answer no-transcript model, the simpler on-watch markdown stripper, the disclaimer in small caption text defaulting to "Not medical advice. Consult your doctor." when blank, and the TTS speaking `response + ". This is not medical advice."` guarded against re-speaking the same response. Clearing chat state on leaving while loading ports directly to `.onDisappear`.
- Meal photo upload lifecycle — supersession, error recovery, and the uncatchable out-of-memory path :: Job supersession, the previous-capture sweep, the retained current photo for the thumbnail, and the typed failure routing all port cleanly to structured concurrency. One behavior does not: Android catches `OutOfMemoryError` and shows "That photo is too large to process. Try a smaller one." On iOS there is no catchable out-of-memory condition — the system jetsams the process. The stated safety property ("the Estimating carbs… spinner can never get permanently stuck") therefore has to be achieved by prevention plus crash-recovery rather than by a catch block. :: REC: Do all three: ImageIO downsampling as the primary prevention, a header-only dimension pre-check reusing the exact Android copy so the message still exists as a user-facing state, and the launch-time marker so a jetsam cannot leave a stuck medical spinner behind. Everything else ports verbatim — cancel the in-flight task before starting a new one so a previous capture's file is never deleted out from under an active read; delete the previous capture off the main actor; three-way failure routing (`CancellationError` rethrown / prevented-OOM copy / "Couldn't read that photo. Try another one."); multipart part name `file`, filename `meal.jpg`, `image/jpeg`, posted on the 90s session; and VISION / NO_PROVIDER as dead-end states with a Back button that calls `reset()` rather than retryable errors.
- Home meal glance — the draggable, position-persisting meal FAB :: The Recent Meal card ports unchanged. The draggable extended FAB does not belong on iOS: there is no FAB idiom, a free-floating draggable control conflicts with the tab bar, the Home indicator safe area, and Dynamic Type layout, and its per-device saved position plus the VoiceOver "Reset position to default" custom action exist purely to compensate for Android's drag being pointer-only. Keeping it would be porting an Android accommodation rather than the capability. :: REC: Ship option 1 with option 2's list row as a redundant entry point, and drop drag entirely. This preserves what the drag existed to protect (the button never obscuring content, and reachability) using layout instead of user labor: the button sits above the tab bar and the Home content keeps its bottom inset so the last card is never covered. Keep every gate: the button is absent when no backend is configured and absent when meal intelligence is locally off; a transient/offline probe failure keeps it visible (only `featureDisabled` hides it) so the meal screen can show its own degraded state; and the setting is re-checked on both the success and failure paths so a toggle flipped mid-flight cannot re-expose it. Keep the Recent Meal card exactly as-is, including the neutral placeholder thumbnail (the backend returns no image) and the embedded verify-before-dosing qualifier.
- Onboarding server setup, connection test, and the inline insecure-LAN-HTTP opt-in :: The form, debounce, and test flow port, but the cleartext story does not. iOS App Transport Security blocks `http://` at the URLSession layer before the app's own policy runs, and ATS cannot be scoped to private IP ranges (see the impossible entry). Separately, iOS 14+ Local Network Privacy means any connection to an RFC1918 address, a `.local` name, or the link-local range triggers a one-time system permission prompt; the app must declare `NSLocalNetworkUsageDescription`, and if the user denies it, connections to a LAN server fail with an opaque error and cannot be re-prompted in-app. That is a second, independent failure mode the Android flow has no equivalent for, and it is the single most likely reason a self-hoster's first connection test fails on iOS. :: REC: See the PM question — the ATS posture is a genuine product/review-risk call. Whichever posture is chosen, the flow itself must gain a Local Network permission step: request it before or during the first connection test, and when it is denied say so specifically ("iOS is blocking access to devices on your local network. Turn on Local Network for GlycemicGPT in Settings.") with a Settings deep link, never a generic "Connection failed". Everything else ports verbatim: the 1000ms debounce after validation, the shared `INVALID_URL_MESSAGE`, persisting the typed URL before the test so the request layer routes to it and restoring the previous known-good URL on failure, the acknowledgement dialog carrying `INSECURE_HTTP_RISK_TEXT` word-for-word, confirming re-running the test with the debounce forced so the flow proceeds without a second tap, and editing the URL clearing both the result and the opt-in affordance. The inline opt-in must stay inline because a fresh install still cannot reach Settings before signing in.
- Onboarding login, BLE-only entry, notification permission, and completion :: The form, validation, error copy, password wiping, and the load-bearing cancel-then-clear ordering in `continueWithoutServer()` all port. Two things change materially. First, iOS presents the notification authorization prompt exactly once per install; a denial is permanent until the user visits Settings, and any options you want (`.criticalAlert`, `.timeSensitive`-capable delivery, `.provisional`) must be requested in that single call — you cannot come back later and ask for critical alerts. That makes the request's composition a one-shot, safety-critical decision rather than the routine runtime permission it is on Android. Second, credential storage moves to Keychain, and the accessibility class is a correctness decision, not a detail: `kSecAttrAccessibleWhenUnlocked` would make the token unreadable while the phone is locked, silently breaking overnight token refresh and any background work the alert path depends on. :: REC: Option 1. Request the full option set including `.criticalAlert` at the end of both completion paths (after login and after "Use without a server (BLE-only)"), because the Android comment applies with more force on iOS: the on-device alert floor is silently suppressed without notification permission, so a user could believe they are monitored when they are not. If the critical-alerts entitlement is not yet granted, requesting `.criticalAlert` is harmless. Keep the ordering property — resolve the permission before flipping the navigate flag, so the prompt does not race the navigation transition. Store the access token, refresh token, and expiry in Keychain with `kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlock`, in a shared access group with the widget/notification-service extensions. Keep the start-destination policy keyed on onboarding completion alone, deliberately independent of session validity, so an expired refresh token cannot lock locally-cached pump data behind an impossible login. Keep clearing the password from state on both success and failure.
- Settings Network section, the URL security policy, and the app-wide insecure-transport banner :: `UrlSecurityPolicy` is pure logic and ports line-for-line to Swift — the strict canonical IPv4 parser (rejecting octal, hex, bare-decimal, leading zeros, >255 octets), the IPv6 parser including IPv4-mapped classification, the `.local` exception requiring a label, and the deliberate refusal to ever perform DNS. What changes is that on iOS this policy is no longer the sole guard: ATS sits underneath it and will reject cleartext before the policy is consulted unless ATS is configured to allow it, and Local Network Privacy sits alongside it and can block the connection entirely regardless of scheme. So the app now has three independent gates where Android had one, and the user-facing state must distinguish them or every LAN failure will look the same. :: REC: Do both. Port `UrlSecurityPolicy` unchanged including `isAllowed`, `isBlockedPendingLanOptIn`, and `isActiveInsecureHttp` — note the Android method is `isBlockedPendingLanOptIn`, not `isBlockedPendingLanHttpOptIn` as the map states. Keep the toggle semantics exactly: enabling routes through the acknowledgement dialog and only confirmation persists it; disabling is immediate; the setting is per-device and survives logout because the LAN box is the same across sign-ins. Keep the app-wide banner driven reactively by the cross-product of the opt-in flag and the base URL through `isActiveInsecureHttp`, so it lights only when cleartext would actually be sent and never for a public `http://` URL the request layer rejects — and keep it rendering above every screen including onboarding. Add a second, distinct banner or card state for "Local Network access denied", because that condition is invisible in the Simulator and will otherwise be misdiagnosed as a server problem.
- Settings Plugins section — pump card and driver activation :: The pump card, its generic (no hardcoded vendor id) driver lookup, the paired/not-paired state, the informational notes reduced from the driver's settings descriptor, the re-pair/unpair/pair buttons, and the unpair confirmation all port. The plugin model underneath does not: iOS forbids dynamic code loading, so drivers are compile-time-linked Swift modules. "Available plugins" becomes a static registry of built-in drivers that can be enabled/disabled, and "activate" means selecting a driver, not loading one. The runtime/custom plugin card is impossible and is listed separately. :: REC: Option 1. Preserve the safety behaviors: deactivation always routes through the confirmation dialog with the same copy, and the derived-visibility rule ports (a source plugin that only makes sense with a backend is hidden in BLE-only mode unless it is already active, so Deactivate stays reachable). Keep the visibility as a derived/computed property rather than filtering at mutation sites so it cannot go stale against the driver list, active set, and backend mode. Keep the per-driver failure isolation so a descriptor read failure drops only the notes while the card survives. Version strings come from the driver module's own metadata.
- Settings Notifications permission status card :: On Android there is effectively one axis: POST_NOTIFICATIONS granted or not. On iOS `UNUserNotificationCenter.getNotificationSettings()` reports many independent axes that can each silently suppress an alarm — `authorizationStatus`, `alertSetting`, `soundSetting`, `criticalAlertSetting`, `lockScreenSetting`, `notificationCenterSetting`, `timeSensitiveSetting`, and `scheduledDeliverySetting` (the Notification Summary, which can delay a non-time-sensitive notification by hours). A straight port of "Enabled / Disabled" would report Enabled while the alarm is muted, summarized, or Focus-suppressed — which directly violates the subsystem's honest-degradation contract. :: REC: Options 1 and 2 combined: a single prominent verdict line in error color when anything would suppress a low alarm — keeping the Android voice, e.g. "Disabled — alerts will not appear on your phone" for the denied case — plus an itemized list of the specific conditions (sound off, critical alerts not allowed, Scheduled Summary on, Time Sensitive not allowed). Re-check on every foreground transition so returning from Settings updates it. The Enable button opens `UIApplication.openNotificationSettingsURLString` on iOS 16+, falling back to `openSettingsURLString`, with the same toast-equivalent fallback copy if the URL cannot be opened. Option 3 is not acceptable for this app.
- Settings alert sounds and the low-alert volume boost :: iOS has no ringtone picker API, no per-channel sound model, and no way to raise system volume — those specific mechanisms are listed as impossible. But the user-facing capability (choose how each alert category sounds; make low alarms loud and unmissable) is achievable in a different shape. Sounds must be files bundled in the app (or in the app container's Library/Sounds), in Linear PCM / MA4 / µLaw / aLaw packaged as .aiff/.wav/.caf, and ≤30 seconds, referenced per notification request via `UNNotificationSound`. Bypassing Focus and the silent switch requires `.critical` interruption level, the `com.apple.developer.usernotifications.critical-alerts` entitlement (granted only by Apple on request), and the user having granted the `.criticalAlert` authorization option. The freely-available `com.apple.developer.usernotifications.time-sensitive` entitlement with `.timeSensitive` interruption level breaks through Focus if the user permits, but not through the silent switch or a low volume. :: REC: Options 1 + 2, with 3 as the documented fallback. Reject option 4. Preserve the section's structure: three category rows (Low Glucose Alert / High Glucose Alert / AI Notification) with the same labels and descriptions, the AI row showing a Silent option and hidden entirely in BLE-only mode because AI notifications are backend-generated. Replace the ringtone picker with a bundled-sound list plus preview. Replace "Boost Volume for Lows" with a control whose copy is honest about what iOS actually guarantees; keep the invariant that low and high alerts always attempt Focus bypass independent of the volume control, and keep low alerts re-alerting on each update while highs and AI alert once (on iOS, distinct request identifiers per low update rather than `setOnlyAlertOnce(false)`). The entire versioned-channel-recreation machinery, its lock, and the non-atomic increment hazard simply disappear, because iOS sound is a per-request property — a real simplification.
- Settings Watch section — connection status, feature configuration, and data telemetry :: Status and telemetry port to WatchConnectivity, but the mental model changes. `WCSession` gives `isPaired`, `isWatchAppInstalled`, `activationState`, and `isReachable` directly — no capability query, no node enumeration, and no need for Android's careful distinction between CapabilityClient (which proves the app is installed) and NodeClient (which does not). The 15s discovery timeout, the nearby-node preference, and the fallback logic all become unnecessary. What does not port is the watch-face gallery and the watch-app APK push, which are listed as impossible; and the feature toggles change meaning, because with no custom face they now configure the watch app's own screens and its WidgetKit complications rather than a face. :: REC: Option 1, with the toggle set trimmed honestly: keep Show IoB, Show Glucose Graph, Alert Vibration, Graph Range (1H/3H/6H, coerced to a valid value with a fallback of 3), Theme, and the graph overlay toggles (basal area, bolus markers, IoB overlay, activity-mode bands) as configuration for the watch app's main screen and its `accessoryRectangular` widget. Drop "Show Seconds", which has no meaning without a face. Sync via `WCSession.updateApplicationContext` (latest-value-wins, exactly the right semantics for config) and keep the Android rule that the sync reads `aiTtsEnabled`/`aiTtsVoice` from the canonical store rather than the config copy, so a value set on the chat screen is never overwritten. Keep the telemetry card verbatim, including the 20–500 mg/dL validation of the reported value, the explicit "(wire, mg/dL)" label so a canonical wire value is never misread as a display value, the `formatTimeAgo` thresholds, and the "Could not read watch data" failure state. Keep the two hint cards for the not-installed and not-reachable states, retargeted at TestFlight installation guidance.
- Settings About — version info, licenses entry, and update awareness :: The version rows and the Open Source Licenses entry port unchanged. The in-app update state machine cannot: downloading and installing an app build is impossible on iOS (listed separately). The capability that remains is telling the user a newer build exists and getting them to it. :: REC: Option 2, with the state machine collapsed to Idle → Checking → (UpToDate | Available | Error). Reuse the existing GitHub error copy (403 rate limit, 404 no dev release, other status codes) and the channel selection between the stable release and the dev tag. Everything after 'Available' is replaced by a single 'Open TestFlight' action. The standalone HTTP client with no auth or base-URL interception ports as a separate `URLSession`; the https-only enforcement and the github.com / objects.githubusercontent.com host allowlist remain good practice for the metadata request even though nothing is downloaded. Keep the version, build type, and active-driver protocol rows, and keep the Open Source Licenses row exactly where it is.
- Settings Battery Optimization card — the background-monitoring-reliability advisory :: iOS has no per-app battery optimization exemption, no `isIgnoringBatteryOptimizations` equivalent, and no Settings destination for such a control — that specific request is not available on the platform. But the card's actual purpose (warn the user when a device setting will kill overnight monitoring, and send them to the fix) has real iOS analogues, and they are arguably more important here because Core Bluetooth background delivery and any background refresh are what keep monitoring alive. :: REC: Options 1, 2, and 3 together in one card that renders only when a pump is paired and at least one condition is degraded, keeping the Android placement between Plugins and Sync and its red warning treatment. The force-quit condition (option 3) is the closest true analogue of Android's battery-optimization kill: on iOS a user swiping the app away from the app switcher stops Core Bluetooth state restoration from relaunching it, which is exactly the 'Android may stop the pump connection when the screen is off' failure in iOS clothing, and it deserves explicit copy. Re-evaluate on every foreground transition, as the Android card does on every `loadState()`.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (24)
- MEAL CARB ESTIMATES ARE NEVER A DOSE. `VERIFY_BEFORE_DOSING_TEXT` = "Rough estimate — an AI guess that's often wrong. Never use it to calculate an insulin dose or bolus." must accompany EVERY carb estimate surface: the Log-a-Meal result (pinned above the scroll region so it can never scroll away), the meal history list header, the common-foods list header, the Re-log dialog, the Edit-common-food dialog (embedded inside the modal because the AlertDialog scrim dims the screen-level one), and the Home Recent-meal card. Domain KDoc calls the posture NON-NEGOTIABLE. Test tag `meal_safety_qualifier`; UI-tested by VerifyBeforeDosingQualifierUiTest.
- Carbs are ALWAYS rendered as a low/high range plus a confidence signal, never a lone integer. A single-point estimate still renders as "≈ N g carbs". Nothing in the meal contract carries insulin, units, or a bolus by design.
- Accessibility merge: `CarbEstimateContent` merges the carb value + confidence + safety qualifier + dispersion note into ONE content description so a screen reader can never announce the number without the never-dose qualifier.
- Carb input bounds (client-side, mirroring the backend's reject-not-clamp): 0.0–1000.0 g, low <= high, NaN rejected. Invalid input NEVER reaches the API and is NEVER silently clamped. Shared by the food-record correction and common-food edit paths so the rules cannot drift.
- Alert threshold validation (SETTINGS, BLE-only editor): all four values must be within 20–500 mg/dL and satisfy urgentLow <= low < high <= urgentHigh. Inputs are parsed in the user's display unit and stored canonically in mg/dL via `value * 18.0156` rounded. `AlertThresholdStore.updateLocal/updateAll` re-validate with `require()` as defense-in-depth and use `commit()` (synchronous). A backend threshold response that fails validation is DROPPED, never clamped, leaving last-known-good values.
- Alert threshold arming: the on-device alert floor NEVER fires off the hardcoded defaults (55/70/180/250) — `isConfigured()` requires a real source (BACKEND sync or LOCAL editor save). Until then the Settings copy says, in ERROR color, "On-device alarms are OFF until you set all four thresholds."
- Backend is master for alert thresholds: with a server configured the Settings editor is READ-ONLY, and `saveLocalAlertThresholds` re-checks `authRepository.isBackendConfigured()` LIVE (not the cached UI flag) and returns without writing. Saving a server URL flips the editor read-only in the same state update.
- Logout clears BACKEND alert thresholds (disarming the floor so it can never fire off another account's values) but PRESERVES LOCAL ones. `clearBaseUrl()` on the BLE-only onboarding path does the same, so a now-server-less user is not left with a departed server's thresholds armed.
- POST_NOTIFICATIONS is requested on BOTH onboarding completion paths — after login AND after "Use without a server (BLE-only)". Code comment: "Requesting POST_NOTIFICATIONS here is safety-critical: the on-device alert floor is silently suppressed without the permission, so a tier-1 user could later believe they are monitored when they are not."
- Settings Notifications card states, in ERROR color when the permission is missing: "Disabled -- alerts will not appear on your phone".
- Low and High glucose alert channels are created with `setBypassDnd(true)` unconditionally, independent of the "Boost Volume for Lows" toggle; the toggle only raises alarm-stream volume for lows. Low alerts use `setOnlyAlertOnce(false)` so the sound re-fires on every update ("Lows are life-threatening").
- Changing an alert sound DELETES and recreates the notification channel with an incremented version (Android channel sounds are immutable). This happens under `channelLock`, and `incrementChannelVersion` is a non-atomic read-modify-write that REQUIRES that lock — a race would produce duplicate channel versions and could leave alerts posting to a deleted channel.
- Glucose unit conversion uses the single canonical constant `MGDL_PER_MMOL = 18.0156` with a written prohibition on introducing a second value (18.02 / 18.0182). Conversion happens ONCE from the most precise mg/dL source and rounds LAST. A glucose SPREAD must use `convertSpread` (offset-free), never `convertValue` — reusing the value converter for a spread is documented as a bug.
- Watch telemetry BG is validated to 20..500 mg/dL and shown as null outside that range, and its label explicitly reads "Last BG sent (wire, mg/dL)" so a canonical wire value is never misread as a display-unit value.
- Debug fault injection (`simulateBackendUnreachable`, `debugFastStaleness`) and the BLE-debug CGM injectors are HARD-GATED to debug builds at the store getter/setter AND again inside the ViewModel (`if (!BuildConfig.DEBUG) return`) because injections write to the same Room cache that drives the real glucose hero and can fire a real OS alarm.
- Debug LOW injections are the alert-floor safety harness: a FRESH 54 mg/dL must FIRE the floor; a 54 mg/dL aged 60s (past the compressed policy's 45s TOO_STALE bound) must be SUPPRESSED with the surface reading "NOT watching".
- `UrlSecurityPolicy` is the SOLE guard preventing a public host from being reached over plaintext http:// (network_security_config permits cleartext globally for the LAN opt-in and cannot express CIDR ranges). It never performs DNS (DNS-rebinding defense), parses only strict canonical IP literals, and requires an explicit acknowledged opt-in plus a private/LAN host. Enabling insecure LAN HTTP is gated behind an acknowledgement dialog and surfaces a persistent app-wide banner.
- AI chat and meal-photo estimates never surface raw exception messages; every failure path maps to curated copy (unit-tested: "never surfaces a raw exception message on unexpected failures").
- The onboarding disclaimer page cannot be swiped past — only the explicit "I Understand" button advances it — and it includes a dedicated card naming the prohibited action for photo carb estimates: "Never use a photo carb estimate to calculate an insulin dose or bolus."
- Meal photo uploads strip ALL EXIF/GPS metadata by re-encoding the bitmap (defense-in-depth on top of server-side stripping), and the transient full-resolution capture file is deleted after upload or on cancellation.
- Every upload/compression failure path — including `OutOfMemoryError` — clears `isUploading`, so the "Estimating carbs…" spinner can never get permanently stuck on a medical surface.
- Meal history / common foods show a terminal error state with Retry when the backend is unreachable, explicitly NOT a misleading "No meals logged yet." empty state.
- AI chat TTS always appends ". This is not medical advice." to the spoken response, and the chat screen carries a permanent footer "AI responses are informational only. Always consult your healthcare provider."
- `AppMarkdownText` strips all image markdown and `<img>` tags from AI-generated content to prevent outbound image fetches, and only opens http/https links.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (45)
  AI Chat screen — page state machine and provider probe | AI Chat — message send, in-memory transcript, length caps, error taxonomy | AI Chat — screen layout, suggestions, markdown rendering, ================================================================================
## Plugin architecture and Nightscout  [plugin-architecture]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (40)
- The app must ship with every supported pump and CGM driver compiled into the signed binary; the user must be able to see every driver the build contains without installing anything, and must never be offered a way to add a driver at runtime.
- The user must be able to view a list of available drivers showing name and version, and activate or deactivate each one.
- Activating a driver that claims a single-instance capability (glucose source, insulin source, pump status, calibration target, bolus-category provider) must automatically deactivate the previously active driver for that capability, and the capability slot must never be momentarily empty during the swap.
- Multiple blood-glucose-meter sources and multiple data-sync sources must be able to be active at the same time; activating a cloud data source must never evict an active pump or CGM driver.
- Deactivating a driver must require an explicit confirmation that names the consequences (may stop glucose monitoring, insulin tracking, or other services it provides).
- The user's driver selection must survive app relaunch, device restart, and app upgrade, and must be restored automatically at launch — including a launch the system triggers in the background because of a Bluetooth event, with no user interface and no network available.
- A driver disabled by a build-time kill switch must be entirely absent from the driver list and must never run, and re-enabling it in a later build must restore the user's prior selection without the user re-choosing it.
- A driver that fails to initialize must be skipped without preventing any other driver from loading and without crashing the app; a driver that fails while being activated must not be recorded as active.
- Each driver must have private, namespaced settings storage that survives deactivation and reactivation, app upgrade, and background operation while the device is locked.
- Pump pairing secrets (device identifier, pairing code, derived handshake secrets) must be stored in the iOS Keychain, must remain readable during a background Bluetooth wake while the device is locked, must never sync to iCloud or leave the device in a backup, and must be purged automatically on a fresh install following app deletion.
- The app must let the user pair with a pump the phone discovers by scanning, showing each discovered device's name and signal strength, letting the user cancel the scan, and failing a connection attempt within a bounded time rather than retrying indefinitely.
- The app must let the user pair with a pump that connects to the phone rather than the reverse. This flow must be presented as a foreground, screen-on, user-attended pairing session that tells the user the exact name to select on the pump's own pairing menu and keeps the screen awake for its duration.
- When an advertise-and-wait pairing fails, the app must show a specific reason — device does not support this mode, advertising failed, handshake timed out, authentication failed, or the pump is already paired to another phone — even though no connection state change occurs.
- Connection state must be surfaced to the user across at least: disconnected, scanning, connecting, authenticating, authentication failed, connected, and reconnecting.
- After the system terminates and relaunches the app in the background, the app must reconnect to a previously paired device and resume data collection without the user re-pairing.
- When a stored device identifier no longer resolves (for example after an app reinstall), the app must attempt recovery before asking the user to re-pair, and must tell the user plainly if re-pairing is required.
- Drivers must expose no therapeutic write of any kind. The driver SDK must contain no bolus delivery, no basal change, and no pump-setting write; connect, disconnect, calibrate, unpair, and reconnect must be the only operations that touch the device.
- Every glucose value entering the system must be rejected — never clamped, never silently corrected — if it falls outside 20–500 mg/dL, at reading construction, at cloud ingestion, at calibration request, and at calibration validation.
- Every bolus must be rejected outside 0–25 units; every basal rate ingested from the cloud must be rejected outside 0–15 units/hour; non-finite values must be rejected by an explicit finiteness check.
- Rejection of an out-of-range record must be recoverable and logged with the limit that was violated; it must never terminate the app and must never write a corrected value.
- Safety limits supplied by the backend must only ever narrow the absolute clinical bounds; a value that would widen them must be clamped on write and clamped again on read, and must never take effect.
- Safety limits must be persisted as a single atomic record and must be available to every driver as a live value that updates as soon as the backend refreshes them; drivers must read them fresh on every use rather than caching a snapshot.
- Any read of pump history, status, or insulin data while no driver is active must return a result the caller can distinguish from a genuine empty result — the app must never present 'no driver installed' as 'the patient had no boluses'.
- The user must be able to enable a cloud data source that pulls their Nightscout-derived glucose and insulin from their own backend account into the same local store the Bluetooth drivers write into, without a second Nightscout client on the device.
- The cloud data source must be off by default, must retain already-synced data when disabled, and must not be offerable at all when no backend is configured — unless it is already active, in which case turning it off must remain reachable.
- The cloud data source must show the time of the last successful sync and a distinct state for each of: never synced, healthy, no Nightscout connection configured, re-authentication required, and sync error that will be retried. The last successful sync time must remain visible even while an error state is shown.
- The user must be able to trigger an immediate sync on demand and see the sync state update without leaving the screen.
- When the user's account has more than one active Nightscout connection, the user must be able to choose which one syncs; when only one exists no choice may be presented; and if the chosen connection is deleted or deactivated the app must report that there is no connection rather than silently syncing a different one.
- Background sync must be best-effort and the interface must never imply a freshness the platform did not deliver: staleness shown to the user must be derived from each reading's real capture time, not from when the app fetched it, so a frozen cloud feed ages out exactly like a frozen Bluetooth feed.
- A background sync run must finish within the time the system grants it or abort cleanly, persisting the progress it made so the next run resumes from the same point; it must never skip, lose, or double-count records, and a first-time full backfill must not be attempted inside a short background window.
- Records that arrive at the same timestamp from a cloud source and a Bluetooth driver must collapse to a single record with a deterministic, documented winner per data type, so no dose or reading is ever counted twice.
- The app must run usefully with no backend configured: driver activation, pairing, Bluetooth data collection, dashboard display, and local storage must all work without a server.
- The app must be able to reach a self-hosted backend on the user's local network, must ask for local network access with a clear explanation, and must show the user when their connection to that backend is unencrypted.
- Logs must never contain glucose values, insulin doses, or backend connection credentials; raw device packet captures may exist only in non-release builds and must never be written to disk unencrypted.
- Drivers must be able to contribute dashboard cards and a tappable detail screen built entirely from a fixed set of declarative elements the platform renders; no driver may supply view code.
- Plugin card ordering must be deterministic across refreshes, with platform cards above driver cards, and driver-supplied content must not be able to degrade or hang the interface (nesting beyond five levels and detail screens beyond one hundred elements must be refused).
- A setting the user types must be persisted when they leave the field or leave the screen, never silently discarded by navigating away.
- The Apple Watch app must not host drivers and must not perform device pairing; it must display data the phone has already collected. Driver management, pairing, plugin cards, and plugin detail screens are phone-only surfaces.
- The app must include a built-in simulated data source that produces realistic, safety-limit-validated readings and exercises the full driver lifecycle, card rendering, settings persistence, and detail screens, so the entire driver-management experience can be developed and validated in the iOS Simulator without hardware. It must be excludable from production builds.
- The app must declare its active driver's bolus-category vocabulary and its mapping to platform categories to the backend when a driver becomes active, and must clear that declaration when no driver or no category provider is active; failure to do so must never block driver activation.

### IMPOSSIBLE (4)
- Runtime plugin loading and execution (DexPluginLoader: DexClassLoader over sideloaded JARs, manifest-driven reflective factory instantiation, discovery from filesDir/plugins/*.jar at startup) :: Apple forbids it at two independent levels. App Store Review Guideline 2.5.2 requires that apps be self-contained and prohibits reading or writing data outside their container and downloading, installing, or executing code that introduces or changes features — and this applies to TestFlight builds too (external-tester builds go through Beta App Review, and the Developer Program License Agreement clause the guideline derives from applies regardless of distribution channel). Independently, the OS enforces it: iOS requires every executable page to be backed by code signed at build time by the developer's certificate, there is no writable-executable memory outside the dynamic-codesigning entitlement that only WebKit holds, and `dlopen` only succeeds for frameworks already embedded and signed inside the app bundle. There is no workaround, no entitlement to request, and no enterprise or TestFlight carve-out. :: ALT: Compile-time-linked Swift driver packages, which is the decision already made. A contributor's driver ships to users only by being merged and included in the next TestFlight build; a developer can run an unmerged driver on their own device by building the fork in Xcode with their own signing identity. If a lightweight extension path is ever wanted, the only lawful version is a purely declarative, non-executable driver descriptor (a JSON/plist GATT map) interpreted by an engine already compiled into the app — that is data, not code, so it is permitted, but it cannot express any protocol requiring an authenticated handshake, so it would not cover either shipped driver.
- Runtime plugin package install and removal (PluginFileManager: SAF file-picker install of a .jar, 50 MB cap, ZIP-magic validation, canonical-path containment, setReadOnly for DexClassLoader; plus the Custom Plugins add/remove UI and its trust warning) :: This machinery exists only to place executable code on disk for DexClassLoader to run. With runtime execution forbidden, an install flow has nothing to install into. Copying an arbitrary file into the app container via UIDocumentPicker is technically possible on iOS, but a file that can never be executed serves no purpose, and shipping an 'Add Plugin' button that produces an inert file would be user-hostile and would likely itself draw review attention under 2.5.2. :: ALT: Remove the flow entirely and replace the Custom Plugins card with a read-only 'Included drivers' list (name, version, protocol, build-gate state), so the information the card conveyed — what drivers this build contains and at what version — still has a home. The trust warning becomes unnecessary because every driver in an iOS build is signed and reviewed as part of the app binary.
- PluginManifest (META-INF/plugin.json: factoryClass, apiVersion, id, name, version, author, description; parse-order-sensitive validation, 65,536-char size cap, resource-shadowing hazard) :: A manifest is the metadata half of a loadable code artifact. With no loadable artifact there is no manifest, no factory class name to reflect on, no independent id that can diverge from the driver's own metadata, and no resource-stream shadowing hazard. Reflective instantiation from a class-name string is additionally not something Swift supports for pure-Swift types. :: ALT: The driver's `PluginMetadata`, declared in Swift and constructed at compile time, is the single source of truth — which also permanently eliminates the Android hazard where a manifest id and a metadata id disagree and the registry keys on one while the UI displays the other. The manifest's apiVersion field becomes a CI-enforced assertion rather than a parsed value.
- RestrictedPluginContext / RestrictedContext in-process sandbox (ContextWrapper subclass blocking ~30 methods: startActivity/startService/bindService, getContentResolver, sendBroadcast/registerReceiver, getSharedPreferences, file and database APIs, createPackageContext, device-protected storage; getApplicationContext trapped to return itself; allowlisted getSystemService set of BLUETOOTH/LOCATION/POWER/ALARM/SENSOR/USB/WIFI with WINDOW deliberately excluded) :: The sandbox is a language-level wrapper whose entire purpose is to constrain untrusted third-party code running in-process. On iOS there is no untrusted in-process code — every line in the binary was compiled and signed by the project — so the threat model it addresses does not exist. It is also unbuildable in principle: there is no `Context` object to wrap, iOS APIs are reached through global singletons and framework types that no wrapper can interpose on, and Swift has no mechanism to deny a linked module access to `CBCentralManager` or `FileManager`. Android's own docs already record that this sandbox is porous (a plugin can reflect over host classes to bypass it) and that true process isolation was considered and rejected as too complex. :: ALT: The iOS trust boundary moves from runtime enforcement to code review plus SPM target dependencies. Driver targets depend only on `DriverAPI` and cannot import the platform target, which is a *compile-time* guarantee stronger than anything RestrictedContext provided at runtime. The safety guarantee that actually mattered survives intact and unchanged: enforcement comes from SafetyLimits and the absence of any therapeutic write in the SDK surface, not from Context restrictions — Android's own stated design philosophy. The credential-isolation goal that ScopedCredentialProvider served (and its documented accepted risk of plaintext SharedPreferences for runtime plugins) is superseded outright by per-driver Keychain namespacing for all drivers, which is strictly stronger than what either Android path had.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (15)
- Three-layer module topology and compile-time driver discovery (Hilt `Set<PluginFactory>` @IntoSet multibinding) :: There is no Hilt/Dagger on iOS and no compile-time multibinding mechanism in Swift. Nothing discovers conformers of a protocol at build time; Swift has no equivalent of annotation processing that assembles a set across modules. Objective-C runtime class enumeration exists but does not see pure-Swift types reliably and is a fragile, review-unfriendly hack. The three-layer separation itself (platform / driver API / drivers) maps perfectly onto SPM targets, and the ProGuard keep rules become moot (Swift is not name-minified). :: REC: Option 1. SPM targets: `DriverAPI` (was `:pump-driver-api` — protocols, capability contracts, event enum, descriptors, domain models, SafetyLimits), `Platform` (was `:app` — registry, renderers, safety-limit enforcement, credential storage), and one target per driver (`TandemDriver`, `MedtronicDriver`), with the Nightscout source living in the app target for exactly the Android reason (it needs the networking + persistence + scheduling stack a driver target must not depend on). Drivers depend only on `DriverAPI`; enforce that with SPM target dependencies, which is a harder guarantee than Android's Gradle convention.
