Keep the explicit registration array as the single, reviewable seam. Also carry over the ID-reservation ordering: reserve each built-in driver's id before constructing it, so a transient init failure cannot leave the id claimable — cheap to keep, and it documents intent even without runtime plugins.
- PluginRegistry (startup, derived capability slots, restore-from-preferences, runtime install/remove) :: Three changes. (1) The runtime install/remove half of the API disappears entirely (see impossible). (2) `synchronized(activationLock)` + ConcurrentHashMap needs to become Swift structured concurrency, and Android's practice of holding the activation lock across file I/O and DEX loading is a mistake not worth reproducing. (3) The biggest one: on iOS the app can be relaunched into the background by Core Bluetooth state restoration with no UI and no scene. Android calls `initialize()` from `Application.onCreate` after Timber/Sentry/networkMonitor and before auth validation; the iOS equivalent must be reachable from `application(_:didFinishLaunchingWithOptions:)` on a background launch, complete synchronously enough that the restored central manager's delegate callbacks find a live driver, and must not depend on any UI, scene, or authenticated backend session. :: REC: Option 2. Registry construction and driver construction happen synchronously at launch, before any Core Bluetooth manager is created; activation, deactivation, and safety-limit refresh are actor-isolated. Keep `initialize()` idempotency as a hard failure on double-call (Android throws IllegalStateException — port it as a `precondition` here, because a double-initialized registry is a programmer error, not a runtime condition). Port the derived-slot resolution rules unchanged, including the deliberate `as? DevicePlugin` downcast that keeps the Nightscout source out of the pump/glucose slots. Port `restoreActivePlugins`'s subtle rules: an absent driver leaves its preference UNTOUCHED (so a gated-off driver's selection survives a later re-enable), but an onActivated *failure* clears it. `initialize()` must never require network or an auth session — the whole restore path has to work in backend-optional local mode and on a background launch.
- DevicePlugin hardware contract (connect(address,config)/disconnect/scan/observeConnectionState, 30s timeout convention, DiscoveredDevice.address) :: Core Bluetooth differs from Android BLE in ways that reach into the data model, not just the API. (a) iOS never exposes a MAC address; you get `CBPeripheral.identifier`, a UUID that is per-app and per-device and is NOT stable across app reinstall — so a persisted 'paired address' can go dead after a reinstall even though the pump is still bonded, and it will never match across the user's phone and any other device. (b) `centralManager.connect(_:options:)` has no timeout and will retry forever, so the 'typically 30 seconds' documented convention must become real code. (c) There are no foreground services; background operation depends entirely on the `bluetooth-central` background mode plus State Preservation and Restoration (`CBCentralManagerOptionRestoreIdentifierKey` + `centralManager(_:willRestoreState:)`), which relaunches the app into the background on a connection or notification event. (d) iOS does not require location permission for BLE (Android does), so the Android permission story shrinks — but `NSBluetoothAlwaysUsageDescription` is mandatory or the app is killed the moment a manager is instantiated. (e) `scan()` as a completing flow maps fine, but iOS drops duplicate advertisements by default and disallows scanning with no service UUID filter while backgrounded. :: REC: Option 2 plus the serial-number backstop from option 3 where the protocol exposes one (Tandem does, via PumpSettings.serialNumber). Concretely: `DiscoveredDevice(name:identifier:rssi:driverId:metadata:)` with `identifier: UUID`; `connect(identifier:config:)` with a driver-enforced 30-second deadline that transitions to a failure state; each driver owns its own `CBCentralManager` created with a driver-scoped restore identifier, and the platform routes `willRestoreState` back to the owning driver by that identifier. Keep the 7-state `ConnectionState` and the no-platform-enforced-transition-table design exactly as-is — each driver still owns its own state machine. Keep `disconnect()` a no-op when not connected. Info.plist/entitlements this subsystem forces: `NSBluetoothAlwaysUsageDescription`, `UIBackgroundModes: bluetooth-central`. SIMULATOR: none of this is exercisable — Core Bluetooth is absent from the iOS Simulator and the central manager reports an unsupported state, so scanning, connecting, pairing, state restoration, and every driver state machine are device-only. Plan the dev loop around a built-in simulated driver (see the contributor-path entry).
- ADVERTISE_AND_WAIT pairing style (Medtronic MiniMed 700-series: phone is the BLE peripheral, pump connects to it as central, user selects the phone by its advertised name on the pump's own menu) and PairingFault reporting :: This is the single hardest item in the subsystem. iOS can act as a peripheral (`CBPeripheralManager`) and can advertise in the background with the `bluetooth-peripheral` mode, but background advertising is deliberately degraded: `CBAdvertisementDataLocalNameKey` is IGNORED in the background, and advertised service UUIDs are moved into a special 'overflow' area that is only discoverable by another iOS device explicitly scanning for those exact UUIDs. The Medtronic pairing flow depends on the pump seeing a human-readable local name so the user can pick the phone from the pump's menu — which means pairing can only work while the app is in the foreground with the screen on. Additionally, iOS gives no API to control the peripheral's advertised device name independently of the system device name in some contexts, and iOS's own bonding is implicit (triggered by an encrypted characteristic access) with no programmatic bond-management API. The `PairingFault` type exists precisely because advertise-and-wait failures produce no ConnectionState transition, so it remains necessary — arguably more so, since iOS surfaces fewer BLE errors than Android. :: REC: Option 1 for pairing, option 2 for reconnection, and prototype this against real hardware before committing the iOS Medtronic driver to a milestone. Requirements it forces: `UIBackgroundModes: bluetooth-peripheral` in addition to `bluetooth-central`; the pairing screen must be explicitly modelled as foreground-and-attended with instructions naming the advertised device name; all five `PairingFault` values must still be surfaced to the user (unsupported, advertise failed, handshake timeout, auth failed, bound elsewhere), plus an iOS-specific sixth condition worth adding: 'pairing requires the app to stay open with the screen on'. Preserve the Android quirks: `connect` ignores the identifier entirely in this topology, and `scan()` emits a device only after the pump has connected. Do not reproduce Android's `forceFirstPair` config key as a raw string map — model connect config as a typed per-driver struct. SIMULATOR: fully unexercisable; CBPeripheralManager is as absent as CBCentralManager.
- Shipped reference drivers' polling model (`GlucoseSource.observeReadings()` implemented as `flow { while(true) { poll(); delay(60_000) } }` in both Tandem and Medtronic) :: An unbounded `while true { try await Task.sleep(for: .seconds(60)) }` does not run on iOS. Once the app is suspended, the task simply stops and resumes at an arbitrary later time with an unbounded gap and no notification that it stalled. Android relies on a `connectedDevice`-typed foreground service to keep this loop alive; iOS has no foreground services at all. Compounding it, both Android drivers swallow poll failures via `onSuccess` with no backoff and no error surface, which on iOS would make an indefinitely stalled feed indistinguishable from a healthy one. :: REC: Option 3, and change the driver contract to make it explicit: `observeReadings()` returns a stream the driver feeds from device events, and the platform separately calls a `catchUp()`/`getCurrentReading()` on each wake. Do not let a driver own a wall-clock cadence — Android's own code comments already say cadence ownership moves to the polling orchestrator, so this port should land there directly rather than inheriting the interim `while(true)` shape. Add what Android lacks: bounded backoff on repeated failures and a surfaced last-successful-read timestamp per driver, so a stalled feed is visible rather than silent. Note that both Tandem and Medtronic will need BLE-level rework anyway (per-driver `CBCentralManager`, restore identifiers, UUID-not-MAC identity), and Medtronic additionally needs the peripheral-role work above; the driver *shape* (thin capability delegates over a BLE layer, per-driver bolus cursor under a mutex, drop-not-clamp on over-cap boluses, `shutdown()` fully tearing down vs `onDeactivated()` merely disconnecting) all ports unchanged.
- PluginContext (what the platform hands a driver) :: `androidContext: Context` has no iOS analogue and its two real jobs must be replaced by explicit, narrower grants: access to Bluetooth hardware, and access to a place to put files. On iOS the hardware grant is a `CBCentralManager`/`CBPeripheralManager`, and there is a genuine architectural question about who owns those managers because state restoration requires a unique, stable restore identifier per manager and iOS restores every one of them at launch. :: REC: Option 3. `PluginContext` becomes a Swift struct with: `pluginId`, `settingsStore`, `credentialStore` (Keychain-backed, see below), `debugLogger`, `eventBus`, `safetyLimits` (a live value, always current), `apiVersion`, and `bluetooth` (the manager factory). Drop `androidContext` entirely and drop the restricted-vs-full context distinction — with only compile-time drivers there is one context flavor, which removes an entire class of Android complexity. Preserve the DebugLogger contract verbatim including its security clause: TX/RX packet direction, opcode, txId, cargo hex, parsed value, error — retained only in debug builds and never persisted unencrypted, because cargo can contain glucose values, insulin doses, and device identifiers.
- Credential storage (PumpCredentialProvider on EncryptedSharedPreferences; ScopedCredentialProvider on plain SharedPreferences for runtime plugins) :: Keychain is not SharedPreferences with encryption bolted on; three behaviors differ in ways that matter. (1) Accessibility class: the default `WhenUnlocked` would make pump pairing secrets unreadable during a background Bluetooth wake on a locked phone, breaking auto-reconnect exactly when it matters. (2) Keychain items SURVIVE app deletion on iOS — delete and reinstall the app and stale pump pairing credentials are still there, which Android's model never produces. (3) iCloud Keychain would sync pump pairing secrets to the user's other devices unless explicitly prevented. Separately, the entire ScopedCredentialProvider isolation mechanism and its documented accepted risk (runtime plugins can't reach AndroidX Security, so their credentials sit in plaintext prefs) evaporate, because there is no untrusted in-process code to isolate from. :: REC: Option 2, with `kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly` — `AfterFirstUnlock` so background BLE reconnect works on a locked device, `ThisDeviceOnly` so pump pairing secrets never leave the phone via iCloud Keychain or an encrypted backup. Add a first-run purge: on launch, if a 'keychain initialized' flag is absent from UserDefaults (which IS wiped on app deletion), clear all driver credential items — this closes the survives-deletion gap and also fixes the Android bug where removing a plugin deleted its settings but left `plugin_creds_<id>` behind. Keep the id-sanitization rule that preserves dots/hyphens/underscores so `com.foo.bar-baz` and `com.foo.bar.baz` stay distinct namespaces. Related storage decision for this subsystem: the readings database the Nightscout mapper writes into must use file protection `CompleteUntilFirstUserAuthentication` (not `Complete`), or background ingestion on a locked device fails; add SQLCipher only if the threat model expands to physical device extraction, and note it is not needed to protect against another app (the iOS sandbox already does that, unlike the Android threat model that motivated EncryptedSharedPreferences). SIMULATOR: Keychain works but is not Secure-Enclave backed and the simulated device is effectively always unlocked, so `AfterFirstUnlock`-vs-`WhenUnlocked` behavior cannot be distinguished there — that distinction must be verified on a real locked device.
- Fail-fast invariant enforcement (Kotlin `require`/`check` throwing catchable IllegalArgumentException in CgmReading, BgmReading, BolusEvent, SafetyLimits, CalibrationRequested, and every UI descriptor) :: This is the highest-consequence translation decision in the subsystem and it is easy to get silently wrong. Android's invariants throw catchable exceptions, and the platform *relies* on catching them: SettingsViewModel wraps `metadata` and `settingsDescriptor()` in `try/catch(Throwable)` specifically so a misbehaving plugin degrades instead of crashing. Swift has no catchable equivalent — `precondition`, `assert`, and `fatalError` trap the process and cannot be recovered from. A literal transcription of `require(...)` into `precondition(...)` converts 'this driver's reading was rejected' into 'the glucose monitoring app crash-loops'. Going the other way and clamping is explicitly called out in the map as the worse failure: it turns a loud driver bug into a wrong number on a glucose chart. :: REC: Option 2 for every value that can originate from a device, the backend, or a driver: `CgmReading`, `BgmReading`, `BolusEvent`, `SafetyLimits`'s strict init, `CalibrationRequested`, and all UI descriptors. Reserve `precondition` strictly for programmer errors that cannot occur at runtime with valid input — double-initializing the registry is the only real instance in this subsystem, and the capability-exhaustiveness check becomes a compile-time exhaustive switch and disappears entirely. The behavioral contract to preserve: construction of an out-of-range reading must FAIL — never clamp, never silently drop without a logged reason naming the violated limit. Where Android caught Throwable around `metadata`/`settingsDescriptor()`, Swift needs no equivalent for compile-time drivers (there are no linkage errors), but keep the descriptor accessors `throws` so a driver that computes an invalid descriptor degrades to 'no notes shown' rather than taking down Settings — and preserve Android's deliberate split where a descriptor failure drops only the informational notes while keeping the pump card, since losing metadata necessarily drops the card.
- PluginMetadata and API versioning (PLUGIN_API_VERSION = 5, exact-equality enforcement at registry init and JAR load, silent skip on mismatch) :: With compile-time-only linking, every driver is compiled against the same `DriverAPI` headers in the same build, so a runtime version mismatch is structurally impossible. Keeping the runtime check as-is would be dead code whose only possible effect is catastrophic: a shipped driver silently vanishing from the driver list with nothing but a log line. Android already demonstrates the failure mode — the shipped example plugin's manifest declares apiVersion 1 against a constant of 5 and would be rejected today, and the architecture doc still says 2. :: REC: Option 2, plus keep the semantic rules unchanged (bump on breaking changes to the driver protocol, capability protocols, context members, event cases, or descriptor cases; no bump for additive changes) and keep the documented changelog. Reset the constant deliberately for the iOS SDK rather than inheriting 5 — and do NOT carry over the doc drift (docs say 2, code says 5, example manifest says 1) or the missing entries (docs omit BOLUS_CATEGORY_PROVIDER, `getFullHistoryLogs`, PairingProfile/PairingFault, the detail-screen API). Keep the `protocolName` + `version` display convention ('Tandem v1.0.0') and the rule that the driver's `version` doubles as its protocol version. Keep the driver-id regex `^[a-zA-Z][a-zA-Z0-9._-]{1,127}$` because it still guards UserDefaults suite names and Keychain service names.
- Nightscout background scheduling (WorkManager: 15-minute unique periodic work + one-shot REPLACE, NetworkType.CONNECTED constraint, exponential retry backoff, stand-down when no backend) :: iOS has nothing that guarantees a 15-minute cadence, and no foreground service to fall back on. `BGAppRefreshTask`'s `earliestBeginDate` is a hint the scheduler is free to ignore; actual delivery is driven by usage patterns and can be zero if the user rarely opens the app, if Background App Refresh is off, or if Low Power Mode is on. `BGProcessingTask` gets minutes instead of ~30 seconds but typically wants the device idle and charging, so it is even less predictable. Silent push (`content-available`) is the only server-driven option and is itself throttled by iOS and suppressed in Low Power Mode — and it requires the backend to hold APNs credentials scoped to this app's Team ID, which a self-hoster cannot obtain. Live Activities are a display surface and grant no background execution; they cannot be used to keep the sync loop alive. :: REC: Option 2 as the baseline, with option 3's push trigger designed for but gated behind an explicit operator capability (see the one PM question). Concretely: register a `BGAppRefreshTaskRequest` with `earliestBeginDate = now + 15min` and reschedule at the START of every handler run (a handler that forgets to reschedule permanently kills the cadence); sync on scene-active; sync opportunistically on any background BLE wake; expose an explicit 'Sync now'. Preserve the Android semantics that still apply: the enable flag persists across a backend stand-down so sync resumes when a backend reappears; `disable()` cancels the schedule but retains cached data; the stand-down cancels any orphaned schedule left from a previous full-stack life. Replace the network constraint with an in-handler reachability check plus `waitsForConnectivity` on the URLSession config. Replace WorkManager's `Result.retry()` + exponential backoff with an explicit reschedule-sooner on transient outcomes. Info.plist this forces: `UIBackgroundModes: fetch` (and `processing` if a longer backfill task is added), plus `BGTaskSchedulerPermittedIdentifiers` listing every task id — a task id missing from that array throws at registration. Most important product requirement: the UI must never imply freshness the platform did not deliver. The existing design already carries this correctly (last-sync timestamp, status badges, and a freshness policy keyed on the reading's capture timestamp), and that must be treated as load-bearing on iOS rather than cosmetic. SIMULATOR: BGTaskScheduler does not fire on its own schedule in the Simulator (or dependably on device); trigger handlers via the debugger's `_simulateLaunchForTaskWithIdentifier:` to validate correctness, and use a real device to observe cadence. `xcrun simctl push` can deliver a silent push to the Simulator, so the push-triggered path is testable in shape but not in throttling behavior.
- NightscoutSyncWorker execution shell (CoroutineWorker.doWork, cancellation rethrown, Transient->retry, never returns failure) :: A `BGAppRefreshTask` handler gets roughly 30 seconds of wall clock and is killed hard if it does not call `setTaskCompleted(success:)` in time — and repeated expirations cause iOS to throttle future scheduling for the app. The Android paging loop it wraps has NO iteration cap and no timeout ('the whole sync runs inside doWork with no explicit timeout'), which is survivable under WorkManager and is not survivable here. A full backfill from cursor 0 over 500-row pages can easily exceed 30 seconds. :: REC: Options 2 and 3 together. Change the engine to persist the cursor after every page (Android persists only on loop exit and on the stall guard), add both a deadline and an iteration cap to the loop, and wire `task.expirationHandler` to cancel the sync task cooperatively — Swift's cooperative cancellation maps directly onto Android's rethrow-CancellationException rule, and cancellation must never be swallowed into a retry. Preserve the outcome mapping's intent: `Disabled`, `NoBackend`, `NoConnection`, `Success` and `AuthError` are all terminal for the run (retrying cannot help until the user acts) and only `Transient` requests a sooner reschedule; always call `setTaskCompleted` on every path. Route a first-run full backfill to the foreground or a `BGProcessingTask` so it is never attempted inside a 30-second window.
- Self-hosted backend reachability for the Nightscout source (App Transport Security and Local Network privacy) :: Verified in the Android repo: `network_security_config.xml` sets `cleartextTrafficPermitted="true"` in base-config specifically so users can point the app at a self-hosted LAN backend over plain HTTP (the app even has an 'insecure-HTTP indicator' for this case). iOS blocks that by default under ATS, and separately, since iOS 14, ANY connection to a local-network address — including a bare `http://192.168.1.x:8000` — triggers the Local Network privacy prompt and requires `NSLocalNetworkUsageDescription`, or the connection silently fails. Neither restriction has an Android equivalent, and both land squarely on the Nightscout source's ability to reach its backend in the self-hosted and backend-optional modes this project targets. :: REC: Option 2. It preserves the Android capability (LAN self-hosting over HTTP) with the narrowest possible exception, and it maps cleanly onto the app's existing insecure-HTTP indicator, which should be carried over so the user can see when their connection is unencrypted. Anything not on the local network must be HTTPS — document that plainly for self-hosters and point at the standard remedies (a reverse proxy with a real certificate, or a private overlay network). Also preserve `isBackendConfigured()` (non-blank base URL) as the single switch for backend-optional local mode, and preserve every behavior it gates: the sync engine's `NoBackend` terminal, status-free outcome; the manager's stand-down that cancels orphaned schedules; and the Settings rule that hides the Nightscout activation control entirely when no backend is configured unless it is already active. SIMULATOR: the Local Network privacy prompt does NOT appear in the iOS Simulator (it uses the Mac's network stack), so a LAN backend that works perfectly in the Simulator can fail on the first real device with no obvious error. This must be on the device test matrix explicitly.
- Settings > Plugins UI (Available Plugins list, activate/deactivate with confirmation, Custom Plugins card with install/remove and trust warning) :: The Available Plugins half ports directly; the Custom Plugins half has nothing to render because runtime plugin installation is impossible on iOS. Leaving an empty or disabled card would be worse than removing it. :: REC: Option 2. Keep everything else verbatim: the derived `visibleAvailablePlugins` rule (computed in the state type so it can never go stale, hiding the Nightscout activation control in BLE-only mode unless already active), name + version per row, activate/deactivate buttons, the mandatory deactivate confirmation naming the consequences ('may stop glucose monitoring, insulin tracking, or other services it provides'), and the pump pairing card shown whenever any pump driver is active with only the active driver's InfoText notes surfaced (never its interactive settings, since the pump card owns live pairing status and pair/unpair). The 'not verified by GlycemicGPT / only install from sources you trust' warning goes away with the mechanism it warned about — every driver in an iOS build is signed and reviewed as part of the app.
- Legacy adapters (PumpDriverAdapter, PumpConnectionAdapter, PumpScannerAdapter, HistoryLogParserAdapter bridging the registry onto four deprecated pump interfaces) :: These exist solely as Android migration scaffolding — they let pre-plugin consumers compile unchanged against interfaces already marked @Deprecated. A greenfield Swift app has no such consumers, so porting them would import dead abstraction plus two live defects. :: REC: Option 2, and deliberately do not recreate the two defects they carry. First, `HistoryLogParserAdapter` returns an empty list rather than an error when no driver is active, so a caller cannot distinguish 'the patient had no boluses' from 'no driver is installed' — on iOS make 'no active driver' a distinct typed error on every capability read (Android's `NoActivePluginException` already exists for the other adapters and should be the uniform answer). Second, `PumpDriverAdapter.getCgmStatus` reads the glucose-source slot while every other read goes through the pump slot, which silently mixes two different drivers when a CGM-only driver runs alongside a pump — make the slot each read targets explicit at the call site. Do port the useful semantics: connection state that follows the active pump driver reactively, an empty scan stream when no driver is active, and cancellation propagating rather than being converted to a failure.
- Community contributor path (compile-time Gradle module PR, plus the runtime JAR path and the plugins/example reference plugin) :: The endorsed compile-time path translates almost directly (a driver becomes an SPM package + one line in the registration array + a PR), but the runtime path is gone, and with it the ability for a non-developer to try a community driver. On iOS, running an unshipped driver requires cloning the repo, opening Xcode, and signing with your own Apple ID (a free account yields a 7-day provisioning profile; a paid account, one year). That is a materially higher bar than `adb push` into files/plugins, and it must be stated rather than glossed. The reference example plugin also cannot exist as a loadable artifact. :: REC: Options 1 and 3 for v1; treat option 2 as a later exploration and be honest about its ceiling — a descriptor language cannot express Tandem's or Medtronic's authenticated handshakes (JPAKE, pairing codes, sequence-cursored history logs), so it would only ever cover trivial notify-only meters and is not a substitute for the runtime path. Option 3 is the important one and is not merely a consolation prize: because Core Bluetooth does not exist in the iOS Simulator, a compiled-in `SimulatedDriver` — the direct descendant of Android's DemoGlucometerPlugin, declaring the same deliberately non-conflicting multi-instance capabilities (BGM_SOURCE + DATA_SYNC), publishing the same events, exercising every card element and every lifecycle hook, and validating each fabricated reading against the live safety limits re-read on every iteration — is what makes the entire Simulator development loop possible. Port its simulator math (base 130 + 50·sin(phase), ±10 jitter, 2π/12 phase step, clamp to 20...500, 12-reading history, 30s default interval with a 5–300s slider at step 5) and, critically, its drop-out-of-range-with-a-log behavior. Gate it behind the feature gate so it can be excluded from production builds while remaining available in TestFlight if desired. Contributor docs must also cover what Android's do not: the required Info.plist keys and background modes a new BLE driver forces, and the fact that a driver's BLE code cannot be tested in the Simulator at all.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (17)
- GLUCOSE VALIDATION BOUNDS — 20..500 mg/dL, enforced in FIVE independent places that must all be reproduced: CgmReading init `require(glucoseMgDl in MIN_MG_DL..MAX_MG_DL)` (20/500); BgmReading init `require(glucoseMgDl in 20..500)`; PluginEvent.CalibrationRequested init `require(bgValueMgDl in 20..500)`; CalibrationTarget.validateBgValue() returning Result.failure outside ABSOLUTE_MIN_GLUCOSE..ABSOLUTE_MAX_GLUCOSE; NightscoutDataMapper.GLUCOSE_RANGE filter. Constructing an out-of-range reading THROWS — so a port that silently clamps instead of throwing changes failure semantics from 'driver crashes loudly' to 'wrong number on a glucose chart'.
- SAFETY LIMITS ARE NARROW-ONLY, NEVER WIDENABLE — SafetyLimits.init hard-requires min>=20, max<=500, maxBasal in 1..15_000 mU/hr, maxBolus in 1..25_000 mU. `safeOf()` clamps rather than throws and is the ONLY path used for backend-supplied values (SafetyLimitsStore.updateAll clamps before persisting AND toSafetyLimits() clamps again on read). A port must preserve both the strict constructor (programmatic callers) and the lenient clamping factory (external data), and must never allow a backend value to widen the absolute bounds.
- DROP, NEVER CLAMP — PumpStatus.extractCgmFromHistoryLogs / extractBolusesFromHistoryLogs / extractBasalFromHistoryLogs and InsulinSource.getBolusHistory all receive SafetyLimits and are contractually required to DISCARD out-of-range records, not clamp them. This is convention + unit tests only; the interfaces do not enforce it. Clamping would silently fabricate a plausible-looking dose or glucose value.
- INSULIN DOSE DISPLAY BOUNDS — BolusEvent.MAX_BOLUS_UNITS = 25f is a hard `require` on units, correctionUnits and mealUnits (all must be >=0 and <=25). BasalReading has no init guard; the only basal ceiling is 15 U/hr, applied at the Nightscout ingestion boundary via ABSOLUTE_MAX_BASAL_MILLIUNITS/1000f. A ported BasalReading with no ceiling would accept an arbitrary rate from a driver.
- UNIT CONVERSION — the ONLY conversion in this subsystem is milliunits→units, `SafetyLimits.ABSOLUTE_MAX_BASAL_MILLIUNITS / 1000f` = 15.0 U/hr in NightscoutDataMapper. SafetyLimits speaks MILLIUNITS (15_000 / 25_000); BolusEvent/BasalReading speak FLOAT UNITS (25f / 15f). Mixing the two by a factor of 1000 in a port would either reject everything or accept a 25,000 U bolus. Glucose is mg/dL end-to-end with NO conversion anywhere in this subsystem.
- NightscoutDataMapper drops NaN/Infinity implicitly — `it.units in 0f..MAX_BASAL_RATE_U_HR` is false for NaN and Infinity because out-of-range float comparisons are false. A Swift port using `abs()`/optional comparisons must add explicit `isFinite` checks or NaN will pass.
- combo_bolus IS DELIBERATELY EXCLUDED from Nightscout bolus ingestion — including it would count an AAPS extendedEmulated temp-basal's extended portion as a point-in-time dose, INFLATING bolus totals and IoB. Reproducing the mapper without this exclusion directly corrupts insulin totals shown to the patient.
- `correction` events are forced to isAutomated = true regardless of the upload's flag (a correction is by definition an automated Control-IQ-style correction in the platform taxonomy). Getting this wrong misattributes automated insulin as user-delivered.
- STALENESS IS TIMESTAMP-BASED AND SOURCE-AGNOSTIC — NightscoutDataMapper stores the reading's REAL capture timestamp (`readingTimestamp`), NOT the sync/fetch time. This is pinned by NightscoutCgmFreshnessTest and is what makes a frozen Nightscout feed age out identically to a frozen BLE feed. FreshnessPolicy.CGM = staleAfterMs 6 min, tooStaleAfterMs 15 min (half-open bounds); the on-device alert floor fires ONLY on FRESH. Stamping sync time instead would make a dead feed look permanently live.
- EVENT BUS HAS NO DELIVERY GUARANTEE — MutableSharedFlow(extraBufferCapacity = 256, onBufferOverflow = DROP_OLDEST, replay = 0). Overflow silently drops the OLDEST event with only a Timber warning; late subscribers see nothing. A CalibrationRequested event can be dropped. Nothing safety-critical currently depends on the bus, but any port that routes alerts or calibration through it inherits an at-most-once, lossy channel.
- SafetyLimitsChanged is PLATFORM-ONLY — PluginEventBusImpl.publish() silently drops it (warning only, no exception) when a plugin attempts it; only the `internal` publishPlatform() can emit it. A plugin able to forge SafetyLimitsChanged could convince other plugins to accept out-of-range data.
- NO THERAPEUTIC WRITE SURFACE — stated in three places (docs, CONTRIBUTING.md, MedtronicDevicePlugin header) as a deliberate architectural decision: no bolus dosing, no basal-rate change, no pump-setting write. calibrate()/unpair()/autoReconnectIfPaired()/connect()/disconnect() are classified as session/lifecycle, not therapy. Capability enforcement at the registry boundary (refusing plugins declaring capabilities outside the official enum) is documented as PLANNED, NOT IMPLEMENTED — today a runtime plugin's declared capability set is trusted.
- RUNTIME PLUGIN SANDBOX IS IN-PROCESS ONLY — DexClassLoader with the app's classloader as parent means a runtime plugin can reflect over host app classes; RestrictedContext is a language-level ContextWrapper, not process isolation. Documented and accepted. Runtime plugin credentials use PLAIN SharedPreferences (not EncryptedSharedPreferences) because plugins cannot access AndroidX Security — readable via run-as on rooted/debug devices, documented as accepted for the current trust model.
- HistoryLogParserAdapter returns emptyList() (not an error) when no plugin is active — a caller cannot distinguish 'no history records' from 'no driver installed'. In a port this must not be mistaken for 'the patient had no boluses'.
- PHI LOGGING RULE — NightscoutSyncEngine logs row counts and HTTP status ONLY, never glucose/insulin values or connection credentials. DebugLogger's interface doc requires raw packet data (cargoHex, parsedValue — which may contain glucose readings, insulin doses, device identifiers) be retained only in debug builds and never persisted unencrypted.
- Cross-source write collisions are resolved by DIFFERENT conflict strategies per table: cgm_readings and basal_readings use IGNORE (first writer wins), bolus_events uses REPLACE (last writer wins). A port that unifies these changes which value the patient sees when a Nightscout row and a BLE row land on the same timestamp.
- Nightscout ingestion filters against ABSOLUTE bounds (20..500, 0..25 U, 0..15 U/hr) and NOT against the user's narrower configured SafetyLimits — a deliberate asymmetry with the BLE extract* path, which does receive the user's limits.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (34)
  Three-layer module topology and dual discovery | Plugin interface and lifecycle contract | DevicePlugin (hardware) contract | PluginMetadata and API versioning | PluginCapability enum and mutual exclusion | Capability interfaces (the read-only SDK surface) | PluginRegistry — startup, state flows, runtime install/remove | PluginFeatureGate (per-plugin kill switch) | PluginPreferences (activation persistence) | PluginSettingsStore (per-plugin key-value persistence) | PluginContext (what the platform hands a plugin) | DexPluginLoader (runtime JAR discovery and loading) | PluginManifest (META-INF/plugin.json format and parsing) | PluginFileManager (JAR install/remove on disk) | RestrictedPluginContext / RestrictedContext sandbox | ScopedCredentialProvider (per-runtime-plugin credential isolation) | PluginEventBus (cross-plugin event bus) | Declarative settings UI descriptors | Declarative dashboard card + detail screen descriptors | Dashboard card renderer (platform-side) | Settings renderer and plugin detail screen | Settings > Plugins UI (activation, custom plugin install/remove) | Legacy adapters (plugin registry → deprecated pump interfaces) | SafetyLimits: definition, clamping, and distribution to plugins | Nightscout source plugin (cloud-mediated DATA_SYNC plugin) | NightscoutSyncManager (WorkManager scheduling) | NightscoutSyncEngine (pull, paging, cursor, outcome classification) | NightscoutSyncStore (typed plugin persistence + reactive state) | NightscoutSyncWorker (WorkManager shell) | NightscoutDataMapper (ingestion validation and source attribution) | Nightscout DTOs and backend endpoints | Plugin declaration sync to backend (bolus categories) | Community contributor path (what shipping a driver requires today) | Shipped reference drivers (what a real driver looks like)


================================================================================
## Data layer, sync, and background execution  [data-and-background]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (42)
- All pump, CGM, bolus, basal, battery, reservoir, alert, raw-history and outbound-queue data must be stored in a single encrypted local database that remains readable while the device is locked, so that background monitoring, alerting and upload continue after the screen turns off.
- The database encryption key must be generated once on first run, be readable only by this app on this device and only after the device has been unlocked at least once since boot, and the app must refuse to open the database rather than mint a second key and orphan existing data.
- A fresh installation must not inherit auth tokens, pump pairing secrets or database keys left behind in the system keychain by a previous installation of the same app.
- The local store must reject duplicates at write time: at most one CGM reading per timestamp, one basal reading per timestamp, one bolus per (units, timestamp) pair, and one raw-history record per pump sequence number.
- When a cloud-sourced record and a pump-sourced record collide on the same timestamp, resolution must be deterministic and documented: first writer wins for CGM and basal, last writer wins for bolus.
- Stored rows that violate current safety invariants — glucose outside 20–500 mg/dL, bolus units outside 0–25 U — must be skipped when read and never displayed, uploaded, or used for alerting.
- Glucose must be stored, transported, compared and threshold-tested in mg/dL everywhere; mmol/L must exist only as a display conversion using the single canonical constant, converted once from the most precise mg/dL value and rounded to one decimal last, with a dot decimal separator regardless of the device's locale.
- The user must be able to choose a data-retention window between 1 and 30 days, defaulting to 7, and the app must enforce it without the user having to open any particular screen.
- Retention must bound the alert history and the raw pump-history table as well as the six pump-data tables, so no table can grow without limit.
- Every uploadable pump event must be durably recorded locally before any upload is attempted, and must survive app suspension, app termination and device restart until the backend accepts it or retention drops it.
- An upload that begins while the app is in the foreground must be able to complete after the user backgrounds the app, without requiring the app to keep running.
- Upload failures must be classified so that transport failures and HTTP 408, 429, 502, 503 and 504 never consume an event's retry budget, while other non-2xx responses do consume it so that a permanently rejected batch is eventually dropped rather than blocking every newer event behind it.
- The outbound queue must be bounded, and when the bound is exceeded the oldest undelivered events must be dropped; the bound must be sized so that a full day without a successful upload does not discard data.
- The user must be able to see how many events are waiting to upload and when the last successful upload happened, and that pending count must not be misleading while an upload is in flight.
- Removing the server URL must leave a fully functional local-only monitor: no undelivered upload rows are retained, no backend request is attempted, local alert thresholds can be set on the device, and the on-device alert floor is the sole alerting path and is described as such.
- Adding a server URL must resume syncing without requiring an app restart, and signing out must preserve both the server URL and the queued events so they drain on the next successful sign-in.
- The app must attempt queue drain, retention, alert-threshold refresh and cloud-source sync at every execution opportunity the system grants it — app activation, Bluetooth wake, scheduled background refresh, background-upload completion — rather than on a fixed interval, and retry eligibility must be computed from stored timestamps so that an irregular schedule delays delivery without losing or corrupting an event.
- The app must state, both inside the app and on a Lock Screen or Home Screen surface, whether anything is currently watching for lows and highs, and that statement must fail toward 'not watching' rather than going stale, optimistic, or silent.
- The app must never claim background coverage it cannot deliver: when monitoring has stopped because the iPhone was restarted, the app was force-quit, Bluetooth is off, Local Network access was denied, or notifications are not authorized, the user must be told which one and what to do about it.
- Onboarding must tell the user explicitly that restarting the iPhone or force-quitting the app stops monitoring until the app is opened again, and that background data collection is best-effort and may miss readings.
- The initial multi-month pump history download must run as a user-visible, cancellable, resumable foreground task with accurate progress, resuming from the last downloaded record rather than restarting, and must keep the screen awake while it runs.
- The user must stay signed in across app restarts; the access token must be refreshed before it expires whenever the app has execution time, and specifically before any upload is handed to the system for deferred delivery.
- A 401 response must trigger exactly one serialized refresh-and-retry shared across all concurrent requests, so a refresh token is never rotated twice concurrently.
- Signing out must always win over any in-flight token refresh; a refresh that completes after sign-out must never restore a session.
- A session that expires while offline must show 'session expired, sign in again' without erasing the server URL, the user's email address, or any local pump history, and must not block the user from viewing local data or the on-device alert floor from arming.
- The app must refuse plaintext HTTP to any host that is not a literal loopback, private, carrier-NAT or link-local IP address or a .local name, enforced both when the URL is saved and again on every request, classifying by literal address only and never by DNS resolution.
- Reaching a server on the local network must request Local Network access, and a denial must be surfaced to the user as a permission problem with a route to Settings, never as a generic connection failure.
- The user must be able to opt in to insecure LAN HTTP explicitly; that choice is per-device and must survive sign-out. Using a private certificate authority over https must be documented as the preferred alternative.
- The app must report three distinct connectivity states — device offline, backend unreachable, reachable — with device-offline taking precedence, flipping to unreachable only after two consecutive transport failures, and excluding both cancelled requests and long-running AI inference calls from that signal.
- The app must be able to sound an urgent-low or urgent-high alarm while the iPhone is silenced or in a Focus mode; if that capability has not been granted, the app must say plainly that alarms will not sound while the phone is silenced rather than presenting itself as armed.
- The on-device alert floor must never alarm off a reading whose own sensor timestamp is older than the freshness bound, off thresholds that were never configured, or while the device clock has moved backwards; the same freshness predicate must gate both the phone alarm and the wrist relay, with no second definition anywhere.
- A backend response carrying alert thresholds, a target glucose range, or safety limits that fails validation must be dropped, never clamped, leaving the last known-good values in place; ordering must be validated on the raw server values before any rounding.
- The user must be able to acknowledge an alert with no network connection; the acknowledgement must stick locally, silence the alarm immediately, and be reconciled with the server exactly once when connectivity returns, and a re-delivered server alert must never re-alarm something the user already acknowledged offline.
- An ongoing low or high must continue to reach the wrist until the episode ends or is acknowledged, and must never be retracted to an 'all clear' on data the app cannot vouch for.
- The Apple Watch app must show current glucose, trend, time since the last reading, insulin on board, and the same coverage claim the iPhone shows, and must age its own copy out on its own clock rather than presenting an unbounded-stale value as current.
- The Apple Watch app must ship in the same build as the iPhone app; there must be no separate watch update, watch install, or watch face installation flow.
- The app must tell the user when a newer build is available and route them to the distribution channel to install it, and must never download or install executable code itself; the app must also surface its own build expiry so the user updates before it stops launching.
- The app must never issue any command that changes pump therapy; the pump interface must expose read-only status queries only, and this must be enforced by the module's public interface, not by convention.
- The app must include a compile-time-linked simulated pump driver and an in-app fault-injection surface — inject a CGM reading, force the backend unreachable, accelerate staleness — so that persistence, sync, retry classification, alert floor, wrist relay and the coverage claim can all be exercised in the iOS Simulator with no physical pump present.
- Release builds must offer a user-accessible diagnostic log export, scrubbed of tokens, email addresses and glucose values, because the platform gives users no way to read the app's system log.
- The app must tolerate unknown fields in every backend response and must apply the documented default for every absent optional field rather than failing to decode the response, and must accept backend timestamps with or without fractional seconds.
- The app must register itself with the backend as an iOS device with a stable per-installation identifier and deregister on sign-out; registration failure must never block use of the app.

### IMPOSSIBLE (5)
- PumpConnectionService — a foreground service with a persistent ongoing notification, a PARTIAL_WAKE_LOCK, and START_STICKY restart :: iOS has no foreground services, no user-dismissable-but-persistent ongoing notification tied to a running process, no wake locks, and no mechanism for the system to restart a killed app. An app is suspended within seconds of leaving the foreground and executes only when the system wakes it. There is no API — public or entitled — that keeps an iOS app running continuously. The battery-driven policy is equally unavailable: ACTION_BATTERY_CHANGED has no background analog, and UIDevice.batteryLevel only updates while the app runs. :: ALT: Three surfaces together, none of which is permanent. (1) Core Bluetooth's bluetooth-central background mode with CBCentralManagerOptionRestoreIdentifierKey state restoration keeps the CONNECTION alive across suspension and lets iOS relaunch the app into the background for peripheral events — this replaces the wake lock's actual purpose (the system holds the link instead of the app holding the CPU) and is in one respect better than Android. (2) For the honest-coverage notification text: a Live Activity (ActivityKit) on the Lock Screen and Dynamic Island, updated from the app whenever it has execution time — but it is capped at roughly 8 hours active (plus a few hours lingering in Notification Center), is user-dismissable, and cannot be recreated without the app running, so it cannot be the permanent honesty surface the Android notification is. (3) A Home Screen / Lock Screen WidgetKit widget showing last reading, its age, and the coverage claim, reloaded via WidgetCenter.reloadTimelines on every execution opportunity; unattended timeline refreshes are budgeted (roughly tens per day) but a widget that shows a timestamp degrades honestly on its own. Replace the battery policy with ProcessInfo.isLowPowerModeEnabled. Do NOT use the audio background mode or a silent-audio keepalive to stay running — Apple rejects it and it would fail Beta App Review.
- BootCompletedReceiver — automatic restart after device reboot :: iOS has no launch-at-boot for third-party apps and no broadcast an app can subscribe to. Apple's documented rule for Core Bluetooth state restoration is that the system does not relaunch an app the user force-quit, and after a device restart the app must be launched by the user before restoration applies. There is no code path by which the app can notice a reboot happened, because no code runs. :: ALT: Core Bluetooth state restoration covers the case the Android receiver does NOT cover — relaunch after the system terminates the app to reclaim memory — but not reboot or force-quit. The only honest mitigations are product ones: tell the user during onboarding that monitoring stops after a restart until the app is opened; keep a Home Screen widget whose displayed data age makes a stopped app visibly obvious; and, when the app does launch, detect a long gap since the last recorded reading and surface it. If a backend and push are adopted, a server-side watchdog noticing that a device stopped pushing could send a push that relaunches the app into the background — that is the only mechanism that can restart monitoring without the user, and it requires the push infrastructure the backend does not currently have.
- Persistent server-alert stream (AlertStreamService's foreground-service SSE connection) :: A long-lived HTTP connection cannot be held open by a suspended iOS app, and there is no background mode that permits it. URLSession background configurations support only upload and download tasks — not streaming data tasks — so SSE cannot be moved there either. Verified against the repo: contract/openapi.json contains no APNs, FCM or push-token endpoint of any kind; server alerts reach the device only via GET /api/v1/alerts/stream or GET /api/v1/alerts/pending. So today there is no mechanism by which a backgrounded iPhone can receive a server-generated alert. :: ALT: Apple Push Notification service is the only path, and it requires a new backend capability (an APNs sender) plus an APNs auth key scoped to the app's bundle identifier. Without push: run the SSE stream only while the app is foregrounded, pull /api/v1/alerts/pending at every execution opportunity, and treat the on-device alert floor as the sole background alerting mechanism — which raises the stakes on the Critical Alerts entitlement considerably. Note that background alert PULLING is also weak: BGAppRefreshTask cadence is opportunistic, so a pull-only design cannot promise timely delivery of a server alert.
- In-app update checking and APK self-install (AppUpdateChecker, REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES, FileProvider install intent) :: iOS forbids an app from downloading and executing or installing code. There is no FileProvider install intent, no ACTION_VIEW for a package, and no sideload path within a TestFlight-distributed app. The entire download/size-check/host-allowlist/sanitize/install pipeline has no target. :: ALT: Keep the GitHub release check (the version comparison, the channel selection, the rate-limit and 404 error mapping are all still useful) but end at an informational banner that deep-links to TestFlight via itms-beta:// or the public TestFlight invitation URL, where the user installs the update. The https-in-code and host-allowlist hardening becomes unnecessary because no executable is fetched. Add a TestFlight-specific concern Android does not have: TestFlight builds expire 90 days after upload, so the app should surface its own expiry date and prompt for an update before it stops launching.
- Wear APK update checking, download and push to the watch (WearAppUpdateChecker, WearApkPusher, WatchFaceInstaller) :: watchOS has no sideloading. A watchOS app is a target inside the iOS app bundle and is installed by the system through the Watch app; there is no API by which the iPhone app can transfer and install an executable on the paired Watch. The watchface APK push is doubly impossible because watchOS has no third-party custom watch faces at all. :: ALT: Nothing is needed — the capability dissolves. The Watch app ships in the same TestFlight build as the iPhone app and installs automatically (or via the Watch app's toggle), so watch and phone versions cannot diverge and the entire channel/version/dev-build-number comparison machinery, the 100 MB cap, the push flow and the optimistic 15-second 'Success' state are all deleted. The watch-face capability's closest substitute is WidgetKit complications on Apple's system faces plus Smart Stack widgets, configured from the iPhone app — this is another subsystem's problem, but the watchface_* settings keys in AppSettingsStore should be repurposed to complication/widget configuration rather than ported.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (8)
- BackendSyncManager — the 3-second queue drain loop, stand-down, and hygiene :: The entire lifecycle is a coroutine loop that lives inside a foreground service and wakes every 3 seconds forever. iOS has no foreground service and suspends the app within seconds of backgrounding; no timer, loop or delay executes while suspended. A push started in the foreground is also killed at suspension unless it is on a background URLSession. Separately, the retry timings (2s/4s/8s/16s/32s, budget exhausted in ~62s) and the 60-second stale-sending reclaim assume a drain opportunity every few seconds, which iOS will not provide. :: REC: Do all three of the first options together. Foreground: run the existing 3s loop unchanged so the behaviour the user sees while using the app is identical to Android. Background: replace the loop with an opportunity-driven drain triggered by app activation, every Core Bluetooth wake, BGAppRefreshTask and BGProcessingTask, and push over a background URLSession with the batch written to a temp file. Keep every piece of the queue's logic byte-identical — the retry classification (408/429/502-504 and transport failures do not increment retryCount), markTransientFailure, resetStaleSending, prune-oldest-at-cap, and the hourly cleanup — because that logic is opportunity-agnostic. Two parameter changes are required: raise MAX_QUEUE_SIZE well above 5000 (at the 15s fast cadence the enqueue rate is ~8 rows/min, so 5000 rows is ~10 hours, and an iPhone that cannot drain for longer than that would silently discard the oldest pump history), and lengthen the stale-sending reclaim beyond 60 seconds since a background-URLSession push legitimately takes longer than that. Keep the reactive backend-configured lifecycle (configured → drain, unconfigured → hourly stand-down purge) driven by the same in-process signal; on iOS the stand-down sweep runs on the same opportunity schedule rather than an hourly timer.
- PumpPollingOrchestrator — poll cadences, keep-alive, and history backfill :: Three concurrent forever-loops at 15s / 5min / 5min, all inside a foreground service holding a wake lock. On iOS: no wake lock exists, no timer fires while suspended, and the 15-second cadence exists specifically because the pump drops idle BLE connections at ~30 seconds — which means a suspended iOS app will lose the link and be dependent on reconnection. The backfill deadlines (2 minutes normal, 20 minutes initial sync) exceed every background execution window iOS offers; BGProcessingTask can run for minutes but only when the system chooses, typically while charging and idle. :: REC: Foreground behaviour is identical to Android — keep all cadence constants, the 500ms request stagger, the 3x low-power multiplier, and the loop composition. Background behaviour must be redesigned around Core Bluetooth as the clock: adopt state restoration and a permanently pending connect(), and run the fast-loop poll burst inside each callback window. Move the low-battery multiplier off ACTION_BATTERY_CHANGED (which has no iOS analog while backgrounded) onto ProcessInfo.isLowPowerModeEnabled and its change notification — a better signal anyway, since it is the user's explicit statement of intent. Drop the wake-lock and the <5%-battery release entirely; there is nothing to release. Run the initial history backfill as a foreground task with a progress UI and idle timer disabled, backed by BGContinuedProcessingTask so it survives a brief backgrounding, and keep the anti-stall guard, the sequence anchor, and the 1-second inter-batch stagger unchanged. State plainly in the PRD that background poll cadence is best-effort and that a suspended iPhone may miss readings the Android build would have captured.
- AuthManager — proactive scheduled refresh and the refresh state machine :: The state machine, the single refresh mutex shared by the proactive timer and the reactive 401 path, the sessionGeneration logout-race guard, and the five-outcome taxonomy are pure logic that port perfectly (and a Swift actor is a better fit than Mutex + @Volatile). What does not port is the scheduling: scheduleRefresh computes a delay to five minutes before expiry and sleeps on an app-lifetime scope. On iOS that Task cannot run while the app is suspended, so a token expiring overnight is never proactively refreshed, and the TRANSIENT_RETRY_DELAY_MS = 60_000 reschedule loop is equally dead. :: REC: Port the state machine, the actor-serialized refresh, the generation counter, the outcome taxonomy and every documented invariant unchanged (Expired preserves the store, Rejected clears the token, Discarded touches nothing, ServerError/NetworkFailure preserve tokens and keep the session Authenticated on a stale token rather than showing a false session-expired banner, logout always wins). Replace the timer with opportunity-driven refresh: check isTokenExpiringSoon(5 min) at app activation, on every background execution opportunity, and as a precondition of enqueuing a background upload — the last one matters most, because a background URLSession upload must carry a token that will still be valid when the system decides to run it. Keep the foreground timer as an optimization only. Keep performRefreshWithRetry's 1s/2s backoff and its rule that exhaustion only flips to Expired when there is genuinely no token.
- UrlSecurityPolicy and cleartext transport policy :: The pure classifier (literal-IP-only, no DNS lookup, strict IPv4 octet parsing rejecting octal/hex/decimal encodings, hand-written IPv6 with one :: and %zone stripping, the .local mDNS exception) ports verbatim to Swift. But the enforcement stack around it changes materially and gains a gate Android has no analog for. iOS App Transport Security replaces network_security_config with different expressiveness, and iOS 14+ requires an explicit Local Network privacy grant before an app may contact any host on the local network — a denial produces connection failures that look like a dead server, not a permission error. Additionally, the Android debug affordance of allowing http:// to ANY host is not available in a TestFlight build without NSAllowsArbitraryLoads, which is review-visible. :: REC: Ship NSAllowsLocalNetworking = YES in the release Info.plist and NSAllowsArbitraryLoads only in a debug-configuration Info.plist. Keep the pure policy in Swift, enforced at both save and request time as today, and do not weaken it to rely on ATS — ATS's exact CIDR coverage should be treated as unverified and the in-code classifier as authoritative. Add explicit Local Network permission handling with a purpose string, a pre-flight probe, and a distinct user-facing error ('Local Network access is off for this app') so a denied grant is never reported as a generic network failure. Note one iOS advantage worth stating in the PRD: unlike Android 7+, iOS apps trust user-installed CA certificates by default, so a self-hoster with a private CA can use https:// without any app change — this is a materially better path than the LAN-HTTP opt-in and should be the recommended setup.
- On-device alert floor gates that the data layer feeds :: The classification, freshness policy, wall-clock rewind high-water mark, cooldown, episode guard and ack-gated re-arm are pure logic that port perfectly. Three of the four arming preconditions change meaning on iOS. Gate 1 (server alerting degraded) is derived from the SSE stream's state — but a persistent SSE connection cannot exist while an iOS app is suspended, so in the background the floor would be permanently armed, inverting the primary dedup layer. Gate 4 (POST_NOTIFICATIONS granted) becomes a much sharper question: to actually alarm through silent mode and Focus, iOS requires the Critical Alerts entitlement (com.apple.developer.usernotifications.critical-alerts), which is granted only by an approved request to Apple; without it a low-glucose alarm is silent whenever the ringer is off. And the floor only fires at all if the app is executing, which on iOS means a Core Bluetooth wake happened. :: REC: Port the floor's logic unchanged — same classify order (urgent first), same FreshnessPolicy (CGM 6/15 min, pump 15/60 min, debug 20/45 s), same ALERT_FLOOR_MAX_FUTURE_SKEW_MS = 60_000, same monotonic wall-clock high-water mark suppressing both firing and the watching claim, same 30-minute cooldown mirroring the server's dedup window, same ack-gated episode guard clamped with min(recentServerAlert, now), same fail-toward-alerting on a failed episode lookup. Redefine gate 1 as 'no live server alert channel' and lean harder on the episode guard, which already reads Room's server-alert history and is the mechanism that makes this safe. Apply for the Critical Alerts entitlement as a launch blocker for the alarm feature, ship Time Sensitive as the fallback, and make the app's honesty surface state which one is active — 'alarms will sound even when silenced' vs 'alarms will not sound while your iPhone is silenced'. Note plainly that the floor can only fire while the app has execution time, so a suspended app with no Bluetooth wake produces no floor alarm at all — this is the honest ceiling of iOS background monitoring.
- CGM watch relay and alert-floor hand-off (processCgmReading) :: The relay's decision logic — one classification feeding both consumers, the isReadingAlertable data-trust gate that skips the ENTIRE alert block (no send, no clear, latch untouched), recovery keyed on the classification being in range rather than on a null mapping, the 5-minute silent refresh, the 30-minute rebuzz matching FLOOR_COOLDOWN_MS, and mutex serialization across the poll loop / manual refresh / debug injector — is pure logic and ports perfectly to a Swift actor. What changes is the transport and the meaning of 'buzz the wrist'. Wear DataLayer becomes WatchConnectivity, which has different delivery classes and budgets, and there is no way to make the Watch vibrate from the phone other than a notification. :: REC: Keep the relay's logic verbatim in an actor. Send state (glucose, trend, timestamp, thresholds, unit, coverage claim) via updateApplicationContext, which matches DataItem semantics closely enough that the existing wire contract survives. Deliver the wrist ALERT as an iPhone local notification and let watchOS mirror it — this collapses the Android design's careful three-path deduplication into one path and is strictly simpler, and it also means the wrist alarm inherits whatever critical-alert capability the phone has. Preserve exactly the two properties that make the Android relay safe: a not-alertable reading skips the whole alert block rather than clearing, and recovery is keyed on the classification being in range so a mapping gap can cost a push but never produce a false 'All clear'. Keep watch-side freshness decay so the Watch degrades to 'stale' on its own clock; do not let it render a relayed value as current indefinitely.
- DataRetentionWorker and retention policy :: WorkManager's guaranteed periodic execution has no iOS equivalent. BGProcessingTask is discretionary — the system decides when, typically while charging and idle, and may skip days for an app the user opens rarely. ExistingPeriodicWorkPolicy.KEEP and the HiltWorkerFactory/manual-initialization machinery are Android-only concerns that simply disappear. :: REC: Do the opportunistic version as the primary mechanism and register a BGProcessingTask as a supplement. Keep the 1/7/30-day setting with the same coercion, and keep the single-transaction six-table delete. While porting, close the two observed retention gaps: the alert table's 7-day cleanup must not depend on the user opening the alerts screen, and raw_history_logs must actually be pruned in full-stack mode (the Android cleanup(cutoffMs) has zero production callers), because on iOS an unbounded table competes for a storage budget the system can reclaim under pressure.
- Nightscout cloud-source sync (WorkManager 15-minute periodic + one-shot) :: The engine — connection resolution honouring an explicit selection only while active, 500-per-array paging, the inclusive cursor relying on idempotent inserts for boundary dedup, the min-of-full-streams cursor advance, the saturated-boundary transient throw, the AuthError-does-not-retry classification, and the whole NightscoutDataMapper validation set including the deliberate combo_bolus exclusion — is pure logic that ports verbatim. Only the scheduler changes: WorkManager's 15-minute periodic floor with a NetworkType.CONNECTED constraint has no iOS equivalent with comparable reliability. :: REC: Register a BGAppRefreshTask and additionally run the sync on app foreground and on every background wake. The Android design explicitly documents that a faster source interval never loses data — it only changes freshness — so a sparse iOS schedule degrades gracefully by construction. Keep the per-connection cursor, the paging loop, the cursor-advance rule, the NoBackend backstop for a run racing a mode flip, and the row-count-only logging discipline unchanged. Surface last-sync time and status in the UI so the user can see freshness rather than assuming a 15-minute cadence that iOS will not deliver.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (15)
- GLUCOSE VALIDITY BOUND 20..500 mg/dL — enforced at many independent sites with NO single owner (documented as a known limitation). Sites in this subsystem: `CgmReading.MIN_MG_DL=20`/`MAX_MG_DL=500` (constructor `require`, so an out-of-range value THROWS); `SafetyLimits.DEFAULT_MIN_GLUCOSE=20`/`DEFAULT_MAX_GLUCOSE=500` and `ABSOLUTE_*` aliases; `AlertThresholdStore.requireSafeThresholds` (`it in 20..500`); `AuthRepository.fetchGlucoseRange` (`it in 20..500`) and `fetchSafetyLimits` (off-by-one variants `min in 20..499`, `max in 21..500`); `PumpDao.observeTimeInRangeCounts` SQL `glucoseMgDl BETWEEN 20 AND 500`; `NightscoutDataMapper.GLUCOSE_RANGE`. Any port must replicate ALL of them or consolidate deliberately.
- UNIT CONVERSION — `GlucoseFormat.MGDL_PER_MMOL = 18.0156` is the single canonical client constant (mirrors backend `MGDL_PER_MMOL` and the web value). The documented rule: convert from the most-precise mg/dL source ONCE and round to one decimal LAST, so thresholds land on conventional values (70→3.9, 180→10.0, 120→6.7, 100→5.6). Never introduce 18.02/18.0182. `convertSpread` (offset-free divide for SDs/differences) must NEVER be replaced by `convertValue`. All formatting uses `Locale.US` so the decimal separator is always a dot — required both for cross-surface consistency and so mmol output still matches the release log scrubber's regex. Storage, transport, every threshold comparison, alert detection, and the 20–500 invariant all stay canonical mg/dL; mmol is display-only.
- INSULIN DOSING BOUNDS — `BolusEvent` constructor `require`s `units`, `correctionUnits` and `mealUnits` each in `0..MAX_BOLUS_UNITS = 25f` (Tandem hardware max). `SafetyLimits` caps basal at `ABSOLUTE_MAX_BASAL_MILLIUNITS = 15_000` (15 U/hr) and bolus at `ABSOLUTE_MAX_BOLUS_MILLIUNITS = 25_000` (25 U); `safeOf()` CLAMPS untrusted backend input rather than throwing, and `SafetyLimitsStore.updateAll` clamps before persisting so every read path returns safe values. `PumpProfileStore.updateAll` requires `maxBolusUnits in 0f..25f`. The documented decision: the mobile app NEVER allows local override of safety limits — only backend sync writes them.
- ALERT THRESHOLD INTEGRITY — the on-device alert floor must alarm at EXACTLY the levels the server would, so `AuthRepository.fetchAlertThresholds` DROPS an invalid response rather than clamping, leaving last-known-good (or the never-synced state, which keeps the floor disarmed). Ordering is validated on the RAW SERVER FLOATS because rounding two server-legal values <1 mg/dL apart can collapse them to equal ints and permanently disarm the floor. Rounded ties are safe only because `classify` checks the urgent band first. `AlertThresholdStore` refuses to persist out-of-range or mis-ordered thresholds (fail loudly, keep the old values). Logout clears BACKEND thresholds so the floor can never fire off another account's values; LOCAL device-set thresholds survive.
- ALERT-THRESHOLD vs DISPLAY-RANGE SEPARATION — `AlertThresholdStore` (what fires alarms) and `GlucoseRangeStore` (display target range) share the same default numbers (55/70/180/250) but are semantically distinct stores. A floor firing at display-range values would alarm at the wrong glucose levels and desync from the server. Do not merge them in a port.
- STALENESS DETECTION — one shared classifier, never forked. `FreshnessPolicy.CGM = (stale 6 min, tooStale 15 min)`, `PUMP = (15 min, 60 min)`, debug-only `CGM_DEBUG_FAST = (20 s, 45 s)`. The alert floor and the watch relay BOTH gate on `AlertFloor.isReadingAlertable`, which requires strictly FRESH against the CGM's OWN SENSOR TIMESTAMP (never poll wall-clock — a warmup or signal-loss poll can succeed every 15 s while returning the same old sensor value). Forward skew tolerance `ALERT_FLOOR_MAX_FUTURE_SKEW_MS = 60_000`; backward wall-clock jumps are caught statefully by a monotonic high-water mark (`isWallClockRewound`), which suppresses BOTH firing and the 'watching' claim. A second staleness definition on either path is the silent-floor bug GLY-115/116 exist to prevent.
- ALERT DELIVERY GUARANTEES — a not-alertable CGM reading skips the ENTIRE wrist relay block (no send, no clearAlert, latch untouched): retracting a shown alert off untrustworthy data would flip a possibly-still-true low into a reassuring 'All clear'. Recovery/clearAlert is keyed on the CLASSIFICATION being in range, not on a null watch mapping, so a mapping gap can cost a push but never produce a false 'All clear'. An ongoing episode is silently refreshed every 5 min (keeping the wrist copy inside the 6-min STALE band) and re-buzzed every 30 min (matching `AlertFloor.FLOOR_COOLDOWN_MS`) so the wrist is never quieter than the phone. The relay seam is mutex-serialized because it is reachable concurrently from the poll loop, the Home manual refresh, and the debug injector.
- OFFLINE ALARM SILENCING — `alerts.acknowledged` is set unconditionally at ack time and NEVER depends on the network; `ack_synced` tracks the server POST. `insertPreservingLocalAck` prevents an SSE re-delivery or a pull from downgrading a locally-acknowledged row and re-alarming. `reconcilePendingAcks` is idempotent and triggered on REACHABLE transitions, SSE open, and pull.
- FOREGROUND-NOTIFICATION HONESTY — the pump-service notification is the ONLY place that can state whether anything is watching while the app is backgrounded. `onStartCommand` rebuilds from the CACHED status, never the optimistic default, because a redundant start during an outage would otherwise replace 'NOT watching' with all-is-well copy and the distinctUntilChanged stream would not repair it. `onCreate` seeds from the provider's pessimistic snapshot. The floor-status watcher is wrapped so its failure freezes the text at its last value rather than taking down the pump service, and the status pipeline `retryWhen`s with a pessimistic emission so the claim degrades but never lies or stops.
- SYNC RETRY CLASSIFICATION — mislabeling a transport failure or a 502/503/504/429/408 as a countable failure would exhaust the 5-retry budget ~62 s into a backend outage and let the hourly cleanup DELETE the queue, discarding everything older than a minute instead of draining on reconnect. Conversely, a persistent 500 must keep counting so a poison batch exhausts instead of head-of-line-blocking every row behind it (getPendingBatch is oldest-first).
- ENQUEUE FAILURES MUST NOT KILL POLLING — `SyncQueueEnqueuer.enqueue` catches everything (rethrowing CancellationException) because the poll loops have no catch of their own; a dropped sync row is harmless, a dead poll loop starves the dashboard AND the alert floor.
- MEAL-INTELLIGENCE FAIL-CLOSED — a 401/403 on the meal-intelligence setting forces the local cache to `false` so a cached/default-ON value cannot leave meal surfaces visible for an account the backend rejects.
- CLEARTEXT TRANSPORT — `UrlSecurityPolicy` is the SOLE guard preventing a public host from being reached over plaintext (network-security-config cannot express CIDR ranges). It classifies by literal IP only and NEVER performs a DNS lookup (DNS-rebinding + thread-blocking). Enforced redundantly at save time and at request time. APK downloads additionally enforce https-in-code plus a host allowlist because an APK download is a code-execution path.
- ENCRYPTION AT REST — all pump/glucose/alert history lives in a SQLCipher database whose 32-byte SecureRandom passphrase is stored in Keystore-backed EncryptedSharedPreferences; a failed passphrase persist THROWS rather than silently regenerating a key that would orphan the data. Auth tokens and pump pairing credentials (including JPAKE-derived secrets) live in separate encrypted preference files.
- READ-ONLY BY CONSTRUCTION — `PumpDriver` intentionally exposes NO insulin-delivery, pump-setting or control methods; every method is a read-only status query. This is a project-wide invariant and must be preserved in any port.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (29)
  Encrypted local database (SQLCipher + Room) | Room entities and DAO surface (pump data tables) | Outbound sync queue (sync_queue table + SyncDao) | BackendSyncManager — queue drain loop, stand-down, hygiene | Sync enqueue path (SyncQueueEnqueuer + PumpEventMapper) | Raw pump history log storage and upload | Token storage (AuthTokenStore) | AuthManager — auth state machine and refresh-token rotation | AuthRepository — login/logout and per-account settings reconcile | Retrofit/Moshi backend surface (GlycemicGptApi) | OkHttp client construction and interceptor chain | UrlSecurityPolicy — server URL transport policy | NetworkMonitor — connectivity and backend reachability | PumpConnectionService — foreground service keeping BLE alive | PumpPollingOrchestrator — poll cadences and history backfill | CGM watch relay and alert-floor hand-off (processCgmReading) | On-device alert floor gates that the data layer feeds | Alert persistence and offline acknowledgement reconcile | BootCompletedReceiver — restart after reboot | DataRetentionWorker and retention policy | In-app update checking and APK self-install (phone) | Wear APK update checking and push | Local settings stores (SharedPreferences layer) | Backend-optional local-only (BLE-only) mode | Nightscout cloud-source sync (WorkManager, backend-mediated) | Device registration | Application startup sequence | Logging, scrubbing, and crash reporting posture | Backend contract pinning and compatibility posture


================================================================================
## Wear OS watch face and companion  [wear-surface]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (42)
- The system must ship one iPhone app and one Apple Watch app from a single distributable bundle, such that installing or updating the iPhone app installs or updates the Watch app, with no separate artifact, no sideloading, and no user-performed developer workflow.
- The system must let a user place GlycemicGPT complications on their chosen Apple Watch face and see, at a glance and without opening any app: current blood glucose with trend, insulin on board, a recent-glucose sparkline, an alert/coverage indicator, and an entry point to AI chat.
- The system must provide guided in-app setup that tells the user exactly which complications exist, what each shows, and how to add them to their watch face — and must detect and surface the case where no complication of ours is currently on the active face.
- The system must show current blood glucose on the wrist with a freshness marker: the value plain when the reading is under 6 minutes old, the value with an explicit stale marker from 6 to 15 minutes, and no value at all past 15 minutes — while continuing to show how old the reading is in every case.
- The system must show insulin on board on the wrist with the same three-tier treatment on a pump policy: plain under 15 minutes, marked stale from 15 to 60 minutes, withheld past 60 minutes, never presented as current when it is not.
- The system must make wrist freshness decay happen on the watch's own clock and without requiring any message from the iPhone, so that an iPhone that dies, is force-quit, or loses Bluetooth causes the wrist to degrade to 'no recent data' on schedule rather than freezing on its last reassuring value.
- The system must never show a value on the wrist that is outside 20–500 mg/dL, at any freshness tier, from any source, including values restored from a local cache after a restart.
- The system must convert to mmol/L for display only, using the single factor 18.0156, dividing once and rounding last, with a dot decimal separator regardless of the device locale — and must never let the display unit reach glucose validation, colour banding, threshold comparison, or plotting geometry.
- The system must sanitize incoming thresholds in a dependent order that guarantees 20 ≤ urgent low ≤ low < high ≤ urgent high ≤ 500 for any input, including corrupt or hostile input, and must fall back to 70/180/55/250 when thresholds are absent.
- The system must show a wrist coverage indicator that distinguishes three states — something is currently watching for lows and highs; the phone reported that nothing is watching, with the specific reason; and the phone has not reported recently — and must reserve the quiet 'all clear' presentation exclusively for the first state.
- The system must encode the wrist coverage state so that it is unambiguous in monochrome and under an arbitrary user-chosen face tint, without depending on hue, because watchOS complications render in an accented mode the app does not control.
- The system must render the five specific 'not watching' reasons with distinct user-facing copy — notifications denied, thresholds not synced, thresholds not configured, pump disconnected, no fresh glucose reading — and must have a distinguishable generic fallback for an unrecognised reason.
- The system must have the iPhone advertise its coverage claim with an absolute expiry, and must have the watch decay that claim to 'no recent data' once the expiry passes, clamped so a corrupt or hostile expiry cannot make a dead phone appear covered for more than 30 minutes or force decay in under 10 seconds.
