- The system must not let the watch derive its own coverage claim; the watch renders and decays the phone's claim and never re-computes it.
- The system must not persist the coverage claim across a watch app restart; after a restart, coverage must read as unknown until the phone reports again.
- The system must treat a device clock that has jumped backward by more than 60 seconds as untrustworthy on both honesty axes: coverage reads unknown, feed freshness reads too-stale, and age labels say so rather than claiming 'just now'.
- The system must deliver an alert to the wrist with a haptic even when the iPhone is unlocked and in active use, which requires the alert notification to be scheduled on the Watch itself rather than mirrored from the iPhone.
- The system must be able to break through Silent Mode, Do Not Disturb, and Sleep Focus for urgent low and urgent high alerts, and must degrade to a Time Sensitive alert with an explicit in-app statement of the reduced guarantee if that capability is unavailable.
- The system must re-alarm the wrist on a sustained alert every 30 minutes, scheduled locally on the Watch so the re-alarm continues even if the iPhone stops responding — and must terminate that ladder, with an explicit stale/not-watching notification, once the underlying reading ages past 15 minutes rather than re-alarming indefinitely off a frozen snapshot.
- The system must let the user turn wrist alerts off, must state plainly in that setting that doing so disables all wrist alerting for lows and highs, and must acknowledge that the operating system and the Watch app give the user independent notification controls the app cannot override.
- The system must never retract a shown alert on the basis of data it cannot vouch for: when a reading is not alertable, the alert relay must take no action at all — no send, no clear, no state change — and must let the wrist copy age out on the watch's own clock instead.
- The system must refresh a still-live alert on the wrist at least every 5 minutes so that an ongoing alert never crosses the 6-minute staleness band and gets greyed out while it is still true.
- The system must let the user dismiss an alert from the wrist — from the alert screen and from the notification itself — and must identify the specific alert being dismissed rather than assuming the most recent one.
- The system must acknowledge a wrist dismiss locally before attempting any server call, so the dismiss is truthful even with no backend reachable, and must clear the phone-side latch so the same episode does not immediately re-alarm.
- The system must show a full-screen glucose graph on the Watch with pan-back-in-time and tap-for-value, in full colour, honouring the user's basal-rate, bolus-marker, insulin-on-board and activity-mode overlay preferences.
- The system must use one glucose Y-axis clamp across every graph surface, so no reading is visible on one chart and clipped off another.
- The system must forward basal, bolus and insulin-on-board history for the last 6 hours to the Watch, capped at 500 records per type, and must reject at receipt any record with a basal rate above 15 U/hr, any bolus component above 25 U, a negative insulin-on-board value, or a non-positive timestamp.
- The system must recover gracefully from a truncated or malformed history payload by rendering no overlay rather than crashing or rendering partial garbage.
- The system must let the user ask the AI a question from the Watch by voice or text, must speak the answer aloud when the user has enabled that, must append the not-medical-advice statement to spoken output, and must offer the same three quick queries.
- The system must tell the user on the Watch, before any loading state, that AI chat requires a configured GlycemicGPT server when none is set up — naming the permanent condition and where to fix it, and never suggesting a retry.
- The system must use a single AI request timeout shared by the Watch and the backend call, so a late answer can never replace a displayed timeout error with a success state.
- The system must cache the most recent glucose reading, glucose history, insulin on board and display unit on the Watch and restore them on a cold start, in storage readable while the Watch is locked, so a complication renders real data immediately rather than '--'.
- The system must sync display and overlay preferences from the iPhone to the Watch on every change and re-sync on every reconnection, and must not offer any preference that does not change observable behaviour.
- The system must show the user, in iPhone settings, the true state of the wrist link: whether a Watch is paired, whether our Watch app is installed, whether any of our complications is on the active face, whether the Watch is currently reachable, the last glucose and insulin values the Watch actually holds with their timestamps, and how much of today's high-priority wrist-refresh budget remains.
- The system must budget wrist complication refreshes against the operating system's daily allowance and must pre-compute all time-driven state changes as future timeline entries, so that freshness decay and coverage expiry always occur even when no refresh budget remains.
- The system must show a glanceable glucose surface on the iPhone Lock Screen and Dynamic Island that also appears in the Watch Smart Stack without the user configuring anything, and must carry the same freshness and coverage honesty as the complications.
- The system must carry full-colour glucose banding — red at or beyond urgent thresholds, amber at or beyond warning thresholds, green in range, with warning boundaries inclusive — on every surface where the operating system permits full colour, and must convey the same information without hue on every surface where it does not.
- The system must be developable and demonstrable end to end in the iOS and watchOS Simulators using an injectable simulated glucose and pump-history source, because Core Bluetooth is entirely unavailable in the Simulator and no real pump data can reach the app there.
- The system must state plainly, in the user documentation and in the app, that the Watch experience is read-only: no interaction with it can deliver insulin, change a pump setting, or modify data.
- The system must warn the user when the installed Watch app build does not match the iPhone app build, and must not attempt to update the Watch app itself.
- The system must share every safety-critical constant and pure decision function — the 20–500 bound, 18.0156, threshold sanitization, the freshness tiers, the 60-second backward-clock guard, colour banding, the coverage decision, and the alert relay state machine — as one compiled module linked by the iPhone app, the Watch app, and both widget extensions, with tests pinning every documented boundary pair and the cross-surface mmol/L fixture.
- The system must expose the same accessibility descriptions on every wrist surface that the Android complications expose today, including the stale-alert, not-watching and no-recent-data phrasings, since screen-reader output is the one channel where the exact copy survives unchanged.

### IMPOSSIBLE (6)
- Custom watch faces — the DIGITAL_FULL and ANALOG_MECHANICAL WFF v2 faces (450×450 circular scenes, five/three complication slots with DefaultProviderPolicy binding our own providers, DigitalClock/TimeText with AMBIENT variants, AnalogClock with drawable hands, branding text, per-variant overlay defaults) :: watchOS provides no third-party watch face API of any kind. Apple owns the face: its layout, its clock rendering, its ambient/Always-On behaviour, its tint, and which complication slots exist. There is no WFF analogue, no way to draw a face, and no way to place branding on one. This has been true for the entire life of the platform and there is no indication it is changing. The face-level control the Android design depends on — knowing exactly which providers occupy which slots, and shipping a layout that guarantees BG, IoB, graph, alerts and chat are all visible together — is simply not available. :: ALT: Three things together get most of the user value. (1) WidgetKit complications in all four accessory families that the USER places on an Apple face — the data still reaches the wrist, but the layout is theirs and each complication must be added manually. (2) A shareable `.watchface` file bundled in the app: watchOS lets an app offer a watch-face configuration (an Apple face type with our complications pre-assigned to slots) that the user taps to add. That is the closest honest analogue to 'Push watch face' — it approximates the DIGITAL_FULL and ANALOG_MECHANICAL layouts using Apple's Modular/Infograph-style faces, but the user must confirm, and we control assignment, not rendering. (3) A Live Activity on the iPhone that iOS forwards into the watch Smart Stack, giving a large glanceable BG/trend/IoB/coverage surface with no user configuration at all — this is arguably a better default than a face for the first-run experience. The per-variant overlay defaults become plain user settings.
- Watch Face Push — phone side (WatchFacePusher: asset SHA-256 verification against BuildConfig, CapabilityClient node discovery, ChannelClient streaming, 120 s watchdog) and watch side (WatchFaceReceiveService, WatchFaceInstaller with addWatchFace/updateWatchFace/setWatchFaceAsActive, DWF validation token, the slot-limit retry, the Samsung double-update complication-rebind workaround, WatchFacePermissionActivity) :: Two independent Apple prohibitions. First, there is no watch face installation API at all — see above; nothing on watchOS corresponds to `WatchFacePushManager`, and no app can set the active watch face. Second, even if a face format existed, App Store Review Guideline 2.5.2 forbids an app downloading, installing, or executing code that is not part of the reviewed bundle; pushing an executable artifact to a paired device is squarely within that prohibition. Neither the `SET_PUSHED_WATCH_FACE_AS_ACTIVE` permission model nor the DWF validator has any iOS counterpart. :: ALT: Ship the complications in the app bundle (reviewed, signed, installed with the app) and replace the push UI with a guided setup flow plus an optional in-app `.watchface` share the user confirms. The integrity guarantees the push path was buying — SHA-256 verification of the asset, the PK magic-byte check, the 50 MB cap, the validation token — are provided instead by Apple's code signing and App Review, which is a strictly stronger guarantee. The 'Success means only that bytes were transferred' gap (and the unimplemented Story 32.4 status listener) becomes moot: there is nothing to transfer.
- Watch APK self-update over the data link (WearAppUpdateChecker against GitHub Releases, downloadWearApk with https/host enforcement and 100 MB cap, WearApkPusher, WatchApkReceiveService, PackageInstaller session, UpdateInstallReceiver, the Idle→Checking→Available→Downloading→Pushing→Installing→Success state machine) :: iOS has no `PackageInstaller`, no `REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES`, and no sideload path. An app cannot install another app or update itself, on the phone or on the watch. App Store Review Guideline 2.5.2 explicitly prohibits downloading and executing code outside the reviewed bundle, and Guideline 3.1.1-adjacent rules prohibit alternative distribution from inside an App Store/TestFlight app. Downloading an executable artifact from GitHub Releases and installing it is not achievable by any supported means, including under the EU alternative-distribution regime (which permits alternative marketplaces, not an app installing another app). :: ALT: The watchOS app ships inside the iOS app bundle, so a TestFlight update of the iPhone app updates the watch app too — no separate artifact, no separate check, no separate install. This removes the entire subsystem rather than replacing it, and it removes the documented lie in it ('Success' being a bare 15-second timer rather than a confirmation). Keep only the honest remnant: an in-app banner when the installed watch app's build number does not match the iPhone app's, with a link to the Watch app so the user can trigger the counterpart install, plus an update-available prompt driven by TestFlight/App Store rather than by GitHub.
- Per-buildType WFF provider-package substitution and the watch-face build pipeline (GenerateWatchFaceXmlTask with the @WEAR_PKG@ hard-fail, per-variant applicationIds matching <client-app-package>.watchfacepush.<identifier>, the classes.dex strip + zipalign + v1-JAR re-sign, updateAppWatchFaceAssets, committed-assets.sha256, the enableResourceOptimizations=false guard, the EPL-1.0 xpath2 processor exclusion) :: Moot: there is no watch face artifact to build, no WFF XML to substitute into, no DwfValidator, and no APK. The entire pipeline — including its genuinely clever safety property, that a release face can never point its complication slots at the debug wear package — exists only because Wear OS resolves complication providers by package name string at face-install time. :: ALT: Nothing is needed. Apple's equivalent problem does not arise: a widget extension is bound to its containing app by bundle identifier and provisioning, so a release build structurally cannot bind to a debug app's providers. Keep one artefact of the intent — a build-time assertion that the watchOS app's `WKCompanionAppBundleIdentifier`, the App Group identifiers, and the widget extension bundle IDs all agree with the containing app for the active configuration — since a mismatch there is the same silent-failure class the @WEAR_PKG@ hard-fail was protecting against. The EPL-1.0 licence exclusion protected by an instrumented test also goes away with the validator dependency.
- Phone-controlled watch face theme (dark / clinical_blue / high_contrast) and Show Seconds on the face :: Apple owns watch face colour and time rendering. There is no API for an app to set a face's tint, colour scheme, or seconds display, and complications render in an accented mode using the tint the USER chose for their face. Note that both of these are already dead code on Android — the map confirms `cfg_theme` and `cfg_show_seconds` are validated, stored, and read by nothing, while the phone Settings UI still presents controls for them. :: ALT: Delete both from the wire contract and the settings UI rather than porting inert controls onto a second platform. Rebind the concept of 'theme' to the watch APP's own screens (alerts, graph detail, chat), which are full colour and fully under our control, and expose it as an appearance/high-contrast accessibility preference — which is genuinely valuable for a glucose app and honest about what it affects.
- Triggering a wrist haptic from the background without showing a notification (the GlycemicDataListenerService vibration path: waveform 0/500/200/500 for urgent, one-shot 300 ms for warning) :: `WKInterfaceDevice.play(_:)` is only honoured while the app is running in the foreground. watchOS provides no API to vibrate the wrist from a background wake, and none to vibrate without a user-visible notification. This is a deliberate platform restriction, not an API gap. :: ALT: Deliver the alert as a notification scheduled on the watch (see the alert-delivery redesign), which plays the system haptic. You lose fine-grained control of the haptic waveform: `UNNotificationSound` and the interruption level determine the pattern, so the urgent-vs-warning distinction must be carried by the notification's interruption level (critical vs time-sensitive) and its sound, not by a custom vibration pattern. Note this changes an observable safety behaviour — an urgent low on Android has a distinguishable double-buzz; on watchOS the differentiator is a louder/DND-piercing critical alert, which is arguably stronger but is entitlement-dependent.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (12)
- Wearable Data Layer contract (WearDataContract): DataClient persistent paths, MessageClient transient paths, ChannelClient bulk paths, capability advertisement, and the `_ts` dedup-buster :: WatchConnectivity has no path-routed bus. It has four primitives with different delivery semantics and the mapping is not one-to-one: `updateApplicationContext` (single latest-value dictionary, replaces previous, background-delivered — the DataClient analogue), `transferUserInfo` (FIFO queue, guaranteed, survives app termination — no Android analogue, strictly better for alerts), `sendMessage` (requires `isReachable`, immediate — the MessageClient analogue), `transferFile` (bulk — the ChannelClient analogue, now only needed if anything bulk survives). Crucially there is only ONE application context per direction, so the eight distinct DataClient paths must be folded into one versioned dictionary or split across transports. Capability advertisement (`android_wear_capabilities`) has no analogue — `WCSession` exposes `isPaired` / `isWatchAppInstalled` / `isReachable` / `isComplicationEnabled` / `activationState` instead. And WidgetKit extensions CANNOT hold a `WCSession` — only the app can, so every wrist-side data arrival must wake the watch app, which then calls `WidgetCenter.shared.reloadTimelines`. :: REC: Keep a single `WearContract.swift` in `GlycemicCore` with the same key names and the same contract test that pins every string — the value of that file was never the transport. Structure it as: (1) ONE application context dictionary with a monotonic `seq: Int64` plus nested sub-dictionaries keyed by the existing path names, carrying CGM, IoB, config, category labels, monitoring status and the glucose unit — latest-value-wins is exactly right for all of these and `updateApplicationContext` costs no budget; (2) `transferUserInfo` for alerts (guaranteed, FIFO, wakes the watch app in the background, survives watch app termination) and for the three history batches; (3) `sendMessage` for alert dismiss and any chat fallback, with `transferUserInfo` as the unreachable fallback; (4) `transferCurrentComplicationUserInfo` reserved exclusively for material complication changes (see the refresh-cadence item). KEEP the `_ts`/`seq` dedup-buster: `updateApplicationContext` is documented to replace the context but is not documented to redeliver an identical one, and a monotonic sequence number costs nothing and preserves the load-bearing heartbeat delivery. Keep the unit token on the CGM payload rather than the config payload, for the documented reason. Close the write-only status gap while porting: the phone must actually consume watch-side status replies rather than repeating the Story 32.4 TODO.
- Phone→watch data streaming (WearDataSender) driven by PumpPollingOrchestrator's 15 s fast loop and 300 s slow loop inside a foreground service :: iOS has no foreground services and no reliable periodic timer. `PumpConnectionService` staying alive for hours is not reproducible. The only dependable wake source while a pump is connected is Core Bluetooth: with `bluetooth-central` in `UIBackgroundModes` plus `CBCentralManager` state restoration, iOS relaunches the app on peripheral notify/connect/disconnect and gives roughly 10 s of runtime (extendable to ~30 s with `beginBackgroundTask`). `BGAppRefreshTask`/`BGProcessingTask` are opportunistic — the system decides, commonly 15 min to hours apart, never at a guaranteed cadence, and NEVER after the user force-quits the app. So a 15 s fast loop cannot exist; the effective cadence is 'whenever the pump/CGM notifies', which for CGM is naturally ~5 min. Separately, wrist delivery is metered: `transferCurrentComplicationUserInfo` draws from a system daily budget (historically ~50/day, exposed at runtime via `WCSession.remainingComplicationUserInfoTransfers`), and 288 CGM readings/day cannot each buy a high-priority complication wake. :: REC: Layer three mechanisms. (1) On every Core Bluetooth wake, write the full latest state into `updateApplicationContext` — unmetered, background-delivered, and it also populates `receivedApplicationContext` for the watch's cold-start bootstrap. (2) Spend a metered `transferCurrentComplicationUserInfo` only when the render would MATERIALLY change: a glucose band crossing (per `bgColor`), a freshness-tier transition, an alert state change, or a coverage-state change — plus a floor of roughly one every 15 minutes. Budget the spend against `remainingComplicationUserInfoTransfers` and degrade to plain `transferUserInfo` when exhausted. (3) In backend-connected mode, additionally let the watch pull with `WKApplication.scheduleBackgroundRefresh` + `WKURLSessionRefreshBackgroundTask` (a few per hour, more when the app's complication is on the active face), which gives the wrist an independent data path and is the honest answer to 'the phone died'. Replace the swallow-everything error handling with a bounded outbound queue — `transferUserInfo` gives guaranteed FIFO delivery for free, which is strictly better than Android's 'a disconnected watch is a no-op'. And rethrow `CancellationError` uniformly (Swift's structured concurrency makes the three-old-methods-swallow-cancellation bug impossible if you use `try` correctly).
- Watch-side data receipt & fan-out (GlycemicDataListenerService): two-pass ordering, per-path validation, and the throttled complication fan-out (BG 30 s, IoB 60 s, graph 15 s/120 s adaptive, alerts unthrottled, history immediate) :: `WearableListenerService` has no watchOS analogue — there is no background service that can be started by a data arrival. The receiver is `WCSessionDelegate` on the watch APP, which the system wakes in the background for `transferUserInfo` / `transferCurrentComplicationUserInfo` / application-context arrivals, and which then must call `WidgetCenter.shared.reloadTimelines`. The two-pass ordering (config before data) becomes unnecessary for the application-context path — one dictionary arrives atomically — but is still needed across transports. More importantly, the Android throttle constants were tuned against CPU cost; on watchOS the constraint is a hard system reload budget, so 30 s/60 s/15 s reload floors would be blown through in under an hour and the system would silently stop honouring reloads. Finally, direct background vibration is not available: `WKInterfaceDevice.play(_:)` only works while the app is running in the foreground, so the alert haptic must become a notification. :: REC: Delete the time-based throttles and replace them with two mechanisms. First, PRE-BAKE every time-driven transition as timeline entries: when a CGM reading arrives, the timeline includes entries at now, ts+6 min, ts+15 min; when a monitoring status arrives, an entry at receivedAt+timeoutMs. That reproduces the entire GLY-116 axis-(b) and axis-(a) decay with zero reloads, which is what the Android UPDATE_PERIOD_SECONDS=300 was paying for. Second, call `reloadTimelines` only on MATERIAL change (band crossing, alert change, coverage change, history backfill) and keep alerts unthrottled as today. Port every per-path validation rule literally — the 0..500 count gate, the 15 U/hr and 25 U caps, the timeout clamp to 10 s..30 min, the VALID_MONITORING_STATES rejection, the `isValidGlucose` reject-the-whole-CGM-event rule, the apply-unit-before-validating rule, the `sanitizeThresholds` defaults 70/180/55/250, and `rebuzz` defaulting to true. Replace the `Vibrator` call with `UNUserNotificationCenter.add()` scheduled ON THE WATCH (see the alert delivery item).
- Complication provider: alerts (bell) — the coverage-honesty glance, encoded entirely as a tint (grey / yellow / red) with a load-bearing six-branch precedence :: This is the single highest-risk item in the whole subsystem. watchOS complications render in an accented/tinted mode: the system applies the watch face's tint to your content and you can only split it into accented and non-accented groups via `.widgetAccentable()`. You cannot rely on arbitrary hue surviving — grey vs amber vs red, the entire encoding of the dead-phone cue, can be flattened to a single face-chosen colour. The Android design deliberately makes 'quiet grey bell' mean 'something is currently watching' and 'amber bell' mean 'coverage degraded or unknown, even with zero alerts'. If both render identically on the wrist, the port silently deletes the dead-phone cue in exactly the case it exists for. :: REC: Re-encode on symbol + text, not colour. Provide `.accessoryCircular` (distinct SF Symbol per branch, `.widgetAccentable()` on the alarming states so they take the face accent) AND `.accessoryRectangular` (symbol plus a one-line state label carrying the same copy the AlertsActivity uses). Keep the pure six-branch `render(alert, coverage, nowMs)` function unchanged in `GlycemicCore` — only its output type changes from a tint Int to a (symbol, label, accentable) triple. Keep the precedence ordering exactly, including the load-bearing rule that the stale-alert quiet state may only render while coverage is Watching. Keep the full `contentDescription` strings as `.accessibilityLabel`, unchanged — VoiceOver is the one channel where the current copy survives verbatim, and for this app it is arguably the most important one. Add a colour-blind/monochrome review gate: the four states must be distinguishable in a greyscale screenshot.
- Complication provider: glucose graph (400×100 ARGB bitmap sparkline, 7-layer draw order, per-segment glucose colouring, basal/IoB/mode-band overlays, hash-based bitmap cache) :: Three separate problems. (1) The output is a fixed 400×100 bitmap sized for a WFF `PartImage` slot; watchOS accessory families are system-sized and the app never controls the geometry. (2) The accessory rendering mode flattens colour, so per-segment red/yellow/green glucose banding, the purple/orange mode bands, the teal/blue-grey basal colouring and the blue IoB overlay all collapse — the overlay system is *entirely* colour-encoded. (3) `.accessoryCircular` is far too small for a 3-hour sparkline with four overlay layers, and even `.accessoryRectangular` is a narrow strip. :: REC: Split it. On the wrist complication, ship `.accessoryRectangular` with a SwiftUI `Canvas` sparkline: glucose line at full accent, target-range band as a low-opacity fill, threshold lines dashed, and NO basal/bolus/IoB/mode overlays — the Android code already hardcodes bolus markers off at 400×100 for exactly this 'too small' reason, and on a watchOS accessory strip the same argument extends to all four overlays. Put the full seven-layer, full-colour rendering in (a) the watch app's GraphDetailActivity equivalent and (b) an iPhone Home Screen widget, where `WidgetRenderingMode.fullColor` genuinely is available and the colour encoding survives intact. Drop the bitmap hash cache — SwiftUI `Canvas` re-renders per timeline entry with no bitmap to cache, and WidgetKit's own entry diffing covers it. Keep the MIN_READINGS=3 gate, the range-window cutoff, thresholds taken from the LAST reading in the window, and the 'x-axis spans first→last reading, not cutoff→now' behaviour. Fix the Y-clamp inconsistency (20/500 sparkline vs 40/400 detail) to one value in `GlycemicCore`.
- Phone→watch monitoring-status mirror with a heartbeat (WearMonitoringStatusForwarder: re-send every max(timeoutMs/2, 5 s) — 3 min in production — from the pump service's scope, with retryWhen resubscribe) :: THE HEARTBEAT MECHANISM IS NOT IMPLEMENTABLE ON iOS. There is no guaranteed periodic wake: no foreground service, no AlarmManager-equivalent that runs arbitrary code, `BGAppRefreshTask` is opportunistic and typically far coarser than 3 minutes, and a force-quit app is never relaunched. The DESIGN INTENT, however, is not only implementable but is MORE necessary on iOS than on Android, because iOS has more ways for the phone to go silent (force-quit, BLE state-restoration not firing, Low Power Mode, the user revoking Bluetooth). The Android design uses arrival CADENCE to restart a watch-side timer; iOS must use an explicit DEADLINE instead, so that a phone that gets only sporadic wakes still advertises a coverage claim the watch can decay correctly. :: REC: Do options 2 and 3 together, and keep option 1 as a best-effort supplement. The monitoring-status payload gains an absolute `validUntilMs` computed by the phone as `now + timeoutMs`; the watch's `coverageFrom` decays against that deadline rather than against `receivedAt + timeoutMs`, so an irregular delivery cadence no longer produces false 'no recent data'. The claim rides along on EVERY application-context write — every BLE wake, every CGM reading, every IoB poll — so it is refreshed by the same event stream that proves the phone is alive, which is exactly what the heartbeat was proxying for. Additionally register a `BGAppRefreshTask` that re-advertises when the system grants one. Keep the wire vocabulary (`server_active`, `floor_watching`, `not_watching` plus the five verbatim reason names) and the contract test that pins it — this is a load-bearing contract. Keep the watch-side clamp of the advertised window to 10 s..30 min and the fail-closed rejection of unknown state strings. Keep monitoring status unpersisted. Explicitly accept and document the honesty hole iOS adds: a force-quit iPhone app will not be relaunched by Core Bluetooth, and the wrist's decay to 'no recent data' is the ONLY signal the user gets — which is precisely the behaviour this design already produces.
- Alert delivery to the wrist: vibration fired directly by the watch service (waveform for urgent, one-shot for warning), gated on showAlert, with rebuzz defaulting to true :: watchOS has no API to trigger a haptic from the background — `WKInterfaceDevice.current().play(_:)` only works while the app is running and foreground. The supported way to alert the wrist is a notification. But mirroring an iPhone local notification to the watch is NOT guaranteed: iOS mirrors notifications to the watch only when the iPhone is locked/asleep and the watch is on-wrist and unlocked; if the iPhone is unlocked and in use, the alert stays on the phone and the wrist stays silent. Separately, breaking through Silent Mode, Focus, Do Not Disturb and Sleep Focus requires the `com.apple.developer.usernotifications.critical-alerts` entitlement, which Apple grants case-by-case on written request and is NOT available by simply ticking a box in Xcode. :: REC: Schedule the alert notification ON THE WATCH. The phone sends the alert as a `transferUserInfo` (guaranteed FIFO, wakes the watch app in the background even when suspended), and the watch app calls `UNUserNotificationCenter.add()` with an immediate trigger. This is the only path that makes the wrist alert independent of iPhone lock state, and it is a genuine improvement over Android. Request the Critical Alerts entitlement for urgent_low / urgent_high with `UNNotificationSound.defaultCriticalSound`; enable the freely-available `com.apple.developer.usernotifications.time-sensitive` entitlement and use `.timeSensitive` interruption level for low/high, and as the fallback for urgent if Critical Alerts is denied. Port the 30-min re-buzz as a LOCALLY SCHEDULED ladder on the watch using `UNTimeIntervalNotificationTrigger` — these fire even when the app is suspended or terminated, so the wrist keeps re-alarming on an ongoing emergency even if the phone dies, which Android cannot do. Bound that ladder: it must terminate once the underlying reading ages past TOO_STALE, and the terminating notification must say the data is stale and nothing is currently watching, rather than continuing to re-buzz off a frozen snapshot. Keep the `showAlert` toggle (rename it honestly — 'Wrist alerts' — since on iOS it suppresses scheduling, and the system also gives the user independent control in Settings and the Watch app, which the UI must acknowledge). Keep `rebuzz` defaulting to true. Preserve the 5-min silent refresh that keeps the shown alert inside the 6-min STALE band — on iOS this rides the natural BLE CGM cadence.
- Phone→watch alert relay state machine (PumpPollingOrchestrator): the isReadingAlertable data-trust gate, watchRelayMutex serialisation, NEW/ONGOING/RECOVERY transitions, WRIST_ALERT_REFRESH_MS 5 min, WRIST_ALERT_REBUZZ_MS 30 min :: The decision logic ports verbatim and MUST — 'never retract an alert on untrusted data' is the most safety-load-bearing rule in the subsystem. What changes is its host: there is no poll loop guaranteed to run, so the 5-minute silent refresh and the 30-minute re-buzz cannot be driven by 'the next iteration of the loop'. `watchRelayMutex` becomes a Swift `actor`, which is a straightforward improvement. :: REC: Make the state machine an `actor` and drive it from Core Bluetooth reading arrivals, which is the natural ~5-min cadence while a pump is connected — this preserves WRIST_ALERT_REFRESH_MS 5 min exactly. Move the 30-minute re-buzz cadence to a watch-scheduled notification ladder (above), so an ongoing emergency keeps alarming the wrist even if the phone stops. Port `isReadingAlertable` and the rule that a non-alertable reading skips the WHOLE block — no send, no clear, latch untouched — with its comment intact. Port the rule that RECOVERY is keyed on the server CLASSIFICATION being null rather than on the watch-type mapping being null. Port `SERVER_TO_WATCH_ALERT_TYPE` and the 'a mapping gap may cost the watch relay, never the alert-floor hand-off' behaviour.
- On-watch AI chat (ChatActivity: voice input, TTS with configured voice, markdown stripping, 30 s timeout) + phone relay (WearChatRelayService: foreground service blocking a binder thread for a 90 s LLM call) :: Three distinct platform breaks. (1) The relay's whole architecture — promote to a foreground service, then `runBlocking` on the binder thread so GMS does not unbind before the LLM answers — is meaningless on iOS: there are no foreground services and `WCSession.sendMessage`'s reply handler times out in tens of seconds, far short of the 90 s API budget. (2) `SFSpeechRecognizer` (the Speech framework) is NOT available on watchOS; `RecognizerIntent.ACTION_RECOGNIZE_SPEECH` has no direct equivalent. (3) `AVSpeechSynthesizer` IS available on watchOS, so TTS ports, but audio session handling and background audio on watchOS are restricted. :: REC: Remove the relay and let the watch call the backend directly. This deletes the foreground-service hack, deletes the 30 s-vs-90 s timeout mismatch bug (documented gap #9), and deletes the whole `WearChatRelayService` class of complexity. The iPhone provisions the watch with the backend base URL and an auth token over WatchConnectivity, and the watch stores the token in its own Keychain with `kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly` (so it survives a locked-wrist background fetch). Keep a `transferUserInfo`-based phone relay ONLY as a fallback for the case where the watch cannot reach the backend. Port the BLE-only-mode gate verbatim, including the exact terminal copy ('AI chat needs a GlycemicGPT server — none is set up on your phone.') and its deliberate refusal to say 'try again' — and check it BEFORE showing any loading state; the watch can evaluate it locally, which also fixes documented gap 'the Chat complication does not check whether a backend is configured'. Voice input: use watchOS's system text-input controller (SwiftUI `TextField` on watchOS presents Scribble/dictation/emoji), which needs no Speech-framework entitlement and covers the dictation use case; handle its absence the way `ActivityNotFoundException` is handled today. TTS: `AVSpeechSynthesizer` with `AVSpeechSynthesisVoice.speechVoices()` for the voice-selection setting — direct port including the pre-compiled markdown-stripping regexes, the '. This is not medical advice.' suffix, and the `spokenResponseId` re-speak guard. Set a single unified timeout (adopt the phone's 90 s and show progress) rather than porting the mismatched 30/90 pair. Keep the three hardcoded quick queries.
- Watch face configuration sync and the phone Settings > Watch UI (WatchConnectionCard, WatchFaceGalleryCard, WatchFaceConfigCard with theme/seconds/variant, WatchAppUpdateCard, WatchDataTelemetryCard) :: Roughly half of this UI controls things that do not exist on watchOS: variant selection (no custom faces), theme (Apple owns face colour), Show Seconds (Apple owns time rendering — and both of these are already inert on Android today), and 'Push watch face' / 'Check for watch update' (both impossible on iOS). What remains — reachability status, toggles that actually gate behaviour, graph overlays, glucose range, telemetry — ports, plus iOS adds new state worth surfacing that Android has no analogue for. :: REC: Delete `cfg_theme` and `cfg_show_seconds` from the wire contract entirely rather than porting toggles that do nothing — Android's own notes confirm they have zero consumers. Rebind 'theme' to an in-app appearance/high-contrast accessibility setting for the watch app's own screens (alerts, graph detail, chat), which is genuinely meaningful. Replace WatchFaceGalleryCard with a guided 'Set up your watch face' flow: which complication families we provide, what each shows, and step-by-step instructions to add them (optionally offering a shareable `.watchface` configuration file per recommended layout — the closest Apple analogue to variant selection). Replace WatchAppUpdateCard with a version-parity banner (iOS build vs watch build) plus a link to the Watch app's install flow. Keep and extend WatchDataTelemetryCard with iOS-specific honesty state that has no Android analogue and matters a lot here: `WCSession.activationState`, `isPaired`, `isWatchAppInstalled`, `isComplicationEnabled`, `isReachable`, and `remainingComplicationUserInfoTransfers` (the remaining daily high-priority wrist-refresh budget). Keep the real toggles — Show IoB, Show Glucose Graph, Wrist Alerts, Graph Range 1H/3H/6H, and the four graph-overlay switches — and keep re-pushing config on reconnect via the monotonic-`seq` context write.
- Wear module manifest, permissions and service registration (VIBRATE, FOREGROUND_SERVICE_DATA_SYNC, POST_NOTIFICATIONS, PUSH_WATCH_FACES, SET_PUSHED_WATCH_FACE_AS_ACTIVE, REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES, BIND_COMPLICATION_PROVIDER, standalone=false) :: Nothing maps one-to-one. iOS uses entitlements plus Info.plist usage descriptions plus capability toggles, several of the Android permissions have no iOS counterpart at all (VIBRATE, REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES, the two watch-face-push permissions), and one iOS requirement — the Critical Alerts entitlement — is gated behind a written request to Apple rather than a manifest line. :: REC: Write the iOS manifest up front and file the Critical Alerts request early, because a denial changes the alerting design. Required set for this subsystem: `UIBackgroundModes` = `bluetooth-central`, `remote-notification`, `fetch`, `processing`; `BGTaskSchedulerPermittedIdentifiers`; `NSBluetoothAlwaysUsageDescription`; App Groups (one for iOS app ↔ iOS widget, a SEPARATE one for watch app ↔ watch widget — App Groups do NOT span the iPhone and the Watch, which is a common and expensive misconception); `com.apple.developer.usernotifications.time-sensitive`; `com.apple.developer.usernotifications.critical-alerts` (Apple-approved); `NSSupportsLiveActivities` and `NSSupportsLiveActivitiesFrequentUpdates` if Live Activities are adopted; `WKCompanionAppBundleIdentifier` and `WKRunsIndependentlyOfCompanionApp = false` (the Android `standalone=false` analogue — the watch app depends on the phone for pump data); and, because the backend is self-hosted and may sit on a LAN, `NSLocalNetworkUsageDescription` + `NSBonjourServices` and a narrowly-scoped ATS exception rather than `NSAllowsArbitraryLoads`. Retire VIBRATE (notifications), FOREGROUND_SERVICE* (no foreground services), REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES and both PUSH_WATCH_FACES permissions (impossible), and BIND_COMPLICATION_PROVIDER (WidgetKit needs no equivalent).
- Installation and distribution (ADB sideload of wear-device-release.apk, optional watchface-release.apk, GitHub Releases, the documented 'most complicated step' developer-grade workflow) :: None of it applies. There is no sideloading, no ADB, no separate watch artifact to install, and no GitHub Releases distribution path for iOS. The stated plan is TestFlight, which brings its own rules: every first build of a version goes through Beta App Review before external testers get it, builds expire after 90 days, and external testing is capped at 10,000 testers. :: REC: TestFlight as the primary path, and this is a MASSIVE user-experience improvement over the Android story: the watchOS app is embedded in the iOS app bundle and installs automatically as a counterpart, so the documented 'the Wear OS install is genuinely the most complicated step' problem simply ceases to exist. Document the two things that replace it: (1) the watch app may need a manual install from the iPhone's Watch app if 'Automatic App Install' is off, and (2) complications must be added by the user to their chosen face — write that guide, because it is the new 'most complicated step'. Carry the honest doc notes forward verbatim, especially 'The watch app is read-only. No interaction with it can deliver insulin, change pump settings, or modify your data' — App Review will read this and it is also true. Add the TestFlight-specific facts to the user doc: 90-day build expiry, the need to reinstall, and that a lapsed build stops monitoring.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (15)
- GLUCOSE VALIDITY BOUND 20..500 mg/dL — `GlucoseDisplayUtils.isValidGlucose(mgDl) = mgDl in 20..500`. Enforced at four watch-side points: (1) `GlycemicDataListenerService` CGM_PATH rejects the whole reading; (2) the same service sanitizes an out-of-range `alert_bg` to 0; (3) `BgComplicationDataSource.render` refuses to show an out-of-range value regardless of age; (4) `AlertsActivity` gates the BG line on `bgValue in 20..500` (a raw inline literal). Listed in docs/contract/safety-constants.md as a shipped duplication site covered by the cross-repo drift guard and the CodeRabbit Medical-Safety gate. The phone re-applies it when reading telemetry back (`SettingsViewModel.loadWatchDataTelemetry`). GAP: `GlycemicWearDeviceApp.bootstrapDataFromDataLayer` applies it to the cached CGM but NOT to the cached alert's bgValue.
- UNIT CONVERSION FACTOR 18.0156 — `GlucoseDisplayUtils.MGDL_PER_MMOL = 18.0156`, duplicated deliberately from the phone's `GlucoseFormat.MGDL_PER_MMOL` and the backend's `MGDL_PER_MMOL` because the wear module cannot depend on `:app`. Guarded by `SafetyConstantWearGuardTest` (exact equality, delta 0.0) and a cross-surface fixture (54→3.0, 70→3.9, 99→5.5, 100→5.6, 120→6.7, 180→10.0, 250→13.9, 400→22.2). The code comment forbids a second value (18.02 / 18.0182). Conversion divides ONCE and rounds LAST, with `Locale.US` so a comma-decimal locale cannot produce '6,7'. Conversion is DISPLAY-ONLY: the wire value, storage, all threshold comparisons, all color banding, all plotting geometry, and the 20..500 invariant stay canonical mg/dL. `bgColor()` deliberately takes no unit parameter so the user's mmol preference structurally cannot reach the banding decision.
- THRESHOLD SANITIZATION ORDERING — `sanitizeThresholds` clamps in a dependent order (`low.coerceIn(40,200)`; `high.coerceIn(max(low+1,100),400)`; `urgentLow.coerceIn(20,low)`; `urgentHigh.coerceIn(high,500)`) so any phone payload — including a corrupt or hostile one — yields `20 ≤ urgentLow ≤ low < high ≤ urgentHigh ≤ 500`. Without this an inverted pair would make `bgColor` mis-band every reading. Defaults when keys are absent: low=70, high=180, urgentLow=55, urgentHigh=250.
- STALENESS DETECTION IS TWO INDEPENDENT AXES (GLY-116) AND BOTH MUST FAIL CLOSED. Axis (b) = local aging of shown values against the WATCH's own clock: CGM 6 min STALE / 15 min TOO_STALE; PUMP (IoB) 15 min STALE / 60 min TOO_STALE; half-open boundaries. BG shows the value with a '?' in the STALE band and drops it entirely (rendering '--' while the age keeps counting) at TOO_STALE. IoB does the same on the pump policy — 'an hour-old value presented as current is dosing-relevant misinformation'. Axis (a) = the phone's mirrored coverage claim, which the watch NEVER re-derives and which decays locally after the phone-advertised timeout. The two are deliberately decoupled: BG greys at STALE age even while the mirrored status still says ServerActive. Complication UPDATE_PERIOD_SECONDS=300 on BG/IoB/Alerts exists solely so this decay happens WITHOUT a phone push — a push-only complication would freeze its last reassuring state forever after the phone dies.
- BACKWARD WALL-CLOCK JUMP GUARD — `STATUS_MAX_FUTURE_SKEW_MS = 60_000` (mirrors the phone's `ALERT_FLOOR_MAX_FUTURE_SKEW_MS`). Ages more negative than −60 s mean the watch clock jumped backward, which understates EVERY age; both axes fail closed on it (`classify` returns TOO_STALE, `coverageFrom` returns NoRecentStatus, `formatAge` returns 'unknown time' rather than the incoherent 'as of just now — data stale'). Small negative ages are tolerated as routine sampling skew so healthy feeds do not flap.
- ALERT DELIVERY GUARANTEES — no delivery receipt anywhere. `WearDataSender` swallows all exceptions with a log line; there is no retry, no queue, no acknowledgement. A vibration fires only when `type != "none" && config.showAlert && rebuzz`; `showAlert=false` (labelled 'Alert Vibration' in phone Settings) silently disables ALL wrist vibration while still updating the display. `rebuzz` defaults to TRUE when the key is absent so an older phone build still buzzes. Sustained alerts re-buzz every `WRIST_ALERT_REBUZZ_MS = 30 min` (= `AlertFloor.FLOOR_COOLDOWN_MS`) and are silently refreshed every `WRIST_ALERT_REFRESH_MS = 5 min` specifically to stay inside the 6-minute CGM STALE band so a still-live alert never greys as 'data stale'.
- NEVER RETRACT AN ALERT ON UNTRUSTED DATA — the phone's relay block is entirely skipped when `AlertFloor.isReadingAlertable(reading, nowMs)` is false: no sendAlert, no clearAlert, latch untouched. Documented reason: retracting would flip a possibly-still-true low into the watch's reassuring 'All clear' off data nobody can vouch for. `clearAlert()` is reserved for a genuinely FRESH in-range recovery reading, and is keyed on the server CLASSIFICATION being null rather than on the watch-type mapping being null (a mapping gap must cost a push, never produce an 'All clear').
- 'ALL CLEAR' IS RESERVED FOR A CURRENT WATCHING CLAIM — `AlertsActivity.NoAlertContent` shows the green 'All clear' ONLY for `WristCoverage.Watching`; `NotWatching` and `NoRecentStatus` each render explicit degraded copy. `AlertsComplicationDataSource` mirrors this: the quiet grey bell requires `covered`, everything else is amber. The stale-alert grey is deliberately gated on `covered` too — 'a real alert whose phone then dies ages past the cap and never auto-clears, and quiet grey there would be indistinguishable from the healthy all-clear, suppressing the dead-phone cue in exactly the case it exists for.'
- MONITORING-STATUS DECAY MUST NOT BE DEFEATED BY A CORRUPT PAYLOAD — the watch clamps the phone-advertised timeout to `10_000L .. 1_800_000L` (floor covers the 20 s compressed debug policy; cap stops a dead phone's last 'watching' surviving for hours) and rejects any `mon_state` outside the three known values. Monitoring status is deliberately NOT persisted across a watch process restart — coverage after a restart is genuinely unknown and the fail-closed render is the honest one. Unknown state strings fail closed to NoRecentStatus.
- HEARTBEAT IS THE DEAD-PHONE DETECTOR — `WearMonitoringStatusForwarder` re-sends on a heartbeat of `max(timeoutMs/2, 5_000)` (3 min in production) as well as on change, because the source flow deduplicates and a healthy steady state would emit once, leaving a disconnected watch holding a frozen 'watching' forever. The `_ts` dedup-buster in the DataMap is what makes the unchanged heartbeat actually deliverable. `retryWhen` with a 5 s delay resubscribes on stream failure — 'a coverage-status stream must never die silently.'
- INSULIN/DOSING DISPLAY BOUNDS — history overlay records are filtered at receipt against Tandem pump safety limits: `MAX_BOLUS_UNITS = 25f` (max single bolus) applied to `units`, `correctionUnits` AND `mealUnits`; `MAX_BASAL_RATE = 15f` U/hr; `iob >= 0f`; all require `timestampMs > 0`. `MAX_HISTORY_RECORDS = 500` per type. The decoder additionally guards against Int overflow when computing required bytes (`count.toLong() * recordSize.toLong()`). Live IoB (`IOB_PATH`) is NOT range-validated — only aged. The watch face is documented as strictly read-only: no interaction can deliver insulin, change pump settings, or modify data.
- REASON-CODE VOCABULARY IS A LOAD-BEARING CONTRACT — the five `MONITORING_REASON_*` constants must match the phone's `FloorNotWatchingReason` enum names exactly; a rename would silently degrade the watch to generic copy. `AlertsCopyTest` pins every arm to a distinct line, described as 'the wear analogue of the store's silent-disarm', which is why `notWatchingReasonCopy` is `internal` rather than `private`.
- WFF PROVIDER PACKAGE MUST TRACK THE BUILD TYPE — `GenerateWatchFaceXmlTask` throws a GradleException if the template lacks the `@WEAR_PKG@` token, because a hardcoded package would leave RELEASE faces pointing complications at the DEBUG wear app, producing a face with permanently dead glucose/IoB/graph slots on a fresh production install. Backed by `WatchFacePusherTest` assertions that release assets never reference the debug wear package.
- PUSHED APK INTEGRITY — the phone verifies the bundled WFF asset's SHA-256 against a `BuildConfig` value Gradle computes from the same committed file at build time (so the runtime tamper check is always in lockstep), and refuses to push on mismatch. The watch validates the received file's PK zip magic bytes, caps it at 50 MB (100 MB for the wear APK), and requires a non-empty DWF validation token before calling `addWatchFace`/`updateWatchFace`. `committed-assets.sha256` plus the JVM drift gate prevent a hand-edited or partially regenerated asset from shipping.
- APPLICATION-ID EQUALITY IS A SILENT-FAILURE HAZARD — if `:wear-device`'s applicationId ever diverges from `:app`'s, the Data Layer drops every message and DataItem with no error surfaced to the user; the watch would keep rendering its last cached (reassuring) values until they age out. Discovered the hard way in Story 32.8.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (29)
  Architecture: single phone app + watch-side services (no separate companion phone app) | Wearable Data Layer contract (WearDataContract) — mirrored verbatim in both modules | Phone→watch data streaming (WearDataSender) | Watch-side data receipt & fan-out (GlycemicDataListenerService) | Watch-side state store (WatchDataRepository) with SharedPreferences persistence | Cold-start bootstrap (GlycemicWearDeviceApp) | Complication provider: BG (blood glucose) | Complication provider: IoB (insulin on board) | Complication provider: glucose graph (on-watch sparkline renderer) | Complication provider: alerts (bell) — the coverage-honesty glance | Complication provider: AI chat (bubble) | Watch face variant: DIGITAL_FULL (WFF v2) | Watch face variant: ANALOG_MECHANICAL (WFF v2) | Per-buildType WFF provider-package substitution (build-time) | Watch face push: phone side (WatchFacePusher) | Watch face push: watch side receive + install (WatchFaceReceiveService, WatchFaceInstaller, WatchFacePermissionActivity) | Watch APK self-update over Data Layer (WearApkPusher → WatchApkReceiveService → UpdateInstallReceiver) | Phone→watch monitoring-status mirror + watch-local decay (GLY-116 axis a) | Watch freshness policy (WatchFreshness) — mirrored, not shared | Glucose validation, unit conversion and color banding (GlucoseDisplayUtils, GlucoseUnit) | On-watch alerts screen (AlertsActivity) | On-watch AI chat (ChatActivity) + phone relay (WearChatRelayService) | Alert dismiss flow (watch → phone) | Phone→watch alert relay state machine (PumpPollingOrchestrator) | Compact history overlay wire format (WearHistorySerializer) | Full-screen interactive graph on the watch (GraphDetailActivity) | Watch face configuration sync and the phone Settings > Watch UI | Wear module manifest, permissions and service registration | Installation and distribution (documented user-facing procedure)


================================================================================
## Pump driver API and safety model  [pump-driver-api]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (28)
- The app must define the absolute glucose validity range 20-500 mg/dL exactly once, in a module shared by the phone app, the watch app and any widget extension, and every reading model, safety-limit type, calibration validator and event validator must reference that single definition.
- CI must fail the build if the glucose bounds, the mmol/mg-dL factor 18.0156, or the Tandem epoch offset 1199145600 drifts from its canonical value, and must exercise every validator at both its accept and reject boundaries.
- Every reading model (CGM, BGM, bolus) must reject out-of-range construction by throwing, so a driver cannot publish an unvalidated value and a caller that omits filtering fails loudly rather than storing bad data.
- All history extraction and bolus-history paths must drop out-of-range readings and doses rather than clamp them, and tests must prove that a 900 mg/dL record and a 40 U bolus are absent from the result rather than coerced to 500 and 25.
- Drivers must read the current safety limits at every validation pass, and a test must prove that narrowing the limits mid-stream changes which readings are accepted without restarting the driver.
- The app must accept safety limits only from backend sync and must never offer a local override; limits received from the backend must be clamped rather than crash the app, and any clamping must be recorded and surfaced.
- The app must expose the age of the last safety-limit sync and must treat a never-synced install as stale.
- A user must be able to see every pump driver compiled into their build, which drivers are active, and which capability slot each one occupies.
- Activating a driver must never leave its capability slot empty during the swap, and activating a conflicting single-instance driver must automatically deactivate the previous one.
- Deactivating a driver must stop its work while preserving its credentials and settings, so reactivation does not require re-pairing.
- A maintainer must be able to disable a specific driver at build time such that it is absent from the driver list, never instantiated and never polled, while the user's prior selection is preserved for when it is re-enabled.
- A user must be able to pair a Tandem t:slim X2 by scanning, choosing from a device list and entering the pump pairing code, with model-appropriate instructions derived from the advertised name and generic instructions for an unrecognized model.
- A user must be able to pair a Medtronic 700-series pump by keeping a pairing screen open while the phone advertises, selecting the phone from the pump's own menu, and must be shown a specific reason when the attempt stalls: Bluetooth off, Bluetooth permission denied, this device cannot advertise, advertising rejected, handshake timed out, authentication rejected, or the pump is still bound to another phone.
- The app must reconnect automatically to a previously paired pump after being backgrounded, after being terminated by the system, and after device reboot, without the user reopening the app.
- The app must remain able to identify a previously paired pump after the app is reinstalled or the phone is replaced, or must clearly tell the user that re-pairing is required and why.
- When a user unpairs a pump the app must clear its stored credentials and disconnect, and must tell the user when an additional Forget This Device step in iOS Settings is required to complete removal.
- The app must distinguish 'no new reading' from 'the pump has not responded', and must surface the age of the most recent successful read rather than presenting an indefinitely stale value as current.
- The app must backfill pump history after any period of lost connectivity or app suspension, and a full history sync started by the user must be able to continue while the app is backgrounded.
- Pump credentials and pairing codes must be stored encrypted, unavailable before first device unlock, excluded from iCloud and encrypted backups, and removed when the app is deleted and reinstalled.
- Raw BLE packet payloads must be retained only in debug builds and only in memory, and must never be written to any log or file that can leave the device.
- The app must expose no insulin delivery, basal change or pump setting write anywhere in the driver API, and CI must fail if a driver introduces a write to a therapy control point or a symbol matching a delivery-verb denylist.
- The app must ship a fully simulated pump driver, runnable in the iOS Simulator without Bluetooth, that exercises connection, authentication failure, link loss, reconnection, background restoration, both pairing styles, every pairing fault, and out-of-range readings.
- The app must ship a recorded-trace replay driver so that real Tandem and Medtronic packet parsing and safety filtering can be validated in the Simulator without a physical pump.
- A user must be able to fork the repository, supply their own Apple Team ID and App Store Connect API key, and produce a signed TestFlight build through GitHub Actions without owning a Mac and without any project-published binary.
- The app must declare NSBluetoothAlwaysUsageDescription and the bluetooth-central and bluetooth-peripheral background modes, and must degrade to a clearly labelled unavailable state when Bluetooth is off, unauthorized or unsupported.
- Every driver plugin ID must match the reverse-domain identifier pattern and every settings key must match the settings-key pattern, enforced at build time, because both values are also persistence keys.
- Event delivery between drivers and the platform must be explicitly best-effort with bounded buffering, and no user-facing alerting may depend on it for a delivery guarantee.
- A driver that is compiled into the app but not registered in the driver catalog must fail CI rather than be silently absent from the running app.

### IMPOSSIBLE (3)
- Runtime plugin loading (DEX JAR sideloading via DexPluginLoader) :: iOS forbids executing code that is not present in the signed app bundle. There is no DexClassLoader equivalent, `dlopen` of a downloaded binary is blocked by code signing and by App Store and TestFlight policy, and Swift has no reflective instantiation of a class named by a manifest string. Every element of this capability is unimplementable: the DexClassLoader with the app classloader as parent, the 65,536-char-bounded META-INF/plugin.json read, `loadClass(factoryClass).getConstructor().newInstance()`, the PluginFactory cast, and the exact-match apiVersion rejection. :: ALT: Compile-time-linked driver targets — already the decided architecture — plus the fork-and-build distribution model as the authorship path: a third-party driver author forks the repo, adds a Swift package target conforming to DevicePlugin, registers it in DriverCatalog, and the user builds and signs it into their own TestFlight. The PluginManifest fields (id, name, version, apiVersion, factoryClass, author, description) survive as static metadata on the factory type, validated by the same reverse-domain ID regex and by a build-time apiVersion assertion instead of a runtime rejection. The trust step moves from 'user taps install and accepts a warning' to 'user builds with their own signing key'.
- Plugin JAR file management (PluginFileManager install/remove) :: With no runtime loading there is nothing to install. The entire mechanism is Android-specific plumbing for a capability iOS does not have: the Storage Access Framework document picker, the .jar suffix requirement, filename sanitization, the canonical-path traversal guard with its trailing-separator refinement, the 50 MB streaming abort, the PK\x03\x04 ZIP magic check, `setReadOnly()` for DexClassLoader, and the setWritable-then-delete removal. iOS also gives a user no supported way to place an executable artifact into an app's container. :: ALT: Nothing user-facing survives. The equivalent trust decision moves to build time and becomes visible in the repository: which drivers a given fork links is declared in DriverCatalog and in the xcconfig feature flags. The one thing worth keeping in spirit is the security warning — the pre-build documentation and the in-app driver list should state which drivers are compiled into this build and that they were reviewed as source rather than contained at runtime.
- Runtime plugin sandbox (RestrictedContext + ScopedCredentialProvider) :: RestrictedContext is a ContextWrapper that throws SecurityException for roughly thirty Context operations and allowlists seven system services. iOS has no per-module runtime capability restriction: all code in an app bundle shares one sandbox, one entitlement set, one container and one Keychain. Swift cannot deny one target access to CoreBluetooth, the file system or URLSession at runtime, and there is no getSystemService chokepoint to allowlist. The stated design philosophy — that safety enforcement comes from backend-synced SafetyLimits rather than blanket Context restriction — is the part that does survive intact. :: ALT: Compile-time containment in place of runtime containment: each driver lives in its own SPM target declaring only SafetyCore, PumpDriverAPI and CoreBluetooth as dependencies, so importing UIKit, URLSession or app internals is a build error rather than a caught SecurityException; back that with a CI lint over driver-target imports and a CODEOWNERS review gate on driver code. ScopedCredentialProvider's deliberate unencrypted-SharedPreferences compromise disappears entirely — on iOS every driver uses the same Keychain-backed store, which is strictly stronger than the Android runtime-plugin path and removes that documented revisit trigger.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (12)
- DevicePlugin (hardware) extension — connect/disconnect/scan contract :: Three Android assumptions break. (1) `address: String` is a MAC on Android; iOS never exposes a BLE MAC — a peripheral is identified by `CBPeripheral.identifier`, a UUID that is app-specific, device-specific and NOT stable across app reinstall. (2) CoreBluetooth's `connect(_:options:)` has no timeout at all and retries indefinitely, including across app suspension and relaunch — the opposite of Android's documented 30s convention. (3) `scanForPeripherals(withServices: nil)` discovers nothing while the app is backgrounded; background scans must pass explicit service UUIDs and always coalesce duplicates, and only UUID-filtered scans are restored after a background relaunch. Medtronic's 'ignores address entirely' case is unaffected. :: REC: Adopt DeviceIdentity and both connect forms. Use a driver-owned 30s timeout for user-initiated connect to preserve the Android contract, and the never-timing-out pending connect for auto-reconnect — that is CoreBluetooth's designed reconnect path and is strictly better than Android's backoff loop. `scan()` must always pass the driver's service UUID set; unfiltered scan becomes a documented foreground-only debug affordance. Keep `config` as a free-form string map with `pairingCode` preserved.
- ConnectionState machine :: The 7 states port, but iOS adds two conditions the enum cannot express. First, `CBManagerState` (poweredOff, unauthorized, unsupported, resetting) is a first-class, user-actionable condition: connect() cannot even be attempted and there is no state to show — Android reaches the same situation by a different path and the enum papers over it. Second, Core Bluetooth state restoration relaunches the app straight into an already-connected peripheral without passing through SCANNING/CONNECTING/AUTHENTICATING; `willRestoreState` fires before `centralManagerDidUpdateState`, in a short background window, and cannot resume arbitrary driver work — the driver must rebuild session and auth context from persisted credentials. :: REC: Option 2 plus option 3's synthetic transition, with no new public case. Keep the 7 names and the StateFlow-always-has-a-value guarantee for cross-platform parity, add the parallel availability stream (the PairingFault precedent is exactly this pattern), and emit synthetic transitions on restoration. AUTH_FAILED stays terminal-ish, requiring an explicit new connect(); explicit disconnect and give-up still land on DISCONNECTED.
- PairingProfile / PairingStyle / PairingFault — the ADVERTISE_AND_WAIT path :: CENTRAL_SCAN (Tandem) is fine. ADVERTISE_AND_WAIT (Medtronic MiniMed 700-series) collides with two hard CoreBluetooth rules. When backgrounded, `CBPeripheralManager` drops `CBAdvertisementDataLocalNameKey` entirely and moves service UUIDs into the overflow advertising area, discoverable only by iOS devices explicitly scanning for that UUID — a pump scanning by name will never see a backgrounded iPhone. And `advertisedName` is only settable in the foreground advertisement payload; the GAP Device Name a pump may actually read is the user's device name ('Dan's iPhone'), which an app cannot set. iOS also cannot clear a BLE bond programmatically. :: REC: Options 1 and 3 together, with 2 documented as the fallback. Implement the foreground-only pairing modal, keep `PairingProfile.advertisedName` as the displayed expectation, and extend PairingFault with iOS-specific cases — `bluetoothUnauthorized`, `bluetoothPoweredOff`, `backgroundAdvertisingNameless` — alongside the existing five. PERIPHERAL_UNSUPPORTED maps to `CBPeripheralManager.state == .unsupported`; ADVERTISE_FAILED to a failed `startAdvertising` callback; BOUND_ELSEWHERE stays the same heuristic timeout with no protocol signal.
- Device discovery models and Tandem model detection :: `TandemPumpModel.fromAdvertisedName` ports verbatim — `CBAdvertisementDataLocalNameKey` yields the same advertised string, and the whitespace-collapse, case-insensitive prefix match, empty-string and 'Tandem Pump' fallback-to-UNKNOWN behaviors are all unchanged, tests included. What breaks is `DiscoveredDevice.address` and the legacy `DiscoveredPump`: no MAC, a per-install UUID, and RSSI only available at discovery time (a connected peripheral requires an explicit `readRSSI()`, so `rssi` must stay optional and the legacy `rssi ?: 0` coercion becomes actively misleading). :: REC: Options 2 and 3. Persisting the serial alongside the peripheral UUID is what makes 'you reinstalled, we found your pump again' possible; without it, every reinstall or bundle-identifier change forces a full re-pair, which for Tandem means re-entering the pump pairing code from the pump screen. Delete DiscoveredPump. Keep the `metadata: [String: String]` bag on DiscoveredDevice for driver-specific advertisement data.
- GlucoseSource capability — the 60-second poll :: Both shipped drivers poll every 60,000 ms in an unbounded loop. iOS has no foreground service and suspends the app between Core Bluetooth callbacks: a self-scheduled Timer or `Task.sleep` does not fire while suspended, and BGAppRefreshTask cannot be scheduled tighter than roughly 15 minutes — opportunistic, system-decided, not guaranteed. Holding an open BLE connection wakes the app for peripheral events, not for its own timers. So an exact 60s cadence is achievable only in the foreground. Worse, the Android implementations swallow failures via success-only handling, so on iOS an indefinitely stale display would be indistinguishable from a quiet pump. :: REC: Option 2 as the architecture with option 3 as a supplement, and move cadence ownership out of the capability into a platform polling orchestrator — where Medtronic's KDoc already says it is headed. Explicitly do NOT port the swallow-failures behavior: surface poll failure and last-successful-read time, because iOS will produce far more gaps than Android and staleness must be visible rather than inferred.
- PumpStatus capability — unpair(), autoReconnectIfPaired(), and full history sync :: The six read methods and the three pure `extract*` functions port unchanged; drop-never-clamp is language-independent. Two lifecycle methods do not. An iOS app cannot remove a Bluetooth bond — only the user can, via Settings > Bluetooth > Forget This Device — so `unpair()` can clear credentials and disconnect but may leave a stale bond that blocks re-pairing with a fresh key. `autoReconnectIfPaired()` maps far better on iOS than Android because an indefinite pending connect plus state restoration does the work, so its shape should change too. Both currently sit on PUMP_STATUS, meaning a device driver without PUMP_STATUS has no unpair path at all. :: REC: All three. `unpair()` becomes `async throws -> UnpairResult` carrying `requiresSystemForget: Bool`; both lifecycle methods move to DevicePlugin where they belong. Keep `getFullHistoryLogs`'s defaulted delegation to `getHistoryLogs` (Tandem overrides, Medtronic does not) and run the unbounded variant under BGContinuedProcessingTask so a long Tandem backfill survives backgrounding. Fix the KDoc property names (glucoseLow/glucoseHigh/maxBolus do not exist) rather than porting them.
- PluginFactory and dual discovery :: Half of this capability disappears: the runtime path — no-arg constructor plus reflective instantiation from a sideloaded DEX JAR — is impossible on iOS. The compile-time path also has no direct equivalent: Swift and SPM have no Hilt `@Binds @IntoSet` multibinding, and Swift cannot enumerate types conforming to a protocol at runtime. Drivers must be registered in an explicit, hand-maintained list, which introduces a failure mode Android does not have: a driver that compiles but was never added to the list is silently absent from the app. :: REC: Option 1 plus option 3's test. The driver set is small and fixed, and a codegen build plugin adds complexity to a repo every user must build themselves. `metadata` stays available without instantiation as a `static var` on the factory type so the settings screen can list drivers without creating them, and the platform — not the factory — still calls `initialize(context)` with the same context instance handed to `create`.
- PluginMetadata and PLUGIN_API_VERSION :: The struct ports trivially, but the version check changes meaning. On Android it is a runtime gate rejecting a mismatched sideloaded JAR; with compile-time linking a driver can never be older than the app it is built into, so a runtime `apiVersion != 5` check is dead code. Its real iOS value is as a source-compatibility marker for out-of-tree forks carrying a private driver. This is also the moment to resolve the three-way drift — code says 5, docs/dev/plugin-architecture.md says 2, and the example README and META-INF/plugin.json say 1, which is why the shipped example would be rejected today. :: REC: Option 2 plus SPM semver on PumpDriverAPI, bumping major on breaking changes to Plugin/DevicePlugin, capability protocols, PluginContext parameters, PluginEvent cases, or descriptor cases — the same rule Android documents. Set the Swift constant to 5 and fix all three documentation sites in the same change; do not carry the drift into the new repo. Keep the reverse-domain plugin-ID regex and the 'Tandem v1.0.0' protocolName+version rendering convention.
- Deprecated pump interfaces (PumpDriver, PumpConnectionManager, PumpScanner, HistoryLogParser) and their registry adapters :: These four interfaces plus four adapters exist purely to keep pre-plugin Android call sites compiling. A green-field Swift port has no such call sites, so porting them imports roughly eight types of pure technical debt — including two genuine defects. HistoryLogParserAdapter returns an empty list when no pump plugin is active, indistinguishable from 'no new records', which masks data loss; and the deprecated `limits: SafetyLimits = SafetyLimits()` default parameters silently substitute absolute limits whenever a caller forgets to pass the user's narrowed configuration. :: REC: Option 3. Delete the deprecated surface entirely, and make 'no active pump plugin' a typed error everywhere — never an empty array, never a default-constructed SafetyLimits. Also delete the `SafetyLimits()` default parameter from every signature so the user's configured limits must be passed explicitly; that default is a latent safety bug rather than a convenience, and the port is the only cheap moment to remove it.
- Safety-constant drift guard (the 20..500 bound duplicated across ~15 sites) :: The Android guard works three ways, the third being a comment-stripped source scan for inline `20..500` literals across modules a test cannot import. That third mechanism is fragile, and the port is the moment to remove the need for it. Separately, the Android repo's static-analysis gates lean on Semgrep's Kotlin and Java rulesets, which have no Swift equivalent, so 'the same security testing in substance' must be reconstructed from different tools rather than by copying the workflow. :: REC: Option 3, and state the security-gate mapping plainly rather than by tool name. Semgrep parses Swift but there is no Swift security ruleset comparable to p/kotlin or p/java; the substance is recovered from MobSF for iOS/Swift static analysis, `xcodebuild analyze` (Clang static analyzer), SwiftLint custom rules encoding this project's own invariants, and Periphery for dead code. Language-agnostic gates port by name unchanged: gitleaks/trufflehog for secrets, zizmor for GitHub Actions, osv-scanner for Package.resolved (thinner Swift coverage than Maven — supplement with Dependabot's SPM support).
- Fidelity of the example driver as a reference :: On Android the demo plugin is a nice-to-have; on iOS it becomes load-bearing. Core Bluetooth does not exist in the iOS Simulator, and the lead developer has no iPhone and no Apple Watch, so every non-hardware validation runs in the Simulator. But the Android demo is a plain Plugin, not a DevicePlugin: no connect/disconnect/scan, no ConnectionState, no PairingProfile or PairingFault, no credential use, no DebugLogger, no CalibrationTarget participation, and no capability entry for its declared DATA_SYNC. Those are precisely the surfaces most in need of Simulator exercise. :: REC: Options 2 and 3, both shipped in the repo and both runnable in the Simulator and in CI. The scriptable simulator is how the connection state machine, pairing faults, restoration and safety filtering get automated coverage; the trace-replay driver is how parser correctness gets covered without a pump. Also fix the demo's apiVersion inconsistency and its use of the raw 20...500 literal instead of the configured limits.
- Build configuration and toolchain pins :: Nothing here survives literally: Gradle modules, compileSdk 35 / minSdk 30, jvmTarget 17, ProGuard consumer rules, the Gradle lockfile, kotlinx-coroutines exported as an api dependency, and the d8 DEX packaging step all disappear. The fork-and-build distribution model adds constraints Android does not have: bundle identifiers, App Group identifiers and Keychain access groups must be templated per user because every fork signs with a different Team ID, and the proguard keep-rules for capability reflection have no analogue because Swift capability lookup is not reflective. :: REC: Option 3. Pin Swift and Xcode versions in the CI workflow and in a `.xcode-version` file, and commit `Package.resolved` as the lockfile equivalent. SafetyCore must build for iOS, watchOS and the widget extension so no surface outside the phone app can construct an invalid reading. Templated TEAM_ID, BUNDLE_ID_PREFIX and APP_GROUP live in an xcconfig the forking user fills in once, following the Loop/LoopWorkspace pattern.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (15)
- Glucose validity bounds 20..500 mg/dL are the load-bearing invariant and are duplicated across ~15 sites (SafetyLimits.DEFAULT_/ABSOLUTE_MIN|MAX_GLUCOSE, CgmReading.MIN_MG_DL/MAX_MG_DL, BgmReading require, PluginEvent.CalibrationRequested require, CalibrationTarget.validateBgValue, plus app/wear sites). A drift-guard unit test (app/src/test/.../contract/SafetyConstantDriftGuardTest.kt) enumerates them; docs/contract/safety-constants.md is the source of truth. A Swift port must either consolidate to one constant or reproduce an equivalent guard.
- CgmReading, BgmReading and BolusEvent validate in their constructors and THROW. A driver that constructs one from unfiltered pump data crashes the coroutine instead of dropping a record. Swift value types must decide deliberately between throwing initializers and failable ones — and any change to failable silently converts crashes into dropped data.
- DROP, never CLAMP. All three PumpStatus.extract* methods and InsulinSource.getBolusHistory must discard out-of-range readings/doses rather than coerce them. A clamped 900 mg/dL becoming 500, or a clamped 40 U bolus becoming 25 U, would display fabricated clinical data.
- Two independent bolus caps must both be honored: BolusEvent.MAX_BOLUS_UNITS = 25f (type-level, units) and SafetyLimits.maxBolusDoseMilliunits (default/absolute 25_000 mU, user-narrowable). The milliunits-vs-units ×1000 scale difference between SafetyLimits and the reading models is an easy conversion bug with dosing-display consequences.
- SafetyLimits.safeOf() clamps rather than throws for backend input, and its ordering (min clamped to 20..499 first, max then coerced to clampedMin+1..500) means a nonsensical config like (min=450, max=300) silently yields a 1 mg/dL-wide accept window (450..451) with no warning — nearly all readings would then be dropped.
- SafetyLimits must be read from the StateFlow's CURRENT value on every validation pass, not cached at initialize(). ReadingSimulator documents this explicitly; caching would let a driver keep validating against revoked, wider limits after a backend narrowing.
- The PluginEventBus is lossy by design: MutableSharedFlow(extraBufferCapacity = 256, onBufferOverflow = DROP_OLDEST), no replay. It is NOT a delivery guarantee — NewGlucoseReading / NewBgmReading / CalibrationRequested can be silently dropped under burst, and blocked platform-only publishes return silently. Any alerting built on it needs its own guarantee.
- Glucose polling is fixed at 60_000 ms in both shipped GlucoseSource implementations, and failed polls are swallowed via `onSuccess` only — a persistently failing pump produces an indefinitely stale display with no distinguishing signal. Staleness must be derived from CgmReading.timestamp; nothing in the API surfaces it.
- CalibrationTarget.validateBgValue checks ABSOLUTE bounds (20..500), not the user's narrowed SafetyLimits, and the demo's manual-entry path also uses the raw 20..500 literal. Sending an out-of-user-range fingerstick as a CGM calibration is a direct patient-safety path.
- Medtronic's bolus cursor advances on a successful fetch BEFORE the orchestrator persists, so a transient save failure can skip a bolus on that path. This is explicitly tolerated only because the slow-loop raw-history sync (Room-persisted per sequence, restored on restart) is the durable path. Porting the fast path without the durable path would lose insulin records.
- HistoryLogParserAdapter returns an EMPTY list when no pump plugin is active, which is indistinguishable from 'no new records' — silent data loss rather than an error.
- DebugLogger carries raw glucose/insulin/device-identifier bytes; implementations must retain cargoHex/parsedValue only in debug builds and never persist them unencrypted.
