- Runtime plugins get UNencrypted credential storage (ScopedCredentialProvider) and load in-process with the app's classloader as parent — both accepted risks under the stated trust model, with documented revisit triggers.
- Activation deliberately calls onActivated() on the new plugin BEFORE deactivating the conflicting one, so two plugins transiently hold the same single-instance capability (e.g. two live GLUCOSE_SOURCEs). The window is intentional (slot never empty) but readings from both can arrive.
- The read-only capability model is the top-level safety architecture: no bolus/basal/therapeutic write surface exists anywhere in the SDK. Any port must preserve this closed capability set.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (45)
  Plugin base interface + lifecycle contract | DevicePlugin (hardware) extension | ConnectionState machine | PluginCapability enum and cardinality rules | Typed capability lookup (getCapability + as* helpers) | PluginMetadata and PLUGIN_API_VERSION | PluginFactory and dual discovery | PluginContext (driver-facing platform services) | PluginSettingsStore (driver-facing key-value settings) | PumpCredentialProvider (driver-facing secret storage) | DebugLogger (BLE packet tracing) | SafetyLimits value type and hard bounds | SafetyLimits.safeOf() lenient factory | SafetyLimitsStore (backend sync + staleness) | CgmReading model and validation | BgmReading model and validation | BolusEvent model and hard 25 U cap | Remaining domain models (insulin, status, history, hardware) | GlucoseSource capability | InsulinSource capability | PumpStatus capability and extract* safety contract | BgmSource capability | CalibrationTarget capability and BG validation | BolusCategoryProvider capability and Tandem mapping | PluginEvent hierarchy and validation | PluginEventBus and its platform implementation | PairingProfile / PairingStyle / PairingFault | Device discovery models and Tandem model detection | Dashboard card descriptors and CardElement validation | Settings descriptors and key validation | Detail screens and detail-action routing | PluginRegistry: creation, activation, mutual exclusion | PluginRegistry: capability slot state flows | Runtime plugin loading (DEX JAR sideloading) | Plugin JAR file management | Runtime plugin sandbox (RestrictedContext) | Plugin feature gate (kill switch) | Deprecated pump interfaces and their registry adapters | Read-only capability set (deliberate non-feature) | DemoGlucometerPlugin (reference driver) | DemoGlucometerPlugin UI surfaces | DemoGlucometerPlugin detail screen + manual entry | ReadingSimulator (synthetic BGM generator) | Fidelity of the example driver as a reference | Build configuration and toolchain pins


================================================================================
## Tandem t:slim X2 and Mobi BLE driver  [tandem-driver]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (28)
- A user must be able to discover a Tandem t:slim X2 or Tandem Mobi from the iPhone app by scanning for the pump's fdfb service while the pump is in pairing mode, seeing each discovered pump's name and signal strength, and being told plainly when nothing appears because the pump is not advertising, Bluetooth is off, or Bluetooth permission was denied.
- A user must be able to complete first-time pairing by entering the pump's pairing code (6-digit from the t:slim X2 screen, or the 16-character code printed on a Mobi), with the app selecting the JPAKE or V1 handshake automatically from the code length and never asking the user which protocol their pump speaks.
- The app must accept and complete an iOS Bluetooth pairing prompt as part of first connection, and must tell the user in advance that the prompt will appear and must be accepted for the pump to work.
- After a successful pairing the app must reconnect to the same pump automatically, without the pump being put back into pairing mode and without the user re-entering the code, for as long as the pairing is intact.
- The app must keep attempting to reconnect indefinitely while a pairing exists, only stopping when the user unpairs or when the app has positively determined the pairing is no longer valid — a medical monitoring app must never quietly give up on the pump.
- When the app determines the pairing is genuinely broken, it must say so distinctly (not as an ordinary disconnect) and give the user the exact recovery steps, including forgetting the pump in iOS Settings, since the app cannot remove an iOS Bluetooth pairing itself.
- The app must tolerate transient encryption and connection failures after at least one successful session without invalidating the pairing, so that sleep/wake and out-of-range events never strand a user without pump data.
- The app must surface pump connection status to the user as distinct, actionable states: connected, connecting, authenticating, reconnecting, disconnected, pairing invalid, Bluetooth off, and Bluetooth permission denied.
- While the app is in the foreground the user must see pump data refresh at Android-equivalent cadence, with insulin-on-board, basal rate and glucose visible within seconds of opening the app.
- While the app is backgrounded the system must continue collecting pump data opportunistically whenever iOS grants it Bluetooth runtime, and must back-fill any gap from the pump's history log once a session is re-established, so that no delivered bolus, basal change or glucose reading is permanently lost to a background gap.
- Every pump-derived value shown anywhere in the app or on the Watch must carry a visible data age, and values older than a configured threshold must be visually distinguished, because iOS cannot guarantee background refresh timing.
- The app must resume history collection from where it left off across app termination and system relaunch, so that a killed process does not cause repeated re-reads or skipped records.
- The app must read from the pump only. It must never issue a bolus, change a basal rate, alter a pump setting, or write to any pump control characteristic, and this must be enforced by the driver interface rather than by convention.
- The app must reject any glucose value outside 20–500 mg/dL, any bolus above 25 U, and any basal rate above 15 U/hr from every parsing path, live and historical, before that value reaches storage, the dashboard, alerts, or any AI analysis.
- The app must convert pump timestamps to the phone's current local time using the Tandem 2008 epoch and the pump's local-wall-clock semantics, producing timestamps identical to the Android app for the same pump bytes in any time zone.
- The app must report only completed insulin deliveries as delivered insulin, never started, cancelled or errored ones.
- The app must categorize each bolus (Control-IQ automatic, correction, food, food-and-correction, override, other) identically to the Android app for the same pump data.
- The app must read pump battery level and reservoir volume on both t:slim X2 and Mobi, and must never issue the battery request variant that is known to terminate a t:slim X2 session.
- The app must report the pump's model, serial number, firmware version and feature flags, and must continue to function with reduced detail when the pump does not return them.
- An Apple Watch user must see current glucose with trend, insulin on board, basal rate and pump connection status, delivered from the iPhone, on a complication and in the Smart Stack, each with a visible data age and an explicit indication when the phone has not been reachable.
- A maintainer must be able to run the complete pump protocol stack — framing, CRC, reassembly, both authentication handshakes, and every parser — as automated tests on a CI runner with no phone, no pump and no Bluetooth hardware.
- A developer with a Mac and a pump but no iPhone must be able to exercise the real driver against the real pump from macOS, covering pairing, authentication, status reads and history paging.
- A developer must be able to run the entire app in the iOS Simulator against a simulated pump that produces realistic protocol traffic, including pairing, reconnection, and failure cases such as bad pairing codes and dropped sessions.
- The pump pairing code and derived pairing secret must be stored so they are readable when the device is locked but only after first unlock, must never leave the device, and must never sync to iCloud or appear in an encrypted backup restored to another device.
- Diagnostic packet logs must be available to the user for troubleshooting, must never include glucose values, insulin doses, pump serial numbers or Bluetooth identifiers in system logs, and must not be persisted unencrypted.
- The repository's CI must gate every change to the pump driver on: static security analysis of Swift source, secret scanning, known-vulnerability scanning of pinned Swift package dependencies, lint, and the full protocol test suite including the published safety-constant and pump-time vectors.
- The project must document, and CI must verify, that the Swift EC-JPAKE implementation produces byte-identical handshake messages to the Android implementation for the same inputs.
- The app must ship the required Bluetooth usage description and Bluetooth central background mode, and must degrade to a clear explanatory state rather than a crash or a blank screen if Bluetooth permission is denied or restricted.

### IMPOSSIBLE (6)
- BluetoothGatt.refresh() — clearing the cached GATT service database on every connect :: There is no public or private CoreBluetooth API to invalidate the OS service cache, and no reflection escape hatch on iOS. iOS caches a bonded peripheral's service database and will not re-discover on its own. The Android driver calls refresh() on every STATE_CONNECTED specifically because a stale cache silently drops notifications — the exact failure mode iOS is also susceptible to. :: ALT: Rely on the standards-based mechanism: implement peripheral(_:didModifyServices:) and re-run discovery when the pump sends a GATT Service Changed indication (0x2A05). Handle characteristic references going invalid after any reconnect by re-discovering rather than caching CBCharacteristic objects across sessions. For a genuinely stale cache the only user-facing remedy is forgetting the device in iOS Settings, which iOS clears the cache on — so this becomes a documented troubleshooting step rather than an automatic recovery.
- BluetoothDevice.removeBond() — programmatic bond removal :: iOS exposes no API to enumerate, inspect or delete Bluetooth pairings. The user must do it manually in Settings → Bluetooth → Forget This Device. Three Android code paths depend on removeBond (status 0x05 before CONNECTED, status 0x08 with no prior successful session, and three zero-response connections) and none of them can be automated. :: ALT: Detect the same conditions with the same counters, then surface a specific, actionable recovery instruction instead of acting: clear app-side credentials, set AUTH_FAILED, and tell the user exactly which two things to do (forget the pump in iOS Settings, then put the pump into pairing mode and re-pair). Because the automatic remedy is gone, the Android restraint policy matters even more — do not trip this state on transient encryption failures once a successful session has occurred.
- requestConnectionPriority(CONNECTION_PRIORITY_HIGH) — forcing a 7.5–15 ms connection interval :: CoreBluetooth gives the central no control over connection interval, latency or supervision timeout. Only the peripheral may request parameters, via an L2CAP Connection Parameter Update Request, and iOS applies its own policy to what it accepts (Apple's accessory guidelines constrain acceptable intervals to multiples of 15 ms with a minimum of 15 ms). The Android code requests HIGH priority because the pump drops connections at slower intervals. :: ALT: None on the central side. The mitigating fact is that Tandem ships an official iOS t:connect app, so the pump firmware demonstrably negotiates workable parameters with iOS and requests them itself. Verify empirically on Daniel's hardware whether the session survives; if it does not, the only remaining lever is the connect → burst-read → disconnect cycle, which keeps each session short enough that interval-driven timeouts do not accumulate.
- requestMtu(185) and treating MTU negotiation failure as fatal :: iOS negotiates the ATT MTU automatically at connection setup and exposes no request API. There is no MTU callback and therefore no MTU-failure branch to implement. :: ALT: Functionally a non-issue and arguably an improvement: iOS requests an ATT MTU of 185 automatically (matching REQUIRED_MTU exactly), and the driver chunks every write at 18 bytes regardless. Replace the fatal MTU gate with a readiness assertion on peripheral.maximumWriteValueLength(for: .withResponse) >= 20, logged for diagnostics, and delete the close-GATT-and-give-up path that MTU failure triggered on Android.
- A foreground service holding a permanent connection with a guaranteed 15 s poll cadence in the background :: iOS has no foreground services and no user-visible persistent-work affordance that grants unbounded runtime. With the bluetooth-central background mode the connection object survives, but a suspended app cannot initiate a write; runtime is granted only when the peripheral generates an event, and this pump generates none unsolicited. BGAppRefreshTask (~30 s, system-scheduled, typically minutes to hours apart) and BGProcessingTask (charging/idle) cannot deliver a 15 s cadence. Battery-optimization exemptions and Samsung 'Sleeping apps' have no iOS analogue because the restriction is not a setting. :: ALT: Event-driven wakeups instead of timer-driven polling: disconnect after each read burst and leave an indefinite connect() outstanding so the next connection event wakes the app, plus BGProcessingTask for opportunistic history backfill. Accept and state plainly that background freshness is best-effort. The history log makes gaps recoverable, so the honest product promise is 'complete data with variable latency in the background, Android-equivalent cadence in the foreground', surfaced with an explicit data-age indicator rather than an implied-fresh number.
- Apple Watch connecting to the pump directly :: The pump accepts exactly one BLE connection at a time, so a watch-side connection necessarily steals it from the iPhone; there is no shared-connection mode. Independently, watchOS provides neither the bluetooth-central background mode nor CBCentralManager state restoration, so a watch-side driver could not sustain or recover a session even if it were the sole owner. This is a device constraint compounded by a platform one, not an engineering gap. :: ALT: iPhone owns the pump connection exclusively; the Watch receives readings over WatchConnectivity (updateApplicationContext for latest state, transferCurrentComplicationUserInfo for complication updates) and renders complications and Smart Stack entries from the last transferred snapshot with a visible data age. watchOS also has no custom watch faces, so the Android watchface module's role is served by complications on Apple's own faces plus Smart Stack widgets — a narrower surface that should be scoped as such rather than promised as parity.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (14)
- BLE scan, pump discovery, and stable device identity :: Android keys everything off the pump's MAC address, which iOS never exposes. CoreBluetooth gives a per-app, per-iOS-device CBPeripheral.identifier UUID instead. iOS also has no ScanSettings tiers, no location permission requirement, and background scanning is throttled and requires an explicit service-UUID filter. Separately, the pump only advertises while in pairing mode, so scanning is a pairing-time activity only — post-pairing reconnect must never depend on a scan result. :: REC: Do all three. Identifier is the primary key; the serial-number fingerprint is the recovery path. Keep the fdfb service filter (required for background scanning anyway), keep MAC-free dedup by identifier, keep DiscoveredPump(name from CBAdvertisementDataLocalNameKey, rssi). Drop the scan-failure error path — CoreBluetooth reports unavailability through CBManagerState, not a scan error code — and surface poweredOff/unauthorized/unsupported to the user instead. Add a scan timeout and a 'put the pump in pairing mode first' instruction, because iOS shows nothing at all until the pump advertises.
- Connection bring-up sequence :: Four of the six Android bring-up steps have no CoreBluetooth counterpart: GATT cache refresh, connection-priority request, MTU request, and explicit CCCD writes. iOS also bonds implicitly — the OS pairing dialog appears the first time an encrypted characteristic is touched, and cannot be triggered or observed programmatically. :: REC: All three. Keep the ordering guarantee that FFF9 notify is enabled and auth is started before the other five subscriptions, since that ordering is what beats the pump's idle timeout. Replace the MTU-failure-is-fatal branch with the write-length check (iOS auto-negotiates 185 ATT MTU, and the driver chunks at 18 bytes regardless, so the requirement is satisfied without an API). Treat discoverServices/discoverCharacteristics failure as a real recoverable error with a scheduled retry — the Android code only logs it and strands the connection, which is a latent bug worth fixing in the port.
- Connection state machine :: The seven-state enum is fine, but iOS adds mandatory states Android does not have: the central manager can be poweredOff, unauthorized, unsupported or resetting, and a restored-from-termination launch arrives in a state where peripherals exist but no user action initiated them. Rendering any of these as DISCONNECTED tells the user 'pump not connected' when the truth is 'Bluetooth is off' or 'you denied Bluetooth permission'. :: REC: Add the two distinct states. Drop SCANNING (never emitted today) or keep it and finally use it during pairing. Preserve every existing transition and preserve AUTH_FAILED as terminal for auto-reconnect. Preserve the 500 ms post-auth settle before promoting AUTHENTICATING → CONNECTED, and preserve the precondition that a status request throws unless state == CONNECTED.
- Tiered reconnection and backoff policy :: The FAST ladder (2/4/8/16/32 s then 32 s flat) and the SLOW 120 s timer both depend on the app running to fire a timer. On iOS a backgrounded app is suspended and neither Timer nor Task.sleep fires; BGTaskScheduler gives ~30 s of app-refresh at system-chosen times, nowhere near a 2 s ladder. The passive autoConnect=true GATT has no direct name on iOS but has an exact functional equivalent that is actually better. :: REC: Option 2. Foreground: keep the exact FAST ladder and its counters so user-visible reconnect behavior matches Android. Background: cancel timers, issue one indefinite connect(), and let CoreBluetooth do the kernel-side work — this is the true analogue of the SLOW-phase autoConnect GATT and preserves the medical-device 'never stop trying' policy without any CPU wake. Keep reconnectAttempt/phase state across both so the ladder resumes where it left off on foregrounding. Also port ReconnectSchedulerTest, and fix its known off-by-one (the real fast phase is attempts 1–9, ~190 s, not 1–10/222 s).
- Bond-loss and failure detection :: The entire policy is keyed on Android GATT status bytes 0x05, 0x08 and 0x13, which CoreBluetooth does not expose. iOS reports CBError/CBATTError domains with a coarser vocabulary (peerRemovedPairingInformation, encryptionTimedOut, connectionTimeout, peripheralDisconnected, insufficientAuthentication, insufficientEncryption) and gives no bond-state query at all — there is no equivalent of BOND_BONDED. :: REC: Hybrid. The three behavioral counters port cleanly and carry most of the value: rapidDisconnectCount (3 rapid pre-CONNECTED disconnects → AUTH_FAILED), encryptionFailureCount with MAX=20, and zeroResponseConnectionCount (3 connections with no notification → pairing invalid), all reset by a successful session or any notification. Critically, preserve the restraint: never treat a post-successful-session failure as bond loss, and tolerate 20 consecutive encryption failures before giving up. On iOS the cost of getting this wrong is higher than on Android, because the recovery action (forget the device in Settings, put the pump in pairing mode, re-pair) cannot be automated at all.
- Unpair and credential teardown :: unpair() today is disconnect + clear credentials + removeBond(). The removeBond half is impossible on iOS (listed separately); what needs redesign is the flow around it, because leaving a stale iOS pairing behind while clearing app credentials produces a state Android never has: the OS still holds a bond the app cannot see or use. :: REC: Option 1, non-blocking but prominent, with a deep link to App-Prefs Bluetooth if available and plain instructions if not. Keep the rest identical: cancel auto-reconnect, cancel auth/settle/reconnect tasks, clear the operation queue and pending requests, cancel the peripheral connection, set DISCONNECTED, clear Keychain items. Also expose a 'Repair pump' entry point that runs the same teardown, since on iOS re-pairing is the universal recovery for anything the driver cannot fix.
- Auth-success credential persistence :: Android stores the pairing code and the JPAKE derived secret + server nonce as hex through PumpCredentialProvider (EncryptedSharedPreferences). iOS has no equivalent; the choice of Keychain accessibility class directly determines whether background reconnection can authenticate, because the auth handshake must run in a background wake with the device possibly locked. :: REC: Option 1, and it is not a close call: WhenUnlocked breaks the product, and ThisDeviceOnly is mandatory so a pump pairing secret never syncs to another device via iCloud Keychain or a restored backup. Everything else in onAuthSuccess ports unchanged — cancel the auth timeout, zero all four counters, reset the phase to FAST, set hadSuccessfulSession, persist the pairing on first success, persist the JPAKE derived secret and server nonce (this is what enables confirmation-mode reconnect), then fire the init sequence and arm the 500 ms settle. Note that Keychain items survive app deletion, which is a genuine benefit for a fork-and-build audience that reinstalls often.
- EC-JPAKE cryptographic core :: BouncyCastle's ECNamedCurveTable/ECPoint arithmetic has no Swift equivalent. CryptoKit and swift-crypto expose P-256 only as ECDH/ECDSA primitives; neither exposes point addition, scalar multiplication of an arbitrary base point, or the raw field arithmetic the ZKPs need. ECDH can be abused to get a scalar-times-point but returns only the x-coordinate, which is insufficient. This is the single largest genuinely-new engineering item in the subsystem. :: REC: Option 1. The fork-and-build distribution model makes a pure-Swift, dependency-light package far more valuable than a marginally faster C one, and this code runs a handful of times per pairing so performance is irrelevant. Preserve every wire-format decision exactly: uncompressed 65-byte points with a 1-byte length prefix, scalars fixed at 32 bytes big-endian with a 1-byte length prefix (the fixed length is load-bearing — variable length broke the 165/330-byte split offsets ~1.6% of the time), curve id 23, ASCII ids client/server, ZKP hash = SHA-256 over length-prefixed G, V, X and the id reduced mod n, mulSecret blinded with a random 16-byte b as (b·n + s)·x mod n, derivedSecret = SHA-256 of K's x-coordinate. Validate with differential test vectors generated from the Kotlin implementation with a seeded RNG, plus a Kotlin-client-vs-Swift-server cross-handshake test. Use CryptoKit's P256 x963Representation only as an on-curve validity check for decoded points.
- Sustained connection, keep-alive cadence, and poll scheduling :: Everything the driver reads is polled — the pump pushes nothing unsolicited — and the pump drops idle connections at roughly 30 s. Android holds this open with a connectedDevice foreground service and a 15 s fast loop. iOS has no foreground services; with the bluetooth-central background mode the connection itself survives, but a suspended app cannot initiate a write, and CoreBluetooth only grants runtime when an event arrives from the peripheral. A poll-only peripheral therefore generates no wakeups, the app never runs, the 30 s idle timer expires, and the pump disconnects. :: REC: Option 1 as the architecture, with option 2 as a supplement for backfill. Design the driver around a connect → auth → burst-read (IoB, basal, CGM, plus history when due) → disconnect cycle whose whole duration fits comfortably inside a background wake, rather than a long-lived connection with a slow poll loop. Because the history log preserves everything, gaps between wakes are recoverable — this is exactly why the pagination cursor matters more on iOS than on Android. Do NOT promise Android's 15 s background cadence in the PRD; state honestly that background freshness is best-effort and foreground freshness matches Android. Also plan for a Live Activity or a time-sensitive notification as the user-visible signal that data has gone stale, since the app cannot silently guarantee freshness.
- Background relaunch and state restoration :: Android's process can be revived by a foreground service and its BLE callbacks survive. iOS instead relaunches a terminated app into the background on a CoreBluetooth event, but only if the central was created with CBCentralManagerOptionRestoreIdentifierKey, and willRestoreState hands back peripherals with no application context and only a few seconds of runtime — it cannot resume arbitrary work such as an in-flight history pagination loop or a half-finished JPAKE handshake. :: REC: Option 1, then let the ordinary connection flow take over once a real connect event arrives. Treat every restored connection as a fresh session: re-run authentication from scratch (Android already does this on every reconnect, so this is parity, not a compromise), reset all assemblers and pending requests, and never assume an in-flight request survived. The nextHistoryIndex cursor, which Android holds only in memory, must become durable on iOS precisely because the process is killed routinely — that is a real behavior improvement the port should make deliberately.
- Plugin surface and capability wiring :: TandemDevicePlugin is discovered at runtime through Hilt multibinding into a PluginFactory set. iOS forbids dynamic code loading, so the driver becomes a compile-time-linked Swift package conforming to a driver protocol, and registration must be a static list. :: REC: Option 2 — an explicit array is honest about what is linked and reviewable in a diff, which matters for a source-distributed medical monitoring app. Preserve the plugin's declared identity verbatim (id com.glycemicgpt.tandem, name, version, protocolName, description) and its four capabilities (GLUCOSE_SOURCE, INSULIN_SOURCE, PUMP_STATUS, BOLUS_CATEGORY_PROVIDER). Map onActivated → autoReconnectIfPaired and onDeactivated/shutdown → disconnect onto app lifecycle. Keep the empty settings descriptor and empty dashboard-card list — the generic pump card already renders pairing status and Unpair. Replace TandemGlucoseSource's naive 60 s polling flow with an AsyncStream fed by the shared connection scheduler, because two independent pollers competing for a single-connection pump is worse on iOS than on Android.
- Pump data on Apple Watch :: Not a limitation of watchOS BLE — watchOS does have CoreBluetooth — but of the pump: Tandem pumps accept exactly one BLE connection at a time, so a Watch that connects steals the connection from the iPhone. Compounding it, watchOS has no bluetooth-central background mode and no CBCentralManager state restoration, so a watch-side driver could not maintain a session anyway. :: REC: Option 1, unequivocally. Use WCSession updateApplicationContext for latest-value state (coalescing, survives being backgrounded) and transferCurrentComplicationUserInfo for complication-driving updates, with a defined budget-exhaustion fallback. Complications and Smart Stack widgets render from the last transferred snapshot with an explicit staleness age — never a stale number with no age. Option 2 should be explicitly ruled out in the PRD: connection stealing during a handoff race would leave both devices unable to read the pump.
- Simulator-untestable driver validation :: CoreBluetooth does not exist in the iOS Simulator, and the lead developer has a pump and a Mac but no iPhone. Without a substitute, essentially none of this subsystem is exercisable by the person writing it, and every change would queue behind the one maintainer with hardware. :: REC: Both 1 and 2, and treat them as first-class deliverables rather than test scaffolding. Define the driver's dependency on CoreBluetooth as a narrow protocol (scan, connect, discover, subscribe, write, notify, disconnect, state) so a FakeTransport can drive the entire stack in unit tests and a SimulatedPump can drive the whole app in the Simulator. The macOS bench is the higher-leverage item: it lets the lead developer validate real JPAKE pairing, real framing and real history paging against his own pump with no iPhone at all, leaving Daniel's hardware for iOS-specific behavior — background wakes, state restoration, Keychain-while-locked, watch delivery — which is exactly the set the Mac cannot cover. Caveat the bench honestly: the pump's single-connection limit means the Mac and the iPhone cannot both be paired-and-connected at once.
- Security and dependency gates for this module :: The Android gates for this code are Semgrep --config p/kotlin,p/java,p/secrets, OSV-Scanner v2.3.3 over committed Gradle lockfiles, and a pinned bcprov-jdk18on 1.84. Semgrep's registry has no maintained Swift ruleset, so 'run Semgrep' is not portable; the requirement is the substance, not the tool name. :: REC: CodeQL(swift) + Semgrep p/secrets + OSV-Scanner on a committed, exact-pinned Package.resolved + SwiftLint, and state plainly in the PRD that CodeQL's Swift coverage is narrower than Semgrep's Kotlin rules — the compensating control is that this module's risk surface is memory-safe parsing and crypto, both of which are better covered by the ported vector tests and a differential test against the Kotlin EC-JPAKE than by any generic ruleset. Two Android-specific gates simply disappear: consumer ProGuard rules (no R8) and the instrumented-test requirement for Base64 (Foundation Base64 unit-tests on CI). Note that if the pure-Swift EC option is chosen there is no third-party crypto dependency left to scan at all, which is a meaningful supply-chain improvement over pinning BouncyCastle.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (13)
- Glucose validity bounds 20..500 mg/dL are enforced in four independent places for this driver: SafetyLimits (min/max plus absolute ceilings that configuration cannot widen), parseCgmEgvResponse, parseCgmEventPayload, parseCgmG7EventPayload, and again by CgmReading's own init require. A widened or dropped check admits sensor garbage into the dashboard, alerts and AI analysis. The bound is a cross-repo constant guarded by SafetyConstantDriftGuardTest and mirrored in the backend.
- TANDEM_EPOCH_OFFSET = 1199145600 plus the deliberate ZoneOffset.UTC → ZoneId.systemDefault() two-step. Getting this wrong shifts every CGM/bolus/basal timestamp by the phone's UTC offset (up to 14 h), which corrupts staleness detection, alert timing, IOB decay reasoning and chart ordering. Verified vectors exist for negative, positive and zero offset zones.
- TandemBleDriver.getCgmStatus calls StatusResponseParser.parseCgmEgvResponse(cargo) WITHOUT passing SafetyLimits, so the live CGM read path silently validates against the 20..500 defaults rather than the user's configured limits. Only the history-extraction path receives the caller's limits. Any port must decide deliberately which behavior is intended.
- The live basal read (parseBasalStatusResponse, opcode 41) applies no rate ceiling at all — unlike the history path, which rejects commandedRate > maxBasalRateMilliunits (15 000 mu/hr). A corrupt or misframed 9-byte cargo can display an arbitrary basal rate up to 4.29e6 U/hr.
- Insulin unit conversions: IOB, basal rate, bolus volume and correction/meal portions are all milliunits ÷ 1000f. Reservoir (InsulinStatusResponse) is the exception — it is WHOLE UNITS as a uint16 and must NOT be divided. Mixing these up misreports dose or reservoir by 1000x.
- Bolus dose cap: parseLastBolusStatusResponse and parseBolusDeliveryPayload both reject > maxBolusDoseMilliunits (default/absolute 25 000 mu = 25 U); BolusEvent's init throws above 25 U. Only bolusStatusId==3 (COMPLETED) and deliveryStatus==0 (Completed) are reported — Started/Canceled/Error events must never be counted as delivered insulin.
- BLE protocol correctness: CRC-16/CCITT-FALSE (0x1021, init 0xFFFF, little-endian on the wire) is the only integrity check on every message; a CRC bug would let corrupted glucose or dose bytes through as valid. PacketAssembler has no length check against the declared cargo length and no stall timeout, so a lost final chunk leaves a partial buffer that is only cleared by a txId change or an explicit reset.
- txId is 8-bit and wraps every 256 requests. Status responses are matched purely by txId with no opcode check, so a late response from a wrapped-around transaction could in principle satisfy a different pending request. The code mitigates by evicting/cancelling colliding pending entries but does not verify that the response opcode matches the request opcode + 1.
- JPAKE round-4 key confirmation compares the server HMAC with ByteArray.contentEquals, which is not constant-time; and the V1 handshake never verifies the pump's centralChallengeHash (bytes 2-21 of the opcode-17 response), so the V1 path authenticates the client to the pump but not the pump to the client.
- Bond-loss policy is patient-affecting: removeBond() forces a manual re-pair that requires the pump to be physically put into pairing mode. The code deliberately NEVER removes the bond on INSUFFICIENT_ENCRYPTION once hadSuccessfulSession is true, tolerating up to 20 consecutive failures first, because sleep/wake renegotiation failures self-resolve. Reproducing that restraint matters — an aggressive iOS port would strand users without pump data.
- Battery opcode ordering: V2 (144) is known to return GATT_ERROR 133 on t:slim X2 and kill the connection before any fallback can run. V1 (52) must be tried first; V2 is a fallback only. Reversing the order breaks the whole session, not just the battery read.
- No staleness signal on several readings: IoBReading, BasalReading (live), ReservoirReading and BatteryStatus all carry Instant.now() rather than a pump timestamp, so a stale value cached by a failing poll is indistinguishable from a fresh one at the model layer. Only CGM and bolus carry true pump time.
- History cursoring correctness: opcode 58's firstSeq/lastSeq are record INDICES, not event sequence numbers, and opcode 60 takes a start INDEX. Treating them as sequence numbers silently skips or re-reads records. The sinceSequence argument to getHistoryLogs is not used to filter the 26-byte FFF8 path at all — deduplication relies entirely on the DB insert's IGNORE on a unique timestamp index.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (40)
  GATT service and characteristic UUID map | BLE scan and pump discovery | Tandem message framing (Packetize.encode) | CRC-16/CCITT-FALSE | Inbound packet reassembly (PacketAssembler) | Connection bring-up sequence | Serialized GATT operation queue | Authentication mode selection (V1 vs JPAKE, bootstrap vs confirmation) | JPAKE handshake state machine | JPAKE round 3/4 session key and confirmation | EC-JPAKE cryptographic core | HKDF and HMAC helpers | V1 legacy authentication (firmware < 7.7) | Auth success: credential persistence and session init | Post-auth initialization sequence | Connection state machine | Tiered reconnection and backoff policy | Bond-loss and failure detection | Unpair, bond removal, and GATT cache refresh | Status request/response transport on FFF6 | History-log stream transport on FFF8 | History-log pagination and progressive cursoring | HistoryLogRequest cargo encoding (opcode 60) | TANDEM_EPOCH_OFFSET and pump-time conversion | IoB read (opcode 108/109) | Basal read and activity-mode caching | Live CGM read (EGV opcode 34/35 + HomeScreenMirror 56/57) | Last bolus status read (opcode 48/49, V1 and V2) | Reservoir and battery reads (t:slim X2 vs Mobi divergence) | Pump version, features and settings reads | History record formats and stream cargo parsing | CGM extraction from history logs | Bolus extraction from history logs (event 280) | Basal extraction from history logs (event 279) | Bolus category derivation and platform mapping | SafetyLimits validation bounds | Plugin surface and capability wiring | Read-only design constraint | Debug packet logging | Opcode table (complete, as implemented)


================================================================================
## Medtronic 680G/770G/780G read-only driver  [medtronic-driver]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (27)
- A user must be able to pair a MiniMed 680G/770G/780G by opening a pairing screen in the app, seeing the phone become discoverable as 'Mobile 000001', selecting that name on the pump, and watching the app move through waiting -> connecting -> connected, without entering any code, creating a Medtronic account, or using a browser.
- The pairing screen must state, before the user starts, that the pump must first be removed from MiniMed Mobile or any other phone, and that the app must stay open and in the foreground for the whole first-time pairing.
- The system must surface each distinct pairing failure with its own message and a Try Again action: no pump has connected yet (60 s first-pair window elapsed), Bluetooth is off or permission denied, could not start advertising, the secure handshake timed out (30 s), and the pump rejected the secure handshake.
- A terminal handshake rejection must latch: the app must stop advertising, must not auto-reconnect, and must require the user to explicitly retry pairing.
- Once paired, the app must reconnect to the pump on its own whenever the app is running, and must tell the user when a reconnect has not succeeded for a sustained period rather than silently showing stale data.
- The app must be able to receive and serve pump-initiated Bluetooth events while backgrounded, including being relaunched into the background by the system after termination, and must re-establish a fresh secure session on every such relaunch without requiring the user to re-pair.
- The system must never persist secure-session cipher state across app termination; a restored connection must always begin a new handshake.
- A user must be able to unpair the pump from within the app, and the app must then instruct the user to also forget the device in iOS Settings, because the app cannot remove the Bluetooth bond itself.
- The app must read, from the pump only: sensor glucose with a trend arrow, insulin on board, active basal rate, delivered bolus history, reservoir volume, pump battery percentage, and pump model/serial/firmware identity.
- The app must never write any therapeutic value to the pump. This must be enforced at three independent layers: a transport allow-list permitting writes only to RACP 0x2A52, CGM SOCP 0x2AAC and IDD SRCP 0x105; construction of GET/report-class opcodes only; and a driver that declares no control capability.
- The app must reject rather than clamp any out-of-range physiological value: sensor glucose outside 20–500 mg/dL, reservoir outside 0–300 IU, IOB outside 0–100 IU, basal above 15 U/h, bolus above 25 U, battery outside 1–100%.
- The app must reject any value decoded from a reserved IEEE-11073 no-value sentinel and must never present it as a reading or as a trend arrow.
- A malformed, truncated, or hostile pump response must produce a reported read failure, never an application crash and never a fabricated reading.
- The app must never advance a history cursor past a record the pump actually sent: an undecodable frame must fail its page, a failed page must fail the whole history call, the paging walk must be driven by the requested sequence window rather than by page content, and a recovered final record must pass a structural-completeness check before acceptance.
- The app must serialize all pump exchanges so that only one is in flight at a time, and must guarantee that a long history backfill cannot starve the recurring glucose read.
- A cancelled or timed-out exchange must leave no live notification subscription behind, so the next exchange starts from a clean session state.
- Glucose must be handled in mg/dL end to end; no mmol/L conversion may be introduced without a verified sample from a mmol/L-display pump.
- History timestamps must be resolved from the pump's naive local reference time interpreted in the phone's current time zone; records with no resolvable reference time must be dropped rather than shown with a guessed time.
- SmartGuard auto-basal micro-boluses must remain excluded from bolus totals, and the app must state this limitation to the user.
- Pump pairing identity must persist in the Keychain with an accessibility class that permits reading after first unlock, so a background reconnect works while the device is locked.
- No log line at warning level or above may contain a health value, a raw pump payload, or a peer device identifier, because warning-level logs are forwarded to error reporting.
- The full driver below the Bluetooth transport — state machine, handshake, session reader, gateway, all parsers, all extractors and all safety gates — must be exercisable in the iOS Simulator against a simulated transport, and the secure-handshake implementation must be verified in CI against a captured real-pump pairing trace with no hardware present.
- A maintainer must be able to fork the repository, supply their own Apple Developer credentials as repository secrets, and get a signed build delivered to their own TestFlight from GitHub Actions, with no project-published binary and no App Store submission.
- The build must ship with UIBackgroundModes bluetooth-central and bluetooth-peripheral, NSBluetoothAlwaysUsageDescription, and Core Bluetooth restore identifiers on both the central and peripheral managers.
- CI must gate on: a pinned secrets scan, a repo-authored Swift static-analysis rule set covering the driver's read-only allow-list and its numeric-safety and log-redaction invariants, Swift 6 strict concurrency with warnings as errors, a committed Package.resolved treated as the lockfile, and a dependency advisory scan — failing on high-severity findings only and failing closed if a scanner crashes.
- The Apple Watch must display sensor glucose, IOB, active basal, reservoir, battery and pump connection state sourced from the phone or the backend, and must never attempt to connect to the pump itself.
- The connection and pairing state must be visible to the user without opening the app, via a Live Activity while a session is active and via a time-sensitive notification when the pump link has been lost long enough to matter.

### IMPOSSIBLE (6)
- BLE advertisement manufacturer-specific data (company ID 0x01F9, payload 0x00 || ASCII(localName) || 0x00) :: CBPeripheralManager.startAdvertising supports exactly two advertisement keys — CBAdvertisementDataLocalNameKey and CBAdvertisementDataServiceUUIDsKey. Apple provides no API to emit manufacturer-specific data, service data, or any other AD type from the peripheral role, on any iOS version, with or without an entitlement. :: ALT: Carry the identity in CBAdvertisementDataLocalNameKey ('Mobile 000001') alongside the mode-appropriate service UUID and rely on the pump's Regex('Mobile .{0,7}') name match. The strong supporting evidence is that Medtronic's own MiniMed Mobile for iOS pairs with these same pumps under the identical Apple restriction, which means the pump's discovery filter cannot require manufacturer data. This must nonetheless be treated as unproven until hardware-validated.
- Advertising mode, TX power, timeout and connectable-flag control (ADVERTISE_MODE_LOW_LATENCY vs BALANCED, ADVERTISE_TX_POWER_MEDIUM, timeout=0, setIncludeTxPowerLevel) :: Core Bluetooth exposes no advertising interval, PHY, TX power, duty cycle, timeout or connectability parameters. iOS chooses the advertising interval itself and varies it with app state, device state and radio contention. There is no entitlement or private-adjacent API for this. :: ALT: Reduce advertisingModeFor(paired, forceFirstPair) to selecting only the advertised service UUID (SIG 0xFE82 first-pair vs 0xFE81 reconnect) and delete the interval/power knobs. The RECONNECT rationale (paired pumps scan infrequently and miss long advertising intervals) becomes an unmitigable risk on iOS rather than a tunable, and must be recorded as such.
- Discoverability by the pump while the app is backgrounded :: When an iOS app advertises from the background, Core Bluetooth ignores CBAdvertisementDataLocalNameKey entirely and moves all service UUIDs into a proprietary 'overflow' area that only iOS devices explicitly scanning for those exact UUIDs can decode. A Medtronic pump scanning for a name matching 'Mobile .{0,7}' or for service 0xFE82 therefore cannot discover a backgrounded iPhone. This is documented Apple behavior and cannot be configured or entitled around. :: ALT: First-time pairing must require the app to be open and in the foreground, stated explicitly in the pairing doc and enforced in the UI (start advertising only from the visible pairing screen). For reconnection after a bond exists, the connectable advertisement is still emitted in the background and a central that reconnects by stored peer identity rather than by name will still find it — which is what Medtronic's own iOS app must rely on — but this is unverified for this pump and cannot be assumed. Provide a time-sensitive local notification prompting the user to open the app when the link has been down beyond a threshold.
- Programmatic bond removal on unpair (BluetoothDevice.removeBond() via reflection) :: iOS exposes no public or private-adjacent API to remove a Bluetooth bond. Bonds are system-owned and can only be cleared by the user in Settings > Bluetooth > (i) > Forget This Device, or by erasing the device. There is no entitlement that grants this. :: ALT: unpair() clears the app-side Keychain pairing record, tears down advertising and the session, sets autoReconnect=false, and then presents an explicit two-step instruction to the user to forget 'Mobile 000001' (or the pump entry) in iOS Settings, ideally with a deep link to App-Prefs or at minimum a screenshot. The pairing doc and the failure-message table must both be updated, because a stale bond that the app believes is cleared is a real support-load hazard.
- Consuming org.openminimed:javasake:0.2.0 (and org.bouncycastle:bcprov-jdk18on:1.84) as a binary dependency :: There is no JVM on iOS and no supported way to load or execute Java bytecode; iOS additionally forbids dynamic code loading, which is the same rule that turned the plugin architecture into compile-time modules. Neither the SAKE artifact nor BouncyCastle can be bridged, wrapped, or transpiled into a shipping iOS build. :: ALT: A clean-room Swift reimplementation of the SAKE state machine, Session, SeqCrypt, KeyDatabase and DeviceType, with AES-CMAC implemented per RFC 4493 over CommonCrypto's AES-ECB primitive and AES-CTR via CCCryptorCreateWithMode. GPL-3.0 permits this outright and the iOS project is GPL-3.0, so copyleft propagation remains a non-issue; each Swift file must cite its upstream OpenMinimed source file exactly as the Kotlin port does, and palmarci's attribution must carry into the iOS THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES equivalent. Acceptance is byte-parity against OpenMinimed's captured 780G pairing trace, replayed in XCTest with a deterministic RNG — the same gate the JVM spike used.
- Hosting the Medtronic driver on Apple Watch (watchOS as the pump's BLE peer) :: watchOS provides no bluetooth-peripheral background mode and no supported path for sustained BLE peripheral advertising; watchOS apps are aggressively suspended outside extended runtime sessions, which are user-initiated and time-boxed. Independently of the API surface, the pump binds to exactly one phone at a time, so a watch-hosted peer would displace the iPhone rather than supplement it. :: ALT: The iPhone remains the sole pump peer. The Watch app receives sensor glucose, IOB, active basal, reservoir, battery and connection state over WatchConnectivity from the phone, with the backend as a secondary source when the phone is unreachable, and surfaces them in complications and the Smart Stack. Note that watchOS has no custom watch faces, so the Android watch-face path has no analogue and complications plus a Smart Stack widget are the ceiling.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (11)
- Inverted BLE topology: phone as peripheral, and the second GATT-client connection back to the pump :: CBPeripheralManager can advertise and host a GATT server, so the peripheral half is supported. The break is the handoff: Android captures the pump's BluetoothDevice directly from the GATT-server callback and calls connectGatt on it. Core Bluetooth gives you only a CBCentral, which exposes nothing but an opaque identifier and maximumUpdateValueLength and cannot be converted into a CBPeripheral. There is no documented API to obtain a CBPeripheral for the peer that just connected to your peripheral manager, and the map's own constraint forbids a fresh scan/advertise cycle. :: REC: Implement option 1 as primary with option 2 as automatic fallback, behind a single MedtronicPumpLinkResolver seam with a distinct fault code when both fail. This is the highest-risk unknown in the entire port and must be the first thing validated on real hardware; every parser and reader below it is worthless if the client link cannot be established.
- Advertising contract (what the pump scans for) :: CBPeripheralManager.startAdvertising honors exactly two keys: CBAdvertisementDataLocalNameKey and CBAdvertisementDataServiceUUIDsKey. Manufacturer-specific data (company ID 0x01F9 with the 0x00||name||0x00 payload), TX power inclusion, connectable flag, advertising timeout, and the LOW_LATENCY-vs-BALANCED mode distinction between reconnect and first-pair are all unavailable. iOS also always advertises the GAP local name, inverting Android's deliberate setIncludeDeviceName(false). :: REC: Advertise localName + the mode-appropriate service UUID; keep advertisingModeFor(paired, forceFirstPair) selecting only the service UUID and delete the tx-power/timeout/mode knobs with a comment naming the Apple restriction. Retain the 31-byte reasoning as a doc note only — iOS enforces the budget itself and will silently truncate a long local name.
- GATT-client transport: serialization, blocking operations and timeouts (AndroidMedtronicGattLink) :: The Android transport takes a ReentrantLock and blocks the calling thread until the matching GATT callback fires. On iOS that pattern is fatal: Core Bluetooth delivers all callbacks on the delegate queue, so blocking that queue deadlocks the entire BLE stack, and the driver deliberately shares one serial queue across the peripheral manager, the handshake driver and the client link. :: REC: Go fully async/await with an explicit FIFO gate. Preserve every safety property literally: connect lazily on first use (never requestMtu, which iOS forbids anyway), publish the peripheral and service index only after discovery succeeds, reject any callback whose CBPeripheral is not the current one so a late completion cannot satisfy a later operation's slot, and keep the synthetic -1 LOCAL_TIMEOUT / -2 LOCAL_REJECTED statuses and the CBATTError-to-name mapping for WARN logs. The API 33 write-overload branch and the literal CCCD payloads disappear — setNotifyValue(_:for:) replaces both.
- Dangling-subscription watchdog and cancellation cleanup :: The Android design exists to keep a coroutine cancellation from leaving a subscription live and desyncing the SAKE sequence counter, and it carefully defers blocking CCCD disables off kotlinx's global timeout thread. Swift's cancellation model, the absence of a global timeout thread, and setNotifyValue's async nature all change the shape, but the underlying hazard is identical and the guarantee must be preserved exactly. :: REC: Do all three: cancellation handler drops handlers immediately (the no-dangling-notification guarantee), disables are deferred onto the driver queue, and a 30_000 ms watchdog Task backstops a subscription whose exchange never completes. Keep both identity guards verbatim — skip the disable if the peripheral was replaced by a reconnect, and skip it if the same resolved characteristic has been re-subscribed by a newer exchange.
- MedtronicReadGateway: single-flight, timeouts and history paging :: kotlinx.coroutines.Mutex is documented FIFO-fair, and the gateway depends on that fairness so a long history backfill cannot starve a 15-second CGM keep-alive read. Swift has no fair async mutex: actors make no ordering guarantee and can reorder awaiting continuations. withTimeout and runInterruptible also have no direct Swift equivalents. :: REC: Build a small FIFOAsyncLock type and unit-test its ordering. Everything else ports as-is: resolve link and session BEFORE queueing so 'not connected' fails fast, wrap each read in a 30_000 ms timeout race producing a typed failure, keep the 200-record HISTORY_BATCH_SIZE window walk driven by the requested window and never by page content, keep empty-page-ends-the-walk, keep failed-page-fails-the-whole-call, keep the 50_000 total cap, keep releasing readMutex between pages, and keep the WARN-logs-carry-only-op-and-error-type rule (glucose, IOB, basal and reservoir values are PHI and Sentry ingests WARN).
- Background execution, state preservation and restoration, and the absence of a foreground service :: iOS has no foreground service. An app with UIBackgroundModes bluetooth-central and bluetooth-peripheral is still suspended between BLE events and woken for roughly 10 seconds per event; Timer and DispatchQueue.asyncAfter do not fire while suspended, so the orchestrator's 15-second poll (which doubles as the keep-alive against the pump's ~30 s idle drop) cannot run on a timer in the background. State restoration relaunches the app but hands back only restored peripherals/services/advertisement data — it cannot resume arbitrary work, and the in-memory SAKE session (including its sequence counters) is unrecoverable across termination. :: REC: Event-driven background operation with both background modes and restoration on both managers, plus a Live Activity for connection state. Hard rule for the port: NEVER persist SAKE session cipher state — on restoration always re-run the handshake, which the existing design already supports since a pump resubscribing to the SAKE characteristic restarts it. Restoration handlers must rebuild the state machine explicitly and reassign every delegate.
- Pairing persistence, auto-reconnect and identity :: There is no MAC address on iOS. The stored 'address' becomes CBCentral.identifier / CBPeripheral.identifier, an opaque UUID that is stable per app installation and per peer bond but is NOT stable across app reinstall or after the user forgets the device in Settings. Android's savePairing(address, localName) and getPairedAddress semantics therefore change durability characteristics, and the reconnect path must survive an identifier that no longer resolves. :: REC: Keychain-persist {peer UUID, advertised local name, pump DIS serial} with the serial as the durable identity and the UUID as a cache. Keep isPaired gating reconnectIfPaired, keep disconnect() preserving pairing, keep the SAKE-has-no-pairing-code note that the credential 'code' slot holds the advertised identity string, and keep the never-log-identifiers-above-DEBUG rule (it now applies to the peer UUID, not a MAC).
- Driver registration, DI wiring and lifecycle ownership (plugin -> compile-time module) :: iOS forbids dynamic code loading, so the runtime PluginRegistry / PluginFactory / Hilt @Binds @IntoSet discovery mechanism has no iOS analogue. This is already a decided architectural change; the redesign work is preserving the capability surface and lifecycle contract on the other side of it. :: REC: An SPM target MedtronicDriver depending on a PumpDriverAPI target (the port of :pump-driver-api: SafetyLimits, ConnectionState, CgmReading/CgmTrend, IoBReading, BasalReading, BolusEvent, BatteryStatus, ReservoirReading, PumpSettings, PumpHardwareInfo, HistoryLogRecord, PairingProfile/PairingStyle/PairingFault, PluginSettingsDescriptor). Preserve everything user-visible: id 'com.glycemicgpt.medtronic', the three capabilities GLUCOSE_SOURCE / INSULIN_SOURCE / PUMP_STATUS and nothing else, PairingProfile(ADVERTISE_AND_WAIT, 'Mobile 000001'), the fault stream published separately from the state stream, connect(address:config:) ignoring address and honoring forceFirstPair, and the single 'single_peer_note' Connection-section InfoText rendered in SwiftUI. Hilt's singleton graph becomes one composition root that owns the shared serial queue, the peripheral manager, the central manager, the connection manager, the link and the gateway, and keeps the gated linkProvider that returns nil unless state == CONNECTED.
- JavaSake handshake and SeqCrypt — cryptographic reimplementation in Swift :: Classified as impossible to consume as a dependency (see below); what needs designing is the replacement. Apple ships no AES-CMAC: CryptoKit has HMAC and Poly1305 but no CMAC, and CommonCrypto exposes no public CMAC either. The SAKE server-role state machine (stages 0->1->3->5->6), Session, SeqCrypt with its per-direction sequence counters, KeyDatabase and DeviceType all need Swift equivalents. :: REC: Option 1: a self-contained MedtronicSAKE SPM target with no third-party crypto dependency, AES primitives from CommonCrypto only, and every file citing its upstream OpenMinimed source file exactly as the Kotlin port does. Preserve the phone-as-SAKE-server / pump-as-SAKE-client role assignment, HANDSHAKE_COMPLETE_STAGE = 6, MESSAGE_SIZE = 20, newWakeUpFrame() allocating fresh zeros each call, the serverCrypt-encrypts-outbound / clientCrypt-decrypts-inbound direction mapping with its CipherDirectionConfirmationTest, the throw-before-stage-6 guard, and the not-thread-safe / one-queue-only contract. The bcprov 1.84 pin and the consumer ProGuard keep rules have no iOS analogue and simply disappear.
- Semgrep Kotlin/Java security gate and the AesEcb exclusion :: Semgrep parses Swift but ships no registry ruleset comparable to p/kotlin or p/java, so the Kotlin/Java coverage does not transfer at all. Meanwhile the Swift port will contain intentional AES-ECB (as the CMAC block primitive) that any crypto-misuse rule will flag, reproducing the original exclusion problem in a new toolchain. :: REC: Satisfy the requirement in substance: (a) Semgrep pinned, p/secrets retained, plus a repo-authored Swift rule pack whose highest-value rules are subsystem-specific — no BLE write outside the WritableControlPoint allow-list, no Double-to-Int conversion without a preceding isFinite guard, no unchecked Data subscripting in ble/read, no health value or peer identifier in a log call at warning level or above; (b) gitleaks; (c) Swift 6 strict concurrency as a hard gate; (d) MobSF wired but advisory until a write surface exists; (e) fail on HIGH/ERROR only, WARNING/INFO printed, crashed scanner fails closed — identical gate policy. Document the intentional AES-ECB in an ADR and suppress it with an annotated inline directive rather than a path exclusion, since the Swift CMAC is authored in-tree and will be patched rather than re-vendored.
- tools/medtronic-ble-spike offline de-risk harness and the Simulator test strategy :: Core Bluetooth does not exist in the iOS Simulator (CBManagerState is always .unsupported), and the lead developer has no iPhone. The JVM spike proved two gating unknowns offline; the Swift port needs the equivalent, and iOS adds two new unknowns the JVM spike never covered (the client-link handoff and iOS-shaped advertising). :: REC: Do 1 and 2 as hard prerequisites before any hardware session — they cover the SAKE handshake, all parsers, all safety gates, the reassembler, the paging walk and the state machine, which is the large majority of the subsystem. The Android peripheral-probe README's known-negative note (emulator has no radio) becomes the iOS Simulator's equivalent and must be stated as loudly. Accept that advertising, the client-link handoff, bonding behavior and background reconnect are hardware-only.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (15)
- GLUCOSE VALIDATION — REJECT, NEVER CLAMP. CgmReader rejects any SG outside SafetyLimits 20..500 mg/dL with a MedtronicReadException before constructing CgmReading (whose init re-asserts 20..500). History-extracted CGM readings outside the same bounds are DROPPED. A wrong SG is explicitly deemed more dangerous than a missing one. The Swift port must preserve reject-not-clamp and the ordering (sentinel check -> round -> range check).
- SFLOAT SENTINEL HANDLING. decodeMedFloat16 returns NaN/±Infinity for the reserved IEEE-11073 mantissa codes 0x07FE, 0x07FF, 0x0800, 0x0801, 0x0802 — a divergence from upstream added specifically so a 'no value' sentinel cannot surface as a real glucose or as a bogus trend arrow (trend is only magnitude-bucketed). CgmReader explicitly rejects non-finite glucose. Losing this in the port silently turns sentinels into readings.
- MEDFLOAT32 HAS NO SENTINEL HANDLING. decodeMedFloat32 (used for reservoir, IOB, basal rate, bolus units) deliberately does NOT special-case reserved codes. Every caller MUST gate with isFinite() plus a physiological range check: reservoir 0..300 IU, IOB 0..100 IU, basal >= 0 and <= SafetyLimits.maxBasalRateMilliunits (15_000 mU/h), bolus total <= maxBolusDoseMilliunits (25_000 mU) and <= BolusEvent.MAX_BOLUS_UNITS (25 U). Dropping any of these gates lets a garbled read surface as a real insulin/reservoir number.
- UNIT CONVERSION. Glucose is canonical mg/dL end to end; NO mmol/L conversion exists in this driver and one must not be added without a real European mmol/L-display sample (documented decision, tracked in #809/#708). If ever added, use the shared 18.0156 factor and preserve the reject-outside-20..500 ordering. History BG readings ARE converted: SFLOAT kg/L * 1e5 = mg/dL. Basal/bolus math converts IU to milliunits by *1000 then roundToInt before comparing to limits.
- HISTORY CURSOR SKIP HAZARD (the single most dangerous class of bug here). Multiple layers exist solely to stop a history cursor advancing past data the pump actually sent, which would permanently lose bolus records feeding IOB: (a) any undecodable frame in a page fails the whole page; (b) a failed page fails the whole getHistoryLogs call rather than returning partial newer pages; (c) the paging walk is driven by the requested window, never by page content; (d) a flush-recovered unterminated final record must pass isStructurallyCompleteRecordFrame or the exchange fails loudly; (e) a 'no records found' terminator arriving after streamed frames fails the read. All five must be reproduced.
- BLE PROTOCOL CORRECTNESS — UUID BASES. The Medtronic vendor base uses variant field 0000 (0000XXXX-0000-1000-0000-009132591325), not the SIG 8000. The SAKE characteristic must be vendor-base 0xFE82 while the service is SIG-base 0xFE82, and the phone's DIS container must be vendor 0x0900 not SIG 0x180A. Getting either wrong causes the pump to find the service but not the characteristic, so the handshake never begins and no data is ever read (issue #844).
- RACP SERVICE SCOPING. The SIG 0x2A52 RACP is exposed under BOTH the CGM and IDD services. Resolving it to the wrong service silently reads the wrong log — i.e. glucose data interpreted as insulin history or vice versa. The implementation prefers the last-resolved RACP-bearing service, falls back to an actively-subscribed service, and THROWS rather than guessing. It also refuses to let a Battery/Device-Info read move the scoping context.
- STALENESS / TIMESTAMPS. CGM and IDD status readings are stamped with the phone's now() at read time, NOT a pump-provided capture time (report-last-record returns the most recent measurement; per-record absolute time via CGM Session Start Time is a deferred refinement). History records carry only relative offsets anchored to a naive-local NGP reference time interpreted in ZoneId.systemDefault(); DST gap/overlap and a phone/pump timezone mismatch are explicitly unresolved. Records before any reference time are dropped rather than mis-timestamped.
- SENSOR STATUS NOT YET GATED. Open TODO(48.C2/F) in CgmReader: the CGM Measurement status/annunciation byte is parsed but NOT used, so a sensor in warm-up or an error state can still surface a misleading SG. SensorMessageState (WARM_UP, NO_SENSOR_SIGNAL, CHANGE_SENSOR, SG_BELOW/ABOVE_LIMIT, etc.) is decoded in the IDD status path but not wired into glucose gating.
- READ-ONLY GUARANTEE. Three independent layers: the transport's WRITABLE_CONTROL_POINTS allow-list (RACP, CGM SOCP, IDD SRCP only), only GET/report-class opcodes constructed anywhere, and a plugin that declares no control capability. Any weakening of the allow-list makes a therapeutic write physically possible. Note the documented revisit trigger: introducing ANY write/therapeutic surface requires raising the SAST/threat model.
- SMARTGUARD MICRO-BOLUS ATTRIBUTION GAP. AUTO_BASAL_DELIVERY (0xF001) events are deliberately excluded from bolus extraction because bolus-vs-basal attribution and double-count risk are unvalidated. On 770G/780G this means displayed bolus history under-reports delivered insulin. The user doc states this. Do not 'fix' it in the port by guessing an attribution.
- BOLUS ACTIVATION CORRELATION IS A POSITIONAL HEURISTIC. isAutomated is taken from the record immediately following BOLUS_DELIVERED_P1 in sequence order; a dropped or interleaved record makes the join miss and the bolus silently degrade to manual/non-correction rather than failing. Correction/meal reason is joined by bolus id across programmed-P1/P2. Whole mapping is flagged TODO(48.A2).
- BATTERY 0% IS REJECTED. Valid range is 1..100; 0x00 is treated as spurious (a pump at true 0% over an active link would be powered off). isCharging is hard-coded false.
- SESSION CIPHER SEQUENCE DESYNC. Each notification PDU is individually SAKE-encrypted with its own 3-byte trailer and decrypting advances a sequence counter. A late or duplicate notification decrypted after an exchange finishes would consume the next operation's sequence slot and desync the session (guarded by per-exchange `finished` flags, the subscription watchdog, and the gateway's single-flight mutex). All three guards are load-bearing.
- AUTH_FAILED IS LATCHED. A terminal handshake failure must not be reset by the inevitable pump disconnect and must not start a reconnect loop against a pump that just rejected us; the user has to re-pair. Advertising is stopped on both the auth-failure and handshake-timeout paths.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (42)
  Inverted BLE topology: phone is the peripheral, pump is the central | Advertising contract (what the pump scans for) | UUID base scheme: SIG base vs Medtronic vendor base (issue #844 fix) | Full GATT UUID inventory | Phone-side GATT server registration order and placeholder DIS values | SAKE CCCD subscribe/unsubscribe detection on the phone's GATT server | Connection state machine | Connection faults and their timers | Pairing persistence, auto-reconnect and unpair | Discovery is advertise-and-wait, not a scan | SAKE handshake wire choreography (SakeHandshakeDriver) | MedtronicSakeSession: JavaSake wrapper and session keying model | JavaSake / OpenMinimed dependency and pinned versions | AesEcb Semgrep exclusion (documented security decision) | Static SAKE key-database posture | SerialWorker: single-thread serialization discipline | MTU policy and PDU framing | AndroidMedtronicGattLink: GATT-client transport, serialization and timeouts | Read-only enforcement in the transport (defense in depth) | Shared RACP characteristic service-scoping | Dangling-subscription watchdog and cancellation cleanup | NotificationReassembler and the exact-multiple ambiguity | MedtronicSessionReader: the six control-point exchange patterns | MedtronicReadGateway: single-flight, timeouts and history paging | CgmReader: sensor glucose read path and safety gating | CGM trend-arrow mapping | CGM Measurement record parser (SIG GSS 3.43) | CGM Feature characteristic parser | MedtronicCodec: numeric decoding primitives | IDD Features parsing and per-model capability tiering | IDD Status / IOB / active-basal parsing and validation bounds | Battery and Device Information readers (unencrypted SIG reads) | HistoryReader: IDD RACP record fetch | History record framing, event types and per-event parsing | History timestamp resolution (relative offsets + NGP reference time) | Domain extraction from history (CGM / bolus / basal) and the SmartGuard exclusion | MedtronicDevicePlugin: capability surface and pairing UX contract | Capability delegates: glucose, insulin (bolus cursor), pump status | Device-info mapping onto the shared (Tandem-shaped) domain models | Hilt DI wiring and lifecycle ownership | User-facing pairing procedure (what the user must do ON THE PUMP) | tools/medtronic-ble-spike: offline de-risk harness


================================================================================
## Pairing UX and connection lifecycle surface  [pairing-and-connection-ux]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (32)
- The app must let a user reach a dedicated pairing flow from Settings via a pump card that shows current pairing status, offering Pair Pump when unpaired and Re-pair when paired, and must return the user to Settings automatically once the pump reaches the connected state.
- The pairing flow presented must be determined by the active pump driver, supporting both a phone-scans-and-user-enters-a-code flow and a phone-is-discoverable-and-waits flow, and must change live when the user selects a different driver in Settings.
- All pairing screen copy, blurbs, and code-entry instructions must be supplied by the active driver rather than hard-coded to one vendor, so a driver is never described with another vendor's instructions.
- The app must expose a single connection state with the seven states disconnected, scanning, connecting, authenticating, authFailed, connected, and reconnecting, consumed identically by the pairing flow, the home status row, the debug view, the polling gate, and the read gateway.
- During a scan the user must see nearby pumps identified by name and detected model with a live-updating signal strength, ordered strongest-first, with devices removed from the list when they stop advertising, and must be able to stop the scan at any time.
- A scan must stop automatically after a bounded period of no user interaction so the radio is not left running indefinitely when the user leaves the pairing screen open.
- When a scan cannot start, the user must be told which specific condition prevents it — Bluetooth turned off, Bluetooth permission denied, or Bluetooth unavailable on this device — rather than the scan silently ending.
- The user must be able to enter a pairing code of 6 to 16 characters with no character-class restriction, no truncation below 16, and no auto-correction or auto-capitalisation, and must be able to edit and resubmit that code after a rejection without re-selecting the pump.
- The user must be told, before the system Bluetooth prompt appears, why the app needs Bluetooth, and if permission is denied must be given a one-tap route into the app's iOS Settings pane, with the denial state clearing automatically when they return with permission granted.
- While the app is connecting or authenticating the user must be able to cancel and return to the previous step, and the app must surface a retry path if the attempt has not resolved within a bounded time.
- When pairing is rejected, the user must be shown which category of failure occurred — wrong or expired code, pump no longer trusts this phone, secure handshake timed out, pump already paired to another phone, or Bluetooth unavailable — not a single generic failure message.
- When the pump has removed its pairing information for this phone, the app must present step-by-step instructions to forget the device in iOS Settings and then retry pairing, and must verify recovery afterwards.
- The app must never present the forget-device instruction, or treat a disconnection as a trust failure, when a successful authenticated session has previously been established with that pump and the user has not unpaired.
- Once paired, the app must attempt to reconnect to the pump indefinitely, with no attempt limit and no give-up condition, until the user explicitly unpairs.
- The app must maintain and re-establish the pump connection while backgrounded and while the device is locked, and must resume that connection automatically after the app is evicted by the system or after the device reboots and is unlocked.
- The app must clearly and permanently inform the user that force-quitting the app from the App Switcher stops background pump monitoring until the app is opened again, and must notify the user if a prolonged gap in background activity is detected.
- The user must be able to see current pump connection status from outside the app while paired, on the Lock Screen and Dynamic Island, including whether the phone is currently watching for lows and highs.
- When the app is not watching for lows and highs, that degraded state must be communicated on a surface that does not expire, and the app must never display reassuring alert-status copy when the true status is degraded or unknown.
- For a driver that requires the phone to be discoverable to the pump, the flow must tell the user that the app must remain open and on screen while waiting, and must state that reconnection after a drop also requires reopening the app.
- The user must be warned before pairing that a pump connects to only one phone at a time and that the pump must be removed from the manufacturer's official app first, on both the pairing screen and the Settings pump card.
- Unpairing must have one behaviour regardless of where it is initiated: cancel the connection, stop any advertising, erase that driver's stored credentials, end any external status display, and then instruct the user to complete removal in iOS Settings.
- Unpairing must require an explicit confirmation before it takes effect.
- Pairing credentials must be readable by the app while the device is locked so overnight reconnection works, must not leave the device, and must not be restored onto a different device.
- Credentials for one pump driver must be isolated from every other driver, so selecting a different driver never overwrites or exposes another driver's pairing.
- The app must never display a raw device identifier as the user-facing name of a paired pump; it must identify the pump by name and model.
- A failed reconnection or authentication that occurs while the user is not on the pairing screen must be surfaced to the user rather than being silently absorbed.
- The connection status shown on the home screen must be tappable when pairing has failed, taking the user directly to the pairing flow.
- The app must present a background-monitoring health check in Settings covering notification authorization, Bluetooth authorization, Low Power Mode, Background App Refresh, and force-quit status, with a route to resolve each.
- A maintainer must be able to exercise every pairing state, every failure mode, and the full reconnection lifecycle in the iOS Simulator through a simulated pump driver, without Bluetooth hardware.
- A maintainer must be able to capture a real pairing and reconnection session on physical hardware and replay it as an automated regression test.
- The watch app must expose no pairing, scanning, code entry, or unpairing capability, and must display forwarded pump connection status only.
- The app must state in-product and in documentation which pump drivers are validated against physical hardware on iOS and which are unvalidated, and must not present an unvalidated driver as production-ready.

### IMPOSSIBLE (8)
- Programmatic removal of the OS Bluetooth bond (removeBond via reflection, both drivers) :: iOS provides no public or private API to remove a Bluetooth pairing from the app. Bond removal is exclusively a user action in Settings → Bluetooth → ⓘ → Forget This Device. This is not a missing convenience: three of the Tandem driver's safety-relevant recovery paths (status 0x05 before connect, status 0x08 with no prior session, three consecutive zero-response connections) and both drivers' unpair paths call removeBond, and a stale bond is exactly what blocks a successful re-pair. Android's hidden-API reflection has no analogue — and private-API use would additionally be caught by App Store Connect's static analysis on TestFlight upload, breaking the fork-and-build distribution model. :: ALT: Detect the condition and hand the user an explicit, non-dismissible guided remediation: a sheet stating the pump no longer trusts this phone, with numbered steps for Settings → Bluetooth → ⓘ → Forget This Device and an Open Settings button, then a Retry Pairing action that verifies recovery. `CBError.peerRemovedPairingInformation` makes the most important case reliably detectable — better than Android's status-byte guessing. Preserve the load-bearing counter-rule in the new form: never show the forget-device instruction when `hadSuccessfulSession` is true, because transient encryption renegotiation across sleep/wake resolves itself and a needless forget forces a physical re-pair.
- GATT service-cache invalidation (BluetoothGatt.refresh()) :: Android calls `refresh()` by reflection on every connect specifically to prevent a stale cached characteristic table from silently dropping notifications — a failure mode that presents as a connected pump delivering no data. iOS caches discovered services and characteristics per bonded peripheral and exposes no way to flush that cache. The only invalidation paths are the peripheral sending a GATT Service Changed indication or the user forgetting the device. :: ALT: Handle `peripheral(_:didModifyServices:)` and re-run discovery for the invalidated services, and re-discover services on every fresh connection rather than caching handles in app memory. Then detect the symptom rather than pre-empt it: reuse the existing MAX_ZERO_RESPONSE_CONNECTIONS = 3 heuristic — reaching `.connected` three times with zero notifications received — and route it to the guided Forget This Device flow above. This turns an invisible silent-data-loss failure into an actionable one, which is arguably better than Android's fail-soft `refresh()` that logs a WARN and continues.
