================================================================================
## Release, promotion, signing, and dependency management  [release-and-signing]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (33)
- A maintainer can promote develop to main with a single command, and that one push fans out to version bumping, changelog PR creation, and cherry-pick sync-back, with the same message-string trigger predicates preventing any loop.
- The repository has exactly one version source of truth, bumped automatically from conventional commits, and the iOS app, watchOS app, and every extension always report the identical marketing version.
- Every build uploaded to App Store Connect carries a build number strictly greater than any previously uploaded for that developer account, derived so that it cannot regress when a fork is recreated, a run counter resets, or a maintainer archives locally before archiving in CI.
- A user can fork the repository, add a documented fixed set of secrets to their fork, and produce a build in their own TestFlight without editing any source file.
- Bundle identifiers, App Group containers, and keychain access groups are derived from the building user's Apple Team ID so two independent users never collide in Apple's global App ID namespace.
- A one-time setup workflow registers the App IDs the app needs with exactly the capabilities it uses, and reports which entitlements Apple must grant by application rather than automatically.
- The build pipeline hard-fails, never warns, if the archive is not signed by an Apple Distribution identity for the configured team, if the embedded profile permits debugging, or if the profile is expired.
- A user can validate their signing credentials in isolation — App Store Connect key authenticates, private certificate repository decrypts — without running a build and without any secret value appearing in logs.
- A user can re-run their build on demand and on a recurring schedule so their installed build never lapses past Apple's 90-day TestFlight expiry.
- The app shows the version and commit it was built from, its build date, and the number of days until that build expires, and tells the user how to refresh it.
- The app can tell the user that a newer upstream release exists than the one it was built from and deep-link to TestFlight; it never downloads, stores, or executes code obtained at runtime.
- A user can choose whether their build comes from the stable branch or the development branch, and the running app states which channel it came from.
- Upstream CI publishes iOS Simulator and watchOS Simulator application bundles for every release and for the development channel, so a maintainer with no physical iPhone or Apple Watch can install and exercise the full app in a paired simulator.
- Every direct and transitive Swift package dependency is pinned to an exact version and revision in a committed lockfile, and any build whose lockfile does not match its manifests fails loudly rather than silently re-resolving.
- The Ruby release toolchain is pinned by a committed lockfile and every CI invocation runs through it.
- A bot dependency pull request that changes package manifests has its lockfile regenerated automatically on an Apple toolchain and pushed back to the same pull request, or fails visibly; the job that pushes never executes pull-request-authored code or hooks.
- Dependency updates touching cryptography, secure storage, database encryption, the build toolchain, CI actions, prerelease packages, or any major version can never merge without human approval.
- A required status check refuses to let any bot pull request auto-merge if it changes a file outside the set that the build and dependency-scan gates actually cover.
- Every dependency is checked for known vulnerabilities on every dependency change and on a schedule, and a scanner that crashes fails the gate closed rather than passing.
- Every redistributed dependency's license is checked against an explicit allowlist and the build fails on any license outside it, with GPL-incompatible licenses permanently absent from that list.
- Swift and watchOS sources are statically analyzed for security defects on every pull request by a gate that blocks on high-severity findings, surfaces lower-severity findings without blocking, and reports files it could not parse.
- Secrets are scanned across the repository on every pull request.
- Every workflow definition is linted and security-audited, and every third-party action is pinned to a full commit SHA, with violations blocking merge.
- Every commit is developer-certificate-signed and free of AI attribution trailers, enforced both by a local hook and server-side so an uninstalled hook cannot bypass it.
- Changed-path and commit-type labels are applied automatically to every pull request and drive both the changelog taxonomy and the dependency auto-merge policy.
- Changes to signing configuration, entitlements, release automation, dependency policy, and the safety-critical pump driver trees require the project lead's review.
- No build a user runs ever contains a project-owned telemetry or analytics credential; crash reporting is off by default and can only be enabled with the building user's own credential.
- The Xcode version, Swift toolchain, and every CLI tool used by CI are pinned in the repository and are the same on a maintainer's machine as in CI, with a mismatch failing the build.
- A maintainer can boot a paired iPhone and Apple Watch simulator, install both apps, and stream filtered subsystem logs with a single command.
- Bluetooth-dependent behavior is exercised either on physical hardware by the hardware maintainer or against an in-process simulated pump driver, and every CI gate states explicitly that it does not cover real Bluetooth.
- A fork can pull upstream changes and rebuild without manual merge work, and can see which upstream release it is currently behind.
- The upstream project holds no signing key, certificate, provisioning profile, or Apple credential belonging to itself or to any user.
- Compile-time inclusion of an individual pump driver is controlled by an in-repository build setting that removes the driver from the binary and from the user interface entirely.

### IMPOSSIBLE (4)
- In-app phone update channel client (AppUpdateChecker: download APK, hand to package installer, self-update) :: iOS has no user-invocable package installer and no sideload path for a normal Apple Developer Program account. There is no equivalent of `Intent.ACTION_VIEW` with `application/vnd.android.package-archive`, no FileProvider handoff to an installer, and no 'install unknown apps' permission. `itms-services://` over-the-air installation requires an Apple Developer *Enterprise* Program membership, which requires 100+ employees and whose terms forbid distribution outside the organization — not available to this project or to individual users. App Store review guideline 2.5.2 independently forbids an app downloading and executing code. Every downstream detail (the anchored `^GlycemicGPT-(\d+\.\d+\.\d+)-release\.apk$` selector, `matches.singleOrNull()`, the expectedVersion-equals-tag check, `parseDevRunNumber`, the 403/404 message mapping) has no target to attach to. :: ALT: TestFlight is the update mechanism. Users enable TestFlight's automatic-update toggle and receive whatever their own CI uploads. The app keeps a read-only version *notifier*: it knows the tag and short SHA it was built from, queries the upstream GitHub Releases API for the newest release, and if that is newer shows a non-blocking notice with a deep link to TestFlight (`itms-beta://`). Preserve the two safety properties of the Android selector in the new context: the notice must state the exact version it is comparing against (no ambiguous match), and it must fail silent rather than fail wrong when the API is unreachable or rate-limited.
- APK download hardening (HTTPS enforcement in code, github.com/objects.githubusercontent.com host allowlist, filename sanitization, expected-size verification, cache-dir cleanup) :: This is defense-in-depth around a code-execution path that does not exist on iOS. With no downloadable installable artifact, there is no download to harden, no cacheDir/apk_updates or wear_apk_updates to purge, no filename to sanitize, and no size check to perform. The 100 MB `MAX_DOWNLOAD_SIZE_BYTES` cap likewise has nothing to cap. Retaining the code as-is would be dead code implying a protection that is not being exercised. :: ALT: Apple's own signing and delivery chain is the replacement trust anchor: TestFlight serves only builds signed by the user's Apple Distribution certificate and validated by App Store Connect. Carry the *principles* to the surfaces that do exist on iOS — App Transport Security left at its secure default with any LAN/cleartext exception scoped by NSExceptionDomains to the specific self-hosted backend host rather than NSAllowsArbitraryLoads, an explicit in-code https-and-host check on backend and Nightscout-mediated endpoints, and a Semgrep/CodeQL rule that fails the build on any arbitrary-loads flag. Keep the CI gate that asserts ATS was not weakened.
- Wear APK update client (WearAppUpdateChecker: phone downloads a Wear APK and pushes it to the watch) :: watchOS gives no third-party mechanism to install or update an app on the watch. A watchOS app is embedded inside the iOS application bundle and is installed, updated, and removed by the system together with its host app; WatchConnectivity transfers files to the watch app's sandbox and can never install executable code. The Android design — a separate `GlycemicGPT-Wear-*.apk` asset selected by prefix and suffix, its own `wear_apk_updates` cache, its own run-number comparison against `watchDevBuildNumber` — has no addressable target. Note also that the Android bug this would import (`firstOrNull` instead of the phone selector's anchored `singleOrNull`) is moot. :: ALT: Version lockstep by construction: the watchOS app's CFBundleShortVersionString must equal the host iOS app's, and both ship in one .ipa, so 'the watch is out of date relative to the phone' is structurally impossible after a successful TestFlight install. The app should still surface watch-app reachability and installed-version state via WCSession (`isWatchAppInstalled`, `isPaired`, `activationState`) so a user can see when the watch app has not finished installing, and tell them to open the Watch app on iPhone rather than offering an update action.
- Watch face artifact publication and its signing carve-out (GlycemicGPT-WatchFace-Digital/Analog APKs, WFF assets, WatchFacePusher, the deliberate exclusion of watch-face APKs from the certificate assertion) :: watchOS has no third-party watch face API. Apple ships no SDK for creating, installing, or pushing a custom watch face; faces are Apple's own and third parties cannot add to that set. Consequently there are no watch-face build products to name, sign, upload, or exclude from a certificate assertion, and the runtime tamper check that compares committed WFF asset SHA-256s baked into BuildConfig has nothing to verify. The entire watchface Gradle module, its `digitalFull`/`analogMechanical` flavors, its unconditional debug signingConfig, and its `vcsInfo { include = false }` all disappear. :: ALT: WidgetKit complications in the accessory families (accessoryCircular, accessoryCorner, accessoryRectangular, accessoryInline) placed by the user on any built-in Apple watch face, plus a Smart Stack widget for glanceable data without a face slot. These are signed as part of the app bundle by the user's own Distribution certificate, so they are covered by the same signing assertion as everything else — which is strictly better than the Android carve-out, where the published watch-face APKs were debug-signed. Data refresh is governed by WidgetKit timeline reloads and watchOS budgets, not by a push mechanism.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (19)
- Release artifact build and asset naming (four APKs -> one signed .ipa per user) :: There is no project-published binary. Each user builds and signs into their own Apple Developer account and their own TestFlight, so upstream CI cannot produce an installable artifact at all. The watchOS app ships embedded inside the iOS .ipa and is installed/updated by the system with it, so the phone/wear/two-watchface asset matrix collapses to a single archive. All the find+head-1 renaming, the `GlycemicGPT-<VERSION>-release.apk` naming contract, and `gh release upload --clobber` disappear from the release path. :: REC: Do both of the first two. Upstream release.yml tags, writes notes, and publishes Simulator .app bundles for iOS + watchOS with 90-day retention — that is the only artifact the lead (no iPhone, no Watch) can actually run, and it is unsigned so it needs no credentials. The user-facing artifact is produced solely by the fork's `build.yml` and lands in the user's own TestFlight. Keep the invariant that a doc-only promotion produces no build.
- Release signing-certificate assertion (hardcoded RELEASE_SIGNER_SHA256) :: The equality form is impossible on iOS: every user signs with their own Apple Distribution certificate, so there is no project-wide fingerprint to compare against. But the hazard it guards is real and has an exact analogue — `xcodebuild` can silently archive with an automatically-selected Development identity, producing an artifact that is not distribution-signed. The check must become a property assertion, not a constant comparison, and it must stay a hard fail. :: REC: Option 1, as a mandatory pre-upload step that exits non-zero (never warns), mirroring rule 4's 'wear build failure is fatal at the cert step'. `get-task-allow == false` is the precise iOS equivalent of 'this is not a debug-signed build'. Do not depend on ASC rejection alone — it produces a late, opaque failure and burns the user's build number. Record the check's provenance in-repo, which the Android side never did (`docs/dev/parity-gate-runbook.md` is referenced three times and does not exist).
- Gradle signingConfigs and the silent debug-signing fallback :: No keystores, no aliases, no store passwords. iOS signing is a certificate + provisioning profile pair issued by the builder's own Apple Developer account and selected by Xcode build settings. The Android failure mode (release buildType silently falling back to `signingConfigs.getByName("debug")` when RELEASE_KEYSTORE_FILE is unset) reappears in a different shape as Xcode automatic signing quietly picking a Development identity or a wildcard profile. :: REC: fastlane match, with signing settings pinned in a committed xcconfig (CODE_SIGN_STYLE = Manual, PROVISIONING_PROFILE_SPECIFIER from the match profile name, DEVELOPMENT_TEAM = $(TEAMID)). Automatic signing is exactly the ambiguity that produced the Android debug-fallback bug. Bundle identifiers, App Group ids, and keychain access groups must be templated on `$(DEVELOPMENT_TEAM)` because Apple's App ID namespace is globally unique across all developer accounts — two users cannot both own `com.glycemicgpt.mobile`.
- op-load-signing-secrets composite action :: Its job — materialize a shared project keystore into $RUNNER_TEMP at mode 0600 from a project-controlled 1Password vault — is structurally incompatible with fork-and-build. Upstream must hold zero signing material, because every user signs with their own credentials and upstream never produces a binary. :: REC: Delete it and replace with option 3's validate-only action, invoked by the Validate Secrets workflow and by the build workflow's preflight. Preserve two of its design properties: fail-closed before any build step when a required credential is missing (so a build can never proceed unsigned or wrongly signed), and an `if: always()` cleanup step in the caller that removes the decrypted keychain and profiles, since composite actions still cannot register post-job steps.
- 1Password secret model (vault github / item android-signing) :: The single-source-of-truth secret item and the read-only `ANDROID_ACTIONS_SERVICE_ACCOUNT` token assume one signing identity owned by the project. On iOS the secret set is per-user and lives in that user's fork, and its contents are different in kind (an App Store Connect API .p8, an issuer UUID, a key id, a team id, a match passphrase, a PAT for the private match repo). :: REC: Option 1, with option 2 as an optional local convenience. Option 3 is a non-starter: it would require the project to hold users' Apple credentials. Carry over three properties that made the Android model good: names that map 1:1 onto what the build actually consumes (no translation layer), a non-sensitive canary-equivalent so plumbing can be proven without touching a real key, and a written rotation story — here 'revoke the ASC key in App Store Connect and replace three fork secrets', with no certificate re-issuance.
- versionCode derivation (major*1_000_000 + minor*10_000 + patch) and its update-comparison use :: Safety-critical on Android: the packed integer collides silently at patch > 9999 or minor > 99 and would make the updater report 'up to date' forever. iOS has no packed version integer — it has CFBundleShortVersionString (marketing, X.Y.Z) and CFBundleVersion (build). App Store Connect enforces the real constraint: CFBundleVersion must be strictly greater than any previously uploaded build for that app record, per Apple ID. The Android formula should not be reproduced; reproducing it would import the collision bug for no benefit. :: REC: Option 2. A run-number-derived build number regresses whenever a user recreates their fork, resets the workflow, or archives locally in Xcode before archiving in CI — and App Store Connect then permanently rejects uploads for that marketing version. A clock-derived value is monotonic across all of those. Never reuse the Android packed formula. Document a manual override for the rare user who has already uploaded a higher number.
- DEV_RUN_NUMBER_OFFSET = 500 (permanent, do-not-remove) :: The offset exists because the Android in-app updater does a strictly-greater comparison on a run number parsed out of an asset filename, and a repo split reset the native counter below numbers already on devices. iOS has no in-app updater and no filename-embedded run number, so the +500 constant itself has nothing to attach to. But the underlying invariant — never emit a build number less than or equal to one already emitted for this app record — is *stronger* on iOS, because App Store Connect enforces it and rejects violations permanently. :: REC: Option 1 plus option 3 as an escape hatch: a `BUILD_NUMBER_OFFSET` repository variable defaulting to 0, added to the clock-derived value. That preserves the Android decision's real content (a one-way ratchet with a documented, never-decreasing adjustment) without inheriting a magic constant that no longer means anything. Record it with the same do-not-remove framing: lowering it strands the user's ability to upload, exactly as lowering +500 stranded dev users.
- dev-latest pre-release channel :: The Android dev channel works by publishing a debug-signed APK that the app downloads and installs. iOS has no sideload path, so 'the dev channel' cannot be an upstream-published binary that users install. What survives is the *intent*: a way to run develop-branch code with a distinct identity and a distinct update cadence. :: REC: Option 1 as the baseline, with option 3 for the maintainer workflow. Option 2 doubles every user's provisioning burden (a second App ID, second profile, second ASC record, second TestFlight) and should only be taken if side-by-side install is an actual product requirement. Preserve the fail-closed property from dev-pre-release.yml: if credentials are missing the build aborts before archiving, so an improperly signed build is never uploaded. The path filter that gates the dev build (App/, Sources/, WatchApp/, Widgets/, Package.*, Config/, fastlane/, the workflow itself) ports directly.
- dev-latest publish (delete-and-recreate) :: `gh release delete dev-latest --cleanup-tag || true` immediately followed by `gh release create` exists to minimize the window in which the in-app dev updater 404s. With no in-app updater, no client is polling that tag, so the tight-coupling requirement evaporates. What remains is a rolling pointer to the newest develop build for humans and for Simulator artifact download. :: REC: Option 1 for discoverability, keeping the same one-step delete-then-create so the window stays small and the tag always points at the newest develop head. Drop the 404-mapping requirement on the client side. The 'failed create after successful delete leaves the channel empty' edge case remains but is no longer user-visible as an error state.
- Gradle dependency locking (lockAllConfigurations, per-module gradle.lockfile, resolveAndLockAll) :: SwiftPM has a single `Package.resolved` per workspace, not per-module lockfiles, and it pins both an exact version and a git revision SHA — which is a stronger pin than Gradle's version-only lock. The Android carve-outs (`_internal-unified-test-platform*`, `*DependenciesMetadata`) exist for AGP-specific configuration families that have no SwiftPM analogue, and the custom `resolveAndLockAll` task has no equivalent. :: REC: Option 1, and treat the enforcement flag as mandatory. This is the sharpest trap in the port: SwiftPM's default behavior when `Package.resolved` is stale is to quietly re-resolve and succeed, which silently defeats pinning and would let a dependency-scan gate pass against versions that were never scanned. Also commit `Gemfile.lock` for the fastlane toolchain and run everything through `bundle exec` — that is the second real lockfile in an iOS repo and it is routinely forgotten.
- Renovate tiers and the never-auto-merge set (crypto vendor globs) :: The Android never-auto-merge set is expressed as vendor globs over Maven coordinates: `org.bouncycastle:*`, `net.zetetic:*` (SQLCipher), `androidx.security:*`, `org.openminimed:*` (Medtronic Sake crypto). On iOS most of that graph stops being a dependency at all — CryptoKit and Security.framework replace BouncyCastle and androidx.security, Keychain plus Data Protection classes replace EncryptedSharedPreferences, and there is no published `org.openminimed` artifact, so Sake crypto becomes first-party Swift source. The protection therefore has to move from package rules to source review. :: REC: Option 3. Keep the exact tier shape — Tier B as the only rule adding `automerge`, expressed all-negative, restricted to patch/minor; Tier D rules placed after it so `labels` last-wins and none adds automerge; groups scoped to patch/minor with manual coordinates excluded by name. Then add CODEOWNERS entries and a required-review rule on /Sources/Crypto/, /Sources/Persistence/, and the driver trees, because the crypto that Renovate used to guard is now code the project writes. Preserve the deliberate safe-fail: if Renovate's swift manager does not report the depType the Tier B rule matches on, Tier B matches nothing and everything falls to manual review — verify this empirically before going live, since the swift manager's depTypes differ from gradle's.
- License policy gate on redistributed dependencies (cash/licensee) :: There is no maintained SwiftPM equivalent of the licensee Gradle plugin. The policy content still applies verbatim and matters more, not less: the app is GPL-3.0-only, the whole fork-and-build distribution model exists to keep that workable, and EPL-1.0 must stay deliberately absent from the allowlist because the FSF treats it as GPLv3-incompatible for a combined distributed work. :: REC: Option 1 for enumeration plus a project-owned allowlist JSON and gate script, falling back to option 2's ScanCode where a package's license is not machine-readable. Carry the allowlist over unchanged (Apache-2.0, MIT, BSD-2-Clause, BSD-3-Clause, ICU, SAX-PD, SAX-PD-2.0, plus URL-matched GPL-3.0 for the project's own text), keep EPL-1.0 out, and keep the per-version `allowDependency`-with-a-`because`-reason pattern so a version bump re-raises the judgement. Option 4 is not acceptable — this is a legal-compliance gate, not a hygiene gate.
- Dependency CVE scanning (osv-scanner over Gradle lockfiles) :: osv-scanner's Gradle lockfile parser and OSV.dev's Maven advisory coverage have no equal on the Swift side. OSV.dev does carry a Swift package ecosystem, but advisory volume for SwiftPM is a small fraction of Maven's, and `Package.resolved` parsing is not on par with the Gradle lockfile path. A one-for-one tool swap would produce a gate that is green because it sees nothing. :: REC: Option 3. Keep the Android gate's two best properties: fail closed when the scanner itself errors, and `--no-ignore` so `--recursive` cannot skip a lockfile behind .gitignore. Keep osv-scanner.toml's discipline — no active suppressions, and any suppression requires a written reason plus an `ignoreUntil` forcing re-evaluation. Explicitly record in the PRD that Swift SCA coverage is weaker than the Android baseline, and compensate by minimizing third-party packages (prefer Apple frameworks) and retaining the 7-day `minimumReleaseAge` soak as the primary supply-chain defense, exactly as documented on Android.
- Security Scan Gate (Semgrep p/kotlin + p/java + p/secrets over Kotlin/Java sources) :: Named explicitly because it is the most likely thing to be mis-ported. Semgrep's `p/kotlin` and `p/java` rulesets produce exactly zero findings on Swift — a naive port yields a gate that is permanently, meaninglessly green over the entire application. Semgrep does support Swift as a language, but the registry's Swift rule coverage is thin next to the Kotlin and Java packs. Only `p/secrets` is language-agnostic and ports directly. :: REC: CodeQL Swift `security-extended` as the blocking gate (option 1) — it is the only true dataflow SAST for Swift and is free for public repositories — plus project-authored Semgrep Swift rules (option 2) for the app-specific bans, plus gitleaks over the full history (option 4) alongside the existing org-wide GitGuardian. SwiftLint `--strict` replaces `lintDebug` in the build gate, not the security gate. Keep the Android gate policy verbatim: block on HIGH/ERROR only, surface WARNING non-blocking, surface unparseable files as a warning so silently-skipped findings leave a signal, and fail closed when the scanner exits with an internal error.
- Build gate: unit tests, lint, and instrumented/emulator tests (android.yml) :: The shape survives but the runner class and the coverage boundary change materially. Everything moves to macOS runners at roughly a 10x minute multiplier for private repos, the KVM/AVD machinery disappears, and — decisively — Core Bluetooth does not exist in the iOS Simulator, so no Simulator-run test can exercise any BLE code path. The Android instrumented lane could at least boot a device image. :: REC: Options 1 and 2 together. Option 2 materially cuts macOS minutes and gives the lead a fast local loop; it also matches the compile-time-linked driver architecture, where a driver is a Swift module whose parsing layer has no Core Bluetooth dependency. Option 3 is not viable given the hardware distribution (lead has no iPhone; the hardware maintainer's devices are not CI infrastructure). The aggregate 'iOS Gate' required check keeps the same single-status design as 'Android Gate'. State plainly in the gate's own output that it does not cover BLE.
- Build-time toolchain and flags (Gradle wrapper, AGP, buildTypes, BuildConfig fields) :: Every mechanism changes even though every capability survives. Gradle wrapper + gradle-wrapper.properties + wrapper checksum verification becomes Xcode version selection, for which there is no checksum-verifiable equivalent — you trust the runner image. buildTypes become xcconfig-driven build configurations. BuildConfig fields become Info.plist keys via `$(VAR)` substitution or `SWIFT_ACTIVE_COMPILATION_CONDITIONS` flags. :: REC: Option 1. Pin the Xcode version in-repo and assert at build start that the selected version matches, because an unpinned Xcode bump can silently change signing, entitlement defaults, and SwiftPM resolution. Map the flags directly: UPDATE_CHANNEL -> an Info.plist channel string; DEV_BUILD_NUMBER -> CFBundleVersion (no separate field needed); MEDTRONIC_DRIVER_ENABLED -> a compilation condition that removes the driver module from the binary and the UI, which is stronger than Android's runtime kill switch and matches the compile-time-linked driver decision; watch-face asset SHA-256 fields -> not applicable. Note the genuine loss: nothing in the iOS toolchain corresponds to Gradle wrapper checksum validation.
- Local developer tooling (scripts/mobile-dev.sh) :: Every command maps to a different binary and one command has no mapping at all. adb/emulator become `xcrun simctl` and `xcrun devicectl`; logcat tag filters become `log stream --predicate 'subsystem == "…"'`; APK install becomes `simctl install` of a .app or TestFlight on device. The `ble-raw` lane, which greps BLE_RAW from logcat, cannot exist in the Simulator because Core Bluetooth is absent there. :: REC: Option 1, keeping the same ergonomics and `set -euo pipefail` discipline, plus a paired-simulator helper (`simctl pair` an iPhone and an Apple Watch simulator, boot both, install both apps) since the lead validates the watch app exclusively in the Simulator. Replace the `ble-raw` lane with a `sim-driver` lane that drives the in-process simulated pump driver and streams its log subsystem, and label it in the script's own help text as not-real-BLE. Replace shell.nix with a Brewfile or mise.toml plus `.xcode-version` — nixpkgs cannot provision Xcode.
- Per-user identifier and entitlement provisioning (new; no Android analogue) :: Android needs no per-user registration: an applicationId is a string and a keystore is a local file. On iOS, App IDs are globally unique across every Apple Developer account, so two independent forkers cannot both own the same bundle identifier, and App Groups, keychain access groups, and every entitlement must be registered with Apple before a profile can be issued. Nothing in the Android repo prepares for this and it is the single largest new setup burden. :: REC: Option 1, mirroring the Loop Browser Build model, with option 2 as fallback documentation. Option 3 is impossible — Apple does not permit it. Template every identifier on `$(DEVELOPMENT_TEAM)`. The lane must enable exactly the capabilities the app needs (App Groups, HealthKit if used, Background Modes for bluetooth-central, Time Sensitive Notifications) and must report clearly which capabilities Apple cannot auto-grant — notably `com.apple.developer.usernotifications.critical-alerts`, which requires a per-team application to Apple and is rarely approved for individuals. The build must never hard-depend on an entitlement a user cannot obtain.
- Build freshness and distribution lifecycle (new; replaces the in-app update channel) :: TestFlight builds expire 90 days after upload and stop launching. On Android the in-app updater kept users current; on iOS the user's own CI is the only thing that can. This is the honest functional replacement for the entire AppUpdateChecker/dev-latest mechanism and it has no Android counterpart, so it will be missed if it is not written down as a requirement. :: REC: Option 1 plus option 3's in-app surfacing. Ship the scheduled rebuild workflow in the template so it exists in every fork by default, and have the app display its own build date, expiry date, and days remaining, with a local notification as expiry approaches. Additionally keep a passive upstream-version notice: the app knows the tag/SHA it was built from and can query the upstream GitHub Releases API to say 'a newer release exists — re-run your build workflow', deep-linking to TestFlight via `itms-beta://`. That notice must never download or execute code.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (12)
- Release signing certificate assertion (release.yml): both app and wear-device release buildTypes SILENTLY fall back to the debug signing config when RELEASE_KEYSTORE_FILE is unset. The only thing preventing a debug-signed 'release' APK reaching patients is the post-build apksigner check against RELEASE_SIGNER_SHA256=55f0d0cdabf20b398ad30a0ce3e998e3192666e5c15e6d378b86e1c8de342990. A debug-signed release cannot be installed as an upgrade over a properly signed install, and worse, a debug-key APK is trivially forgeable by a third party — anyone can build and sign an impostor update. Any Swift/iOS port must keep an equivalent hard fail rather than a warning.
- versionCode derivation `major*1_000_000 + minor*10_000 + patch` is used identically in all three Gradle modules AND in AppUpdateChecker.parseVersionCode. It collides silently when patch > 9999 or minor > 99. A collision makes `remoteVersionCode > currentVersionCode` false and the app reports 'up to date' forever — patients would never receive a safety fix.
- DEV_RUN_NUMBER_OFFSET=500 is load-bearing and permanent. The updater uses a strictly-greater comparison on the run number parsed out of the asset filename; removing or lowering the offset publishes numbers below what devices already hold and permanently strands dev-channel users on a stale build (no further updates, including safety fixes).
- Phone APK asset selection is anchored (`^GlycemicGPT-(\d+\.\d+\.\d+)-release\.apk$` / `-dev\.\d+-debug\.apk$`) and uses singleOrNull. Wear and WatchFace APKs share the phone's applicationId and signing key; if the selector matched one, Android would install it IN PLACE OVER the phone app, replacing the CGM/pump app with a watch binary. The stable channel additionally requires the embedded version to equal the release tag, so a stale asset cannot be installed under a version the user was told about.
- APK download path is a code-execution path: https-only enforced in code (not just network_security_config, which permits cleartext for the LAN opt-in), host allowlist {github.com, objects.githubusercontent.com} incl. subdomains, filename sanitization, and an exact expected-size check that deletes a mismatched file. The wear checker caps downloads at 100 MB (100*1024*1024).
- Renovate never-auto-merge set covers crypto/at-rest by VENDOR GLOB (org.bouncycastle:*, net.zetetic:* [SQLCipher], androidx.security:*, org.openminimed:* [Medtronic Sake crypto]) so a future sibling artifact cannot slip past. Doc reason: a silent behavior change here can weaken encryption of patient data without failing a build. Nothing in CI inspects dependency bytecode — the 7-day minimumReleaseAge soak is the primary supply-chain defense.
- CODEOWNERS routes /plugins/pump-driver-api/, /plugins/shipped/tandem/, /plugins/shipped/medtronic/ to the project lead as 'safety-critical BLE parsing and SafetyLimits enforcement'. Any dependency or toolchain change reaching those trees must not auto-merge.
- The label-relay's documented residual risk: auto-merge eligibility is decided from the PR's CURRENT label set, so anyone with GitHub Triage or higher could strip needs-review/security-sensitive from a crypto bump and add `automerge`. Accepted and contained only operationally (restrict Triage to trusted maintainers). A provenance-based tier derivation is explicitly deferred.
- WatchFace release APKs are debug-signed (watchface release buildType unconditionally uses signingConfigs.getByName("debug")) and are deliberately excluded from the cert assertion. They are published as GitHub release assets. The stated mitigation is that users consume the committed in-app WFF assets, whose SHA-256 is compiled into BuildConfig and checked at runtime by WatchFacePusher.
- Debug/`dev-latest` builds are signed with a real shared debug keystore from 1Password and are publicly downloadable. The fail-closed design (composite action aborts before any build if OP_SERVICE_ACCOUNT_TOKEN is unset) is what prevents an unsigned or locally-keyed dev APK from being published.
- The CI Sentry-DSN guard (GradleException when SENTRY_DSN is set and CI=true) prevents an extractable telemetry credential from shipping in the downloadable debug APK of a medical app.
- Licensee EPL-1.0 exclusion is a legal-compliance safety gate: this app is GPL-3.0-only and a new EPL dependency fails the build rather than shipping in a GPL binary.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (45)
  Promotion pipeline topology (develop -> main) | release.yml job graph and gating | release-please configuration and version scheme | Release PR auto-merge | Deployable-change detection for the fallback release | Fallback patch release | Release APK build and asset naming | Release signing-certificate assertion | Gradle signingConfigs and the debug-fallback | op-load-signing-secrets composite action | 1Password secret model | Signing plumbing smoke tests | GitHub App identity model | Changelog cutoff-marker windowing | Merged-PR collection via GraphQL | Changelog scope/sub-category taxonomy | Changelog branch, commit, and PR | Changelog PR validation and admin-merge | Sync-back trigger and need-detection | Sync-back cherry-pick and conflict allowlist | Sync PR creation and admin-merge validation | dev-latest pre-release channel | DEV_RUN_NUMBER_OFFSET = 500 (permanent, do-not-remove) | dev-latest publish (delete-and-recreate) | In-app phone update channel client | APK download hardening | Wear APK update client | Gradle dependency locking | Lockfile regeneration for Renovate PRs | Privileged lockfile push job | Renovate global policy | Renovate tiers and never-auto-merge set | Renovate label-relay and its missing workflow | Renovate File Scope required check | Renovate config validator | Auto-labeling (feeds changelog + Renovate policy) | commit-msg git hook | DCO enforcement | CODEOWNERS routing for release automation | License policy gate on redistributed dependencies | Dependency CVE scanning | Workflow hygiene gates guarding the pipeline | Local developer tooling (scripts/mobile-dev.sh) | Build-time toolchain and flags relevant to release artifacts | Sentry DSN exclusion from published artifacts


================================================================================
## API contract pin and safety invariants  [contract-and-invariants]
================================================================================

### iOS REQUIREMENTS (24)
- The repository must vendor a byte-for-byte copy of the backend's OpenAPI contract at Contract/openapi.json alongside a Contract/CONTRACT_VERSION file, and a maintainer must be able to refresh both in a single PR by copying the whole spec and setting the version from info.x-contract-version — never by hand-editing the spec.
- A maintainer must be able to run the full contract guard suite locally on a Mac in seconds without an iOS Simulator, an App Store Connect account, or a network connection.
- CI must fail any PR in which the vendored CONTRACT_VERSION does not equal info.x-contract-version inside the pinned spec, with a message telling the developer to refresh the pin and its version together.
- CI must fail any PR in which an endpoint or HTTP method the app calls is absent from the pinned spec, naming Contract/openapi.json in the failure, and the app's networking layer must expose its complete endpoint list to the test rather than hiding it behind hand-written URL construction.
- CI must fail any PR in which the pinned spec stops declaring a field the app consumes on a safety-critical response: safety-limits min/max glucose, alert-thresholds urgent_low/low_warning/high_warning/urgent_high, login access_token/refresh_token/expires_in/user, and the Nightscout data array items pump_events[units, event_type] and glucose_readings[value].
- The project must maintain a written standing rule, enforced in review, that any newly consumed optional field whose absence would silently degrade a safety surface is added to the pinned-spec field-presence check rather than left to round-trip tests.
- The app must decode responses through exactly one shared decoder configuration, and the contract tests must exercise that same object, so the test cannot silently diverge from what ships.
- The app must tolerate a newer backend adding unknown fields, including unknown nested objects, and must tolerate an older backend omitting any field the app treats as optional or defaulted — including fields with non-nil defaults, which require explicit handling in Swift.
- The app must fail loudly and refuse to use a response when a field it treats as required is renamed, removed, or sent with the wrong scalar type, and must never coerce a JSON string into a numeric field.
- The app must never model a backend-supplied enumerated value as a strict enum; any unrecognised member must decode to a well-defined unknown case rather than failing the whole response.
- The app must read a backend contract-version signal where one is offered, compare it numerically against the version the build was validated against, and treat a missing or unparseable signal as an older/unknown backend — never as compatible.
- On first contact with a backend that is older or of unknown version, the app must continue to work on all read and monitoring paths and show a single dismissible warning that some features may not work; it must never refuse to run, and it must never show that warning at all when no backend is configured (local mode).
- The app must show the user which upstream commit it was built from, which contract version it was validated against, and which backend it is paired with, on a screen reachable without a backend connection.
- There must be exactly one definition in the codebase of the 20-500 mg/dL glucose validity bound and exactly one definition of the 18.0156 mmol conversion factor, shared unchanged by the iPhone app, the Apple Watch app, every widget and complication extension, and every compile-time-linked pump driver.
- CI must fail any PR that introduces a hardcoded glucose bound or conversion factor outside those canonical definitions.
- Glucose must be stored and compared in mg/dL on every surface; mmol/L must exist only as display and input; converting must divide once from the most precise mg/dL source and round last so that 70 shows as 3.9, 100 as 5.6, 120 as 6.7 and 180 as 10.0 on both phone and watch.
- Every numeric glucose string the app produces must use a dot decimal separator and half-up rounding regardless of the device's locale, and must be byte-identical between phone and watch for the same input.
- Constructing a domain reading with an out-of-range glucose value must fail in a way the caller can handle; no code path that consumes Bluetooth, backend, or persisted data may terminate the process on invalid input.
- The app must reject a backend settings response atomically when any threshold or safety limit is out of range or mis-ordered, retain the last known-good values, and show the user when the values in force were last successfully synced.
- A user must be able to enter thresholds in either unit using their own keyboard's decimal separator, and any out-of-range error message must quote bounds that would themselves be accepted if re-entered.
- A maintainer must be able to validate every part of this subsystem in the iOS and watchOS Simulators, with no physical iPhone, Apple Watch, or pump — which requires a simulated data source that links against the same shared safety constants as every real driver.
- Every shipped source file must carry the project's SPDX header, every file containing ported third-party code must carry that upstream's identifier and copyright instead, and CI must fail a PR that violates either rule — including the EC-JPAKE implementation, which must carry Apache-2.0 attribution in the file itself and not only in a documentation page.
- CI must block any PR whose commits, code comments, or description carry AI-tool attribution, using the same severity model, bot allowlist, and auto-close behaviour as the Android repository, extended to Swift source files.
- The upstream repository must build, test, lint, and security-scan the app without any Apple signing material, and the fork-and-build release workflow must run every safety and contract guard as a hard prerequisite of producing an installable artifact.

### IMPOSSIBLE (3)
- Guaranteed audible urgent-low/urgent-high alerting at the server's exact thresholds, regardless of silent switch, Focus, or Do Not Disturb, for every user out of the box :: Bypassing the ringer switch and Focus requires a UNNotificationInterruptionLevel of .critical, which needs the com.apple.developer.usernotifications.critical-alerts entitlement. Apple grants that entitlement per Team ID, only on individual request through a justification form, and it cannot be self-enabled in Xcode. Under the fork-and-build model every user signs with their OWN Apple Developer team, so the entitlement would have to be requested and granted to each user individually — Apple will not grant it at scale to hobbyist individual accounts. This directly limits the safety invariant that the app must alarm at exactly the server's alert-threshold levels: the thresholds will be correct, but the alarm's audibility is not guaranteed. :: ALT: Ship .timeSensitive as the default interruption level — its entitlement (com.apple.developer.usernotifications.time-sensitive) is self-serve in Xcode, it breaks through Focus when the user permits Time Sensitive notifications, and it plays sound — while respecting the silent switch. Detect the critical-alerts entitlement at build time and upgrade urgent-low/urgent-high to .critical when it is present, so users who do obtain it get the stronger behaviour with no code change. Document in the fork-and-build guide how to request the entitlement, how to grant the app Time Sensitive permission, and that silent mode will suppress audio otherwise. Where the app is foregrounded or holds an active audio session, play an in-app alarm that does not depend on the notification path. Disclose the limitation in MEDICAL-DISCLAIMER.md.
- Upstream-enforced safety gates on the binary each user actually installs :: On Android, upstream CI builds the APK that users side-load, so the Android Gate, Security Scan Gate, Dependency Scan Gate and the two contract guards all ran against the exact artifact. Under the iOS fork-and-build model the installed binary is produced by the USER's own GitHub Actions run, from THEIR fork, at THEIR chosen commit, signed with THEIR certificate, and uploaded to THEIR App Store Connect. Upstream has no signing identity, no visibility into the fork's workflow, and no ability to require a status check there — a fork owner can disable or edit any gate. Additionally, upstream CI cannot produce a signed device build at all, so artifact-level scanning (the iOS analogue of the MobSF/APK class Android evaluated) is unreachable upstream even in principle. :: ALT: Make the gates unbypassable-by-default inside the build workflow the user forks: the release job runs the contract guards, the safety-constant drift guard, SwiftLint and the App Config Gate as hard prerequisites of the archive step, so a red guard means no artifact rather than a warning. Have the workflow build from a pinned upstream tag by default rather than the fork's HEAD. Stamp the upstream commit SHA, the CONTRACT_VERSION the build was validated against, and the guard results into the build's Info.plist, and surface all three on an in-app About/Diagnostics screen so a user (and a maintainer helping them) can verify exactly what is running. Substitute source-level Info.plist/entitlements linting upstream for the artifact scan that cannot run there.
- A Swift SAST ruleset with the breadth of Semgrep's p/kotlin + p/java packs :: No such thing exists from any vendor as of 2026. Semgrep supports Swift as a language and publishes a p/swift pack, but its rule count is a small fraction of p/kotlin's, and the Java pack contributes nothing to a Swift codebase. This is a genuine tooling gap, not a configuration choice, and it means 'the same security testing as the Android repo' cannot be satisfied by keeping the same tool. :: ALT: Compose equivalent coverage in substance from three tools rather than one: CodeQL with the `swift` language pack as the primary taint/dataflow analysis (deepest Swift analysis available, free on public repositories); Semgrep p/secrets retained unchanged, since it is regex-based and language-agnostic; and SwiftLint custom rules encoding project-specific prohibitions that no generic pack will ever have — unpinned NumberFormatter or .formatted() on a glucose value, bare 20/500/18.0156 literals outside the canonical constants file, raw-value enums decoded from backend fields, precondition on externally-sourced data, and `privacy: .public` interpolation of pump identifiers in OSLog. Those custom rules are worth more here than any generic pack, because they encode this app's actual safety invariants. Record the residual gap and its mitigation in docs/dev/security-testing.md exactly as Android recorded the MobSF decision.

### NEEDS REDESIGN (24)
- ContractSmokeTest harness (MockWebServer + real Retrofit + real Moshi) :: None of the three pieces exist. There is no Retrofit, no Moshi, and MockWebServer has no first-class Swift equivalent. Critically, Android's guarantee that the test exercises the app's REAL deserialisation config is maintained by hand-copying NetworkModule.provideMoshi() into the test — a drift hazard the port should close by construction rather than reproduce. :: REC: URLProtocol stub. Expose ONE `APIDecoder.shared` (JSONDecoder with the app's exact date strategy and key policy) as the single object both the production client and the test consume, so Moshi-style config drift is impossible rather than merely discouraged. Model Instant as Date with a custom decoding strategy that accepts ISO-8601 both with and without fractional seconds (ISO8601DateFormatter needs .withFractionalSeconds explicitly, unlike Instant.parse) and throws DecodingError.dataCorrupted on failure. Use explicit CodingKeys per field rather than .convertFromSnakeCase, mirroring Android's explicit @Json(name:) posture.
- Additive-drift tolerance — "optional field absent is tolerated (older backend)" :: Half of this ports free and half breaks silently. Swift's synthesised Codable ignores unknown keys (matching Moshi) and decodes a missing key for an Optional-typed property to nil. But a NON-optional property with a Swift default value does NOT tolerate a missing key — the synthesised decoder throws .keyNotFound and ignores the default. Kotlin constructor defaults are exactly what makes absent-optional tolerated today, so a mechanical port turns fields like NightscoutConnectionDto.isActive = true and NightscoutPumpEventDto.isAutomated = false from tolerant into hard failures. :: REC: Property wrapper / macro for the common case, hand-written init(from:) for the safety-critical models. Whichever is chosen, add a test that enumerates every model field the Android side declares with a Kotlin default and asserts that omitting that key decodes successfully — otherwise this regression is invisible until an older backend is hit. Keep the existing two tolerance tests (unknown extra fields incl. unknown nested objects; glucose_unit_source absent → nil) as-is.
- Endpoint-presence coupling to the pinned spec :: Android derives the endpoint list by Java reflection over @GET/@POST/@PUT/@DELETE/@PATCH on the Retrofit interface. Swift has no runtime metadata for method attributes and Mirror cannot see them, so the list cannot be derived from the client at test time. The guard must instead couple to something the client is structurally forced to use. :: REC: CaseIterable endpoint table, plus a lint rule that no URLRequest is constructed outside the routing layer. Port normalizePath unchanged (`\{[^}]+\}` → `{}`) so client {deviceToken} still matches spec {device_token}, build specOps as [normalizedPath: Set<lowercased method>], and assert the missing list is empty with the same message naming Contract/openapi.json. All 34 operations must resolve, as they do today. Note the same two blind spots survive: path-parameter names are erased, and only presence — not shape — is checked.
- ADR 0001 Decision 2 — app-compatibility signal (designed, deliberately not implemented) :: The deferral rationale was "this work is guard-only and adds no runtime path." That rationale does not survive the iOS distribution model. Under fork-and-build, every user compiles from an arbitrary commit at an arbitrary time and points it at their own self-hosted backend of arbitrary age, so app/backend version skew becomes the normal case rather than an edge case. On Android the shipped fleet moves as one; on iOS it does not. :: REC: Implement the read side now. It is a few dozen lines, it is dead code until the backend emits the signal, and it means a self-hoster who upgrades the app ahead of the backend gets a real warning instead of scattered per-call failures. Keep ADR 0001's ordering rules verbatim: monotonically increasing integer starting at 1, numeric comparison, never semver, unparseable treated as absent and routed to the older/unknown branch — never silently as compatible.
- ADR 0002 — supported compatibility directions :: ADR 0002 establishes only two proven directions (older app → newer backend via backend legacy-value migration and loose pass-through fields; newer backend → older app via client unknown-field tolerance). The third — newer app → older backend — is explicitly NOT established and is handled defensively. A source-distributed iOS app built from HEAD by each user lands in that unproven third direction by default, on day one, for every user whose backend lags their last build. :: REC: All three, in that order of cheapness: keep per-call handling and on-device defaults as the floor; state the minimum backend version the build was validated against in the release notes and on the in-app About/diagnostics screen; implement the read-side compat signal. Preserve the explicit instruction not to treat the tolerant reader as a blanket cross-cadence guarantee — gate specific operations per ADR 0001 rather than assuming tolerance.
- ADR 0003 Decision 1 — smoke test chosen over a full DTO↔spec structural diff :: The decision may well land the same way, but its stated rationale no longer holds and must be re-taken. The alternative was rejected because it would require building a Kotlin-reflection + JSON-Schema resolver modelling @Json renames, defaulted/nullable semantics and Instant adapters. In Swift that infrastructure exists off the shelf: swift-openapi-generator consumes the pin at build time and supports a `filter:` restricted to specific operations, so structural drift on the covered surface becomes a compile error rather than a test assertion. :: REC: Keep the smoke test as the shipped posture (option 1), and record option 2 as evaluated-and-declined with a reason, so the ADR stays honest. The decisive argument for hand-written models is the nullable-consumed exception: generated types make every spec-required field required and every optional field optional, which removes the ability to deliberately declare a field non-optional so it fails loudly (auth tokens, glucose bounds) while another stays optional-but-field-presence-checked (pump_events.units). That hand-tuned asymmetry IS the safety design. Re-evaluate if the covered surface grows well past 34 operations.
- ADR 0003 Decision 2 — safety constants: guard now, consolidate later :: The follow-up should become the design. Android deferred consolidation because it edits safety validators and would enlarge a guard-only change's blast radius, and because :wear-device cannot depend on :app so the wear copy is structurally forced. Neither constraint exists on iOS: a plain Swift SPM target with no platform dependencies is importable by the iOS app, the watchOS app, widget and complication extensions, and every compile-time-linked pump driver module. There is nothing to force a second copy. :: REC: Consolidate up front (option 2). One `GlucoseSafety` enum (mgdlPerMmol, validRange 20...500, and the derived clamp sub-ranges) in a shared target; every validator, view model, store, chart clamp and persistence predicate references it. This is the single largest safety win available in this subsystem: it converts a 19-site count-equality guard into a real single source of truth, and it retires the acknowledged cost that adding a duplication site requires updating both the guard enumeration and safety-constants.md in the same PR.
- GlucoseFormat API surface (phone conversion choke-point) :: The API shape ports, but two formatting behaviours silently differ. (1) Rounding mode: Java's String.format("%.0f") rounds HALF_UP, so formatMean(72.5) → "73"; C/Swift printf uses the IEEE round-half-to-even mode, so String(format:"%.0f", 72.5) → "72". The mg/dL mean string is explicitly documented as byte-identical to a legacy string, so this is a visible parity break. (2) Locale: String(format:) with no locale is non-localised and safe, but NumberFormatter and the modern .formatted() FormatStyle localise by default and would emit a comma separator, breaking cross-surface consistency and release-log scrubbing. :: REC: Option 2 for integers and option 3's locale pin for decimals: explicit half-up rounding then integer formatting for mg/dL means; String(format:"%.1f") (non-localised) for mmol. Add unit tests at exact tie values (72.5, 137.5) to lock the behaviour. Preserve the convertValue/convertSpread distinction exactly — a spread has no zero anchor, keeps a decimal in mg/dL where a value shows as an integer, and reusing convertValue for a spread remains a named bug class. Add a lint rule banning .formatted() and unpinned NumberFormatter on any glucose value.
- Wear GlucoseDisplayUtils (mirror + on-watch clamps + trend glyphs) :: The mirror exists only because :wear-device cannot depend on :app. On watchOS the app imports the same shared Swift package, so the duplicate — and the 18.0156 and 20..500 scan sites inside it — disappear. Separately, two rendering details need watchOS-specific treatment: the ARGB colours must remain distinguishable in the always-on-display dimmed state, and the Unicode trend glyphs (⇈ ↑ ↗ → ↘ ↓ ⇊) are the wrong primitive for a complication or Smart Stack widget. :: REC: Option 2. Keep the clamp arithmetic byte-identical — low clamped to 40...200, high to max(low+1,100)...400, urgentLow to 20...low, urgentHigh to high...500, isValidGlucose 20...500 — and keep the KDoc rule that formatted output is never fed back into validation, colouring, clamping or plotting geometry, which stay mg/dL. Map trends to SF Symbols for complications and Smart Stack while keeping the glyph strings for accessibility labels and speech. Verify red 0xEF4444 / yellow 0xEAB308 / green 0x22C55E remain distinguishable at AOD luminance and to a deuteranope; pair colour with shape or text, never colour alone.
- Safety constant #2 — the 20..500 mg/dL glucose-validity bound :: Nineteen enumerated Android sites with no single owner: importable public constants, private constants, inline range literals in require blocks, a SQL `BETWEEN 20 AND 500`, off-by-one forms 20..499 and 21..500, and two wear coerceIn clamps. Reproducing that on iOS reproduces the hazard the map flags first: a desync in any one site silently accepts or drops readings. :: REC: Option 2, including the persistence predicate — GRDB lets the range be interpolated into the SQL from the constant, and SwiftData/Core Data #Predicate can capture it directly, so there is no reason for a literal to survive. Bound stays inclusive at both ends: 20 and 500 are valid. Any legitimate change still lands in both repos in the same coordinated release with the SoT doc updated in the same PR.
- Domain validators enforcing 20..500 (BgmReading, CgmReading, PluginEvent.CalibrationRequested, EnrichedBolusEvent) :: Kotlin `require` throws IllegalArgumentException, which is catchable and which the drift guard exercises at min, max, min−1 and max+1. Swift's idiomatic equivalent, `precondition`, TRAPS — it cannot be caught and cannot be asserted against by XCTest. Worse, a trap inside a Core Bluetooth delegate callback during a background state-restoration launch kills the process invisibly, with no user-facing signal, and repeated background crashes get the app deprioritised for future relaunch. That failure mode does not exist on Android, where a foreground service crash is visible. :: REC: Throwing init for the domain types (so the drift guard can use XCTAssertThrowsError at all four boundary points) plus a failable convenience initialiser used by every parser and BLE ingestion path, so an out-of-range reading is dropped and logged rather than fatal. Never precondition on any value that originates from BLE, the backend, or persisted data. Preserve the per-site details: CgmReading keeps MIN_MG_DL/MAX_MG_DL as named constants; EnrichedBolusEvent still allows a nil bgAtEvent and still bounds units, correctionUnits and mealUnits to 0...MAX_BOLUS_UNITS; CgmTrend keeps all eight members including UNKNOWN.
- Safety constant #3 — Tandem epoch offset 1199145600 and its conversion :: The constant ports trivially; the conversion does not. Android does LocalDateTime.ofEpochSecond(raw, 0, UTC).atZone(systemDefault()).toInstant() — a deliberate UTC decode followed by re-anchoring to the device zone, because the pump counts seconds since LOCAL midnight 2008-01-01 while the constant is UTC-based. Foundation has no LocalDateTime; Date is always an absolute instant. A naive Date(timeIntervalSince1970: raw) is wrong by the device's UTC offset, which mis-orders insulin history and IOB. :: REC: Option 1, written once in the Tandem driver with the arithmetic covered by tests at DST boundaries in both hemispheres. Two details to pin: use a fixed Calendar(identifier: .gregorian) with TimeZone(identifier: "UTC") for the decode; use TimeZone.current captured at parse time (not .autoupdatingCurrent) so a later timezone change does not retroactively reinterpret already-parsed events, matching Java's systemDefault() snapshot. Handle the DST spring-forward gap explicitly — Calendar.date(from:) for a non-existent wall time must be documented and tested, not left to Foundation's default. Keep the existing rule that all parsers in this object return nil on malformed cargo rather than throwing.
- Drift guard — three-layer mechanism :: Layer 1 (direct constant reads) ports and expands to cover nearly everything once constants are consolidated. Layer 2 (behavioural boundary checks) ports only if the validators throw rather than trap. Layer 3 (comment-stripped source scan of remaining duplication sites) is the layer that changes character: with one canonical constant there is almost nothing left to enumerate, and a count-equality enumeration would then be pure maintenance overhead. :: REC: Option 2. The inverse scan is strictly better than the enumeration: it is self-maintaining (a new duplication site fails without anyone remembering to update a list), it fails on the thing you actually fear (a new hardcoded bound) rather than on any edit, and it needs no per-site expected counts. Keep Layer 1's exact assertions — mgdlPerMmol == 18.0156 with zero tolerance, min/max == 20/500 on every importable constant — and Layer 2's four types × four boundary points. Keep the failure message that names both the guard and docs/contract/safety-constants.md.
- Drift guard SCAN_SITES — the 19-entry enumeration :: Directly downstream of consolidation. Fifteen of the 19 entries exist only because the bound is duplicated; two more exist because :wear-device cannot import :app; one is a SQL literal; one is the Tandem offset. On iOS the wear-mirror entries vanish, the SQL literal can reference the constant, and the private VALID_GLUCOSE_MIN/MAX and MIN/MAX_THRESHOLD constants become references. The enumeration collapses to roughly the Tandem offset alone. :: REC: Option 2, and document the collapse in the iOS safety-constants SoT doc so a reader is not confused by the count difference from Android. Also fix the stale path Android carries: safety-constants.md cites WatchGraphRenderer under presentation/ while the file lives under complications/ — do not reproduce that error. Preserve the ongoing obligation from ADR 0003: adding any new site or any new nullable consumed field on a safety surface requires updating the SoT doc and the guard in the same PR.
- Drift guard scan matching algorithm (countOccurrences + stripComments) :: Both halves have Swift-specific defects. stripComments is a naive regex that removes /* … */ then truncates each line at the first // — but Swift permits NESTED block comments (/* /* */ */), which the regex terminates early on, and a // or /* inside a string literal or string interpolation silently eats code. The token regex also assumes Kotlin literal syntax: Swift numeric literals conventionally use underscore grouping (1_199_145_600), which the un-underscored token 1199145600 will not match, and Swift has no trailing L suffix. :: REC: Option 3 if the scan survives consolidation at all, else option 2. swift-syntax is a first-party SPM package, so the comment-stripping hazard is fully removable rather than merely mitigated. Whatever the mechanism, preserve the boundary semantics that make the guard catch widening: a following digit, '.', '_', 'e' or 'E' must not match, so 500→5000 and 18.0156→18.01565 stop matching and fail. Normalise underscore grouping before comparison so 1_199_145_600 and 1199145600 are the same token.
- Documented exclusions from the drift scan :: Two of the three exclusion categories change meaning on iOS. The 'example plugins are sample code, not shipped' carve-out does not survive: the simulated/demo glucose driver is not optional on iOS — Core Bluetooth does not exist in the Simulator and the lead developer has no iPhone, so a simulated driver is the ONLY way he can validate anything, and it will be compile-time linked into the shipped binary like every other driver. It is therefore shipped code and cannot be excluded. The display-only exclusion still holds but moves (SwiftUI Charts Y-axis clamps rather than a Canvas renderer). :: REC: Option 2, and additionally build the simulated driver in Debug and TestFlight configurations only if the product wants it hidden from a user's own release build — but either way it references the shared constants, so the exclusion is unnecessary. Keep the comment-only exclusion (a proper SwiftSyntax scan makes it automatic). Keep the display-only exclusion for chart axis clamps, with the same explicit revisit trigger: if any display clamp ever feeds a validation or alerting path, it becomes a scan site.
- Backend-response validation sites (reject-and-ignore rules) :: Three sites apply the same bound to the same class of data with three different failure modes and two different ordering strictnesses: AuthRepository logs and ignores the whole response (strict < throughout), AlertThresholdStore throws (≤ … < … ≤ at the urgent boundaries), HomeViewModel clamps each value into 20...500 then rejects on ordering. The map itself flags this as safety-critical, and the consequence — rejecting a response leaves the previously stored thresholds firing the alert floor on stale values — is invisible to the user. :: REC: Option 2. Keep every numeric predicate byte-identical (20...500 membership; range ul<lo<hi<uh; safety limits min<max, min in 20...499, max in 21...500, basal 1...15000, bolus 1...25000; the 900_000 ms / 15-minute refresh interval) but pick ONE ordering rule and ONE failure mode, and make the stale-threshold condition visible: show 'alert thresholds last synced <time>' on the phone and mirror it to the watch. Do not adopt option 3 — refusing to alert is strictly more dangerous than alerting on slightly stale thresholds for a monitoring-only app.
- CI enforcement of the contract and drift guards (Android Gate → iOS Gate) :: Both guards are plain JVM unit tests run by ./gradlew testDebugUnitTest on ubuntu-latest. On iOS they need macOS runners and a different test invocation, and the build step changes fundamentally: upstream CI can build but cannot SIGN, because under fork-and-build the signing identity belongs to each user, not the project. :: REC: Option 2, published as a single required status named 'iOS Gate' so branch protection stays a one-status rule as today. Keep the dorny/paths-filter detect-changes fan-out unchanged so docs-only PRs skip the build. Replace ./gradlew lintDebug with SwiftLint plus SwiftFormat --lint. Add an 'App Config Gate' asserting Info.plist and entitlements posture (no blanket NSAllowsArbitraryLoads, UIBackgroundModes exactly the declared set, no get-task-allow in Release, every required usage-description string present and non-empty) — this is the honest source-level substitute for the artifact-level checks Android declined MobSF for. Note that the guards must still run from within the repo tree, since they read repo files by path.
- Security Scan Gate (Semgrep p/kotlin + p/java + p/secrets) :: Semgrep's p/kotlin and p/java rulesets do not apply to Swift and there is no Swift ruleset of comparable breadth from any vendor. Semgrep does support Swift as a language and a p/swift pack exists, but it is thin — a small fraction of p/kotlin's rule count. Satisfying 'the same security testing as the Android repo' in substance therefore requires a different primary tool, not a renamed one. :: REC: Option 2, published under the SAME required status name 'Security Scan Gate' so the branch ruleset does not change. CodeQL's Swift pack is the deepest Swift static analysis available and is free on public repositories, which this one is. Keep the gate policy identical — fail on HIGH/ERROR only, WARNING/INFO surfaced but non-blocking — and keep the same posture on secret scanning: no dedicated gate, because gitleaks runs via CodeRabbit and GitGuardian runs org-wide. Also carry over the medical-device framing in the workflow header, since it is what justifies the gate being required. State plainly in docs/dev/security-testing.md that Swift SAST rule coverage is thinner than Kotlin's and that the mitigation is a deliberately small dependency surface plus CodeRabbit review.
- Dependency Scan Gate (OSV-Scanner over committed gradle.lockfile files) :: Gradle dependency locking is what gives OSV-Scanner something to read; the six committed lockfiles are the scannable artifact. SwiftPM's equivalent is Package.resolved, but the ecosystem coverage behind it is materially thinner: Swift advisories in OSV/GitHub Advisory Database are sparse compared with Maven, and OSV-Scanner's Package.resolved support must be confirmed at implementation time rather than assumed. :: REC: Both, under the same required status name 'Dependency Scan Gate', with the OSV lane verified against a real Package.resolved before it is made required and Dependabot as the always-on backstop. Commit Package.resolved, pin every dependency with .exact(...) rather than a range (the SwiftPM analogue of Gradle dependency locking), and keep the same trigger paths so any file that can change the resolved graph re-runs the scan, plus the weekly Monday cron that catches newly disclosed CVEs. The strongest available mitigation for thin Swift advisory coverage is a deliberately tiny dependency surface — the Loop/xDrip4iOS projects ship with very few third-party packages and this one should too.
- SPDX in-scope globs and the exclusion-wins rule :: All 16 Android globs are Gradle/Kotlin-shaped and must be rewritten, and one Swift file has a hard constraint the policy's 'first thing in the file, above package' rule violates: Package.swift REQUIRES its `// swift-tools-version:x.y` comment to be the literal first line, so SPDX lines must go on lines 2-3, not line 1. The deliberate 'list Gradle files individually so a recursive glob cannot reach an excluded module' technique still applies and should be preserved for Package.swift files. :: REC: Option 1. In-scope: Sources/**/*.swift and Tests/**/*.swift per module, every Package.swift (lines 2-3), *.xcconfig, scripts/*.sh, fastlane/Fastfile and fastlane/*.rb, and .github/workflows/*.yml. Out of scope, consistent with Android's conservative posture: Contract/openapi.json, Info.plist, *.entitlements, *.xcodeproj/**, *.xcscheme, Assets.xcassets/**, Package.resolved, .swiftlint.yml, renovate/osv/zizmor configs, all Markdown, and anything under .build/ or DerivedData/. Keep the exclusion-wins rule verbatim: where an in-scope glob and an excluded tree both match, the exclusion wins.
- SPDX never-stamp trees and the copied-or-ported objective test :: The objective test — excluded if upstream code was COPIED OR PORTED into the file, in scope if upstream was only STUDIED and reimplemented — ports verbatim and is the right rule. What changes is the population it applies to. On iOS the likely ports are different and larger: a Medtronic driver would again be a port of OpenMinimed (excluded at directory level, same reasoning that drawing the line file-by-file inside a ported module invites misattribution), and any adoption of LoopKit/CGMBLEKit-derived code brings MIT obligations. There is no gradlew/gradle-wrapper equivalent to leave alone; Xcode-generated project files replace it as the untouched category. :: REC: Option 1 as the baseline with option 2 as a stretch. Preserve the two rules that matter most: citing an upstream project or naming an upstream file as a reference is NOT grounds for exclusion (the five Tandem files that cite jwoglom/pumpX2 as a protocol and test-vector reference stay in scope and keep their reference comments intact, and their Swift equivalents should too), and when the objective test and the list disagree, treat the file as excluded and fix the list. Keep docs/THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md authoritative on conflict.
- EcJpake — the open Apache-2.0 attribution gap :: The gap does not just port, it gets harder. Apple's CryptoKit does not implement EC-JPAKE, so the Tandem authenticator needs an implementation in Swift, and every route carries an Apache-2.0 obligation: hand-porting particle-iot/ecjpake-java reproduces the exact gap that is open today; wrapping mbedTLS (which has mbedtls_ecjpake) brings Apache-2.0 in as a dependency. Either way the notice must travel with the code, which is why the Android file's prose-only mention in a header plus a notice living only in THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md is insufficient. :: REC: Option 1 or 2, whichever the crypto review prefers, isolated in its OWN SPM target so the GPL-3.0-only sweep never touches it and the licence boundary is structural rather than a per-file exception. Apache-2.0 is one-way compatible with GPL-3.0, so including it in this GPL-3.0-only work is fine; the obligation is attribution, not relicensing. Do not carry the Android bug forward: the header must contain a real SPDX identifier and a real copyright line, not a prose sentence. Also fix it in the Android repo while the policy is fresh.
- SPDX sweep verification (six checks) — one-time sweep to continuous gate :: The Android sweep is described but has NOT been executed: only seven .kt files in the whole tree contain the string SPDX-License-Identifier and those are licence-screen tests, so no source file carries a header and EcJpake still has no identifier. A greenfield Swift repo has no such backlog, so the right move is to enforce from commit one rather than schedule a sweep that then rots. :: REC: Option 2, named 'License Header Gate'. Port the checks with the same output-not-exit-status discipline (grep -L/-l exits non-zero when nothing matches, which is the passing case): (1) no in-scope Swift file lacks GPL-3.0-only, with the EC-JPAKE target filtered out; (2) no listed non-Swift in-scope file lacks it; (3) no excluded/ported tree contains it; (4) the EC-JPAKE file DOES contain SPDX-License-Identifier: Apache-2.0; (5) LICENSE is unmodified by the PR; (6) the GitHub licence API reports exactly one licence, GPL-3.0. Add a seventh for Swift: every Package.swift has swift-tools-version on line 1 and SPDX on lines 2-3.

### SAFETY-CRITICAL (11)
- Glucose validity bound 20..500 mg/dL (inclusive) is duplicated across 19 enumerated Android code sites with NO single owner. A desync in any one silently accepts or drops readings. The drift guard is a count-equality source scan, not a real single-source-of-truth — a Swift port must consolidate to one constant.
- mmol↔mg/dL factor 18.0156 exists in two Android copies (GlucoseFormat, wear GlucoseDisplayUtils) and must equal the backend's core/units.py MGDL_PER_MMOL. Using 18.02 or 18.0182 is explicitly forbidden. Convert ONCE from the most precise mg/dL source and round LAST to one decimal (70→3.9, 180→10.0, 120→6.7, 100→5.6).
- Canonical storage/comparison unit is mg/dL everywhere; mmol/L is display and input only. Alert and SSE numerics stay mg/dL. Persisting a mmol value as mg/dL (3.9 instead of ~70) silently suppresses low alerts — CodeRabbit hard-fails this.
- Alert thresholds fetched from /api/settings/alert-thresholds are what the ON-DEVICE alert floor fires from; the app must alarm at exactly the server's levels. If validation rejects a response, the previously stored thresholds keep firing (stale-threshold risk).
- Nightscout pump_events[].units is nullable-consumed: a null DROPS the event, so a silent backend rename deletes all NS bolus/basal and UNDERSTATES IOB with no error. Only the pinned-spec field-presence check catches it.
- Tandem epoch offset 1199145600 s (2008-01-01 UTC) with the deliberate UTC-decode-then-systemDefault-reanchor conversion. Drift or a naive Instant.ofEpochSecond shifts every pump event timestamp and mis-orders insulin history/IOB.
- SafetyLimits absolute bounds: glucose 20..500, max basal 15_000 mU/hr, max bolus 25_000 mU. ABSOLUTE_* alias DEFAULT_*, so widening a default widens the absolute ceiling. safeOf() CLAMPS untrusted backend input rather than throwing (min→20..499, max→(min+1)..500).
- App is monitoring-only: no therapeutic or device-control commands, no bolus, no pump writes; the AI never issues device commands (project-wide invariant). Fail-open-with-warning for an unknown backend applies ONLY to read/monitoring paths; any future safety-critical write must be refused when compatibility is unknown.
- AlertThresholdStore.requireSafeThresholds THROWS on out-of-range or mis-ordered thresholds while AuthRepository silently ignores the response — two different failure modes for the same data. Ordering strictness also differs (strict < in AuthRepository's range check vs ≤/</≤ in AlertThresholdStore).
- Wear-side clamps are a second, independent bound expression: sanitizeThresholds low∈40..200, high∈max(low+1,100)..400, urgentLow∈20..low, urgentHigh∈high..500, plus isValidGlucose 20..500. Watch and phone must not disagree on the same mg/dL value.
- The contract drift gate is BUILD-TIME against the vendored pin only. It provides zero runtime protection against an arbitrary user-configured backend; the runtime compatibility signal that would cover that is designed but NOT implemented.

### CAPABILITY NAMES (43)
